## REVIEW

# O'Connor movement review

Read the complete supplied movement in chapter order before judging it. State
whether this is self-review, a fresh-context simulated cold read, or a real human
read. Do not claim independent testing when you have the drafting history.

First give an unscored reader response: where interest caught, where it slipped,
which person matters, which event you expect next, and whether you would continue.
Then use two perspectives: a fluent thirteen-year-old reader and an adult genre
reader. These are lenses, not demographic research. Do not infer a sales forecast.

For each applicable dimension give a 1–10 score, an exact passage location, a reason
and confidence. Mark an absent feature N/A instead of giving invented evidence:

| Dimension | What to judge |
|---|---|
| Opening pull | A concrete reason to care before explanation takes over |
| Keep reading | Unresolved pressure and satisfying local payoff |
| Interest / freshness | Specific people, choices and problems |
| Clarity / flow | Meaning on one pass and clean transitions |
| Character attachment | Agency, vulnerability, recognizable differences |
| Humor / warmth | Character-grounded pleasure, timing, emotional honesty |
| Action / suspense | Trackable contest, adaptation, cost, earned scene length |
| Progression payoff | Effort visibly changes capability and consequence |
| Connection | State, plants and relationships carried across chapters |
| Read-aloud quality | Punctuation, referents, dialogue attribution and cadence |

Anchor interpretation: 5 = understandable but inconsistent; 7 = engaging with
located weaknesses; 9 = compelling with few substantial distractions. These are
editorial judgments, not calibrated measurements. Do not average the two readers
into a release gate. Do not award points for matching another book's word counts.

Separately check canon, physical state, power limits, knowledge boundaries and
reserved disclosures. Cite the project evidence for a contradiction. If only the
plan supports a claim, identify it as plan adherence rather than a canon error.

Return a concise repair brief with at most three priorities. Each needs location,
observed issue, effect on the reader, proposed scope and a strength to preserve.
Retain the developed action the owner requested. Punctuation can repair an unclear
sentence; do not shorten a successful fight merely to reduce its word count.

If comparing runs, keep chapter coverage and scoring rubric equal. Hide model and
seat labels from a fresh reader where feasible. Report tradeoffs and uncertainty;
one movement does not prove which seat is better across a series.

## CHAPTER — manuscript/prologue.md

# Prologue — Somebody Should Write It Down

## 1. The Night of the Fall

*2080*

Ruth Achterberg was rinsing the last plate when the kitchen went white.

It was not a flash, exactly. A flash would have hurt. This was a slow, clean light that came in through the balcony door and lay across the floor tiles as if someone had opened a refrigerator the size of the sky. Then it faded, and the ordinary night came back. The tap was still running. Ruth turned it off.

"Mum," said Mina from the balcony. "Mum, come and look at this."

The apartment was on the ninth floor. From the balcony you could see the river, the bridges, and the long lit spine of the ring road going south. Joel was already out there with his hand on the rail. Saffi was beside him in her socks. Ben was asleep, or meant to be.

"Up there," said Joel. He pointed south and high.

There was a point of light in the sky, far above where any aircraft flew. It was too high for a star to look that bright. It was not getting bigger. It was spreading. As Ruth watched, it opened into a soft white blur, and then the edges of the blur began to turn colors. Red, first. Then a long, rippling green, like oil on water. It crawled across the south of the sky in folds.

There was no sound at all.

"Is that the northern lights?" said Saffi. "They said there might be a storm. On the news, they said."

"That's not the north," said Mina.

Then the lights went out.

They did not flicker. They did not dim. Every light in the city went out at once, from the bridges to the ring road to the window across the courtyard where the Dabrowskis were watching their enormous television. The hum went out of the building. Ruth had not known the building hummed until it stopped. The refrigerator behind her fell silent. The little green eye on the oven clock was gone.

Down in the street, a car rolled to a stop without its headlights. Another one coasted past it and bumped gently into the curb. Nobody honked. The horns had died with everything else.

"Joel," said Mina.

"I know."

"Joel, what is it?"

He was quiet for a moment. Ruth could see his face only because the sky was still glowing. He was an electrician. He had spent twenty years on the city's substations, and he did not look surprised. He looked like a man doing sums he did not like.

"That was a bomb," he said. "A nuclear bomb. High up. Way above the weather."

Saffi took a sharp breath.

"Not here," said Joel quickly. "It's not hitting us. There's no blast, see? No wind. Nothing's on fire. It went off up in the thin air on purpose. When it does that, it throws out a pulse. A wave. It's like lightning hitting every wire at once. Anything with a long wire in it, anything with a chip, it just cooks it."

"Everything?" said Mina.

He held up his phone. The screen was black. He pressed the button and nothing happened.

"Everything with a plug or a battery," he said. "Pretty much."

The colors in the south were fading now, folding down into a dull red smear. Ruth looked at the dark city and could not make it into a shape she knew. There were candles appearing in some windows already, tiny and yellow. A dog was barking somewhere. Down the hall, someone began to pound on the elevator doors.

"Who would do that?" said Saffi.

"Them," said Joel. "Who else? It's been coming for years."

"You don't know that," said Mina.

"Somebody launched it. It didn't launch itself."

Across the courtyard a window scraped open. Mr. Dabrowski leaned out with a flashlight that did not work. He shook it, cursed, and put it down.

"It's the sun!" he called across to them. "They said! A solar storm. It'll be back on by morning."

"It's not the sun, Piotr," said Joel.

"How do you know?"

Joel did not answer. Ruth thought that was because he did know, and because knowing did not help anyone.

She went back inside. In the dark kitchen she found the drawer by feel and took out the box of matches she kept for birthday cakes. She lit a candle. Then she looked at her wrist.

Her father's watch was still running. It was an old wind-up thing with a cracked strap. She had worn it every day for eleven years because she missed him, not because it was useful. The second hand was moving. She held it close to the candle.

Eleven forty-seven.

She found a spiral notebook in the drawer under the phone books. Saffi's old math homework was in the front of it. Ruth turned past the fractions to a clean page and wrote the date at the top. Under that she wrote the time.

Mina came in and stood behind her. "What are you doing?"

"Writing it down."

"Writing what down?"

"What happened," said Ruth. "Somebody should."

Joel came in from the balcony. He watched her write for a while. *White light in the south, very high. No noise. Then colors, red and green. Then everything stopped.*

"Who's going to read that?" he said. He was not being unkind. He sounded tired already, and it was only the first hour.

"Somebody who wasn't here," said Ruth.

She wrote down what Joel had said about the bomb and the pulse. Then she wrote down what Mr. Dabrowski had said about the sun. She wrote *Joel says it is an attack. Piotr says it is a storm. Saffi thought it was beautiful.* She did not write which of them she believed. She did not know which of them she believed.

Ben came out of the bedroom rubbing his eyes. "The fan stopped," he said.

"I know, love."

"Why did it stop?"

Ruth looked at the page. Then she put the pencil down and picked him up, even though he was eight and too heavy, and carried him to the window so that he could see the last of the red going out of the sky.

"We don't know yet," she said. "But I wrote down what we saw."

---

## 2. The Machine Book

*2085 — five years after the Fall*

The queue at the Saint Brigid annex started at the inner fence and went all the way down the hill to the old bus shelter.

Teo Barrasa could see it from the generator room window. He did not look at it often. Looking at it did not shorten it.

The annex had been built twelve years before the Fall to hold a magnetic imaging machine. The machine needed a room that kept every stray signal out, so the builders had lined the walls with copper mesh and set the whole suite on its own buried power plant. Nobody had been thinking about bombs. They had been thinking about clean pictures of people's insides. When the pulse came through on that first night, it went around the annex the way water goes around a stone.

So the annex still had lights. It had a sterilizer, a refrigerator for insulin, three working dialysis machines, and one generator that ran on fuel nobody could make anymore. Across sixty miles of dead country, that made it the only real hospital left.

The Restoration Board owned the fence. The Board owned the fuel. The Board decided who stood in which line.

Teo's book was a hardback ledger with a blue cloth spine. He had found it in a stationery store with its windows smashed. He kept it on the shelf over the dialysis station, and every time he ran a machine, he wrote in it. The date. The patient's name. The hours. The fuel the generator burned. And at the end of each line, in a narrow column he had ruled himself, he wrote why.

*Board card. Crew three.*
*Board card. Family of crew.*
*Board card.*

Most of the lines were like that.

Every few pages there was a different kind of line. *Turned away. No card. Reason given: fuel.* He wrote those too. Nobody had asked him to. Nobody had told him to stop, either, and he had been careful not to ask.

The second winter had been the worst. That was when the power station up the river had gone. Its reactor had sat there without outside power for eleven months, kept cool by backup generators until the last of their diesel ran out. After that nobody could keep it cool. Nobody had been there to try. The steam had gone up in the spring, and the wind had gone east, and the people who came down the river road that summer had their hair coming out in their hands. Teo had written them all down. The book had a long stretch of names that ended in the same word.

"Teo." It was Marguerite, the night nurse, leaning in the door. "There's a girl at the fence."

"There's always a girl at the fence."

"This one's swelling up. Her ankles are like bread. Her mother says she hasn't passed water in four days." Marguerite looked at the floor. "No card."

Teo looked at the board on the wall. Machine two was booked at six for a man from the Board's line crew. The line crews spent their days stringing new wire between dead towers. The Board said they were rebuilding the grid. Old Farrow, who had been an electrician before the Fall and came to the annex for his heart pills, said the same thing every visit. You could not rebuild a grid when every transformer in the country had burned out on the same night. You could not make transformers without factories. You could not run factories without a grid. "We're stringing wire to nothing," he said. "But it gives the young ones something to do."

It was four o'clock. Machine two was free for two hours.

"What's her name?" said Teo.

"Lina."

He went out to the fence himself. The girl was about ten. She was sitting on her mother's coat with her face puffy and grey. The mother looked at Teo the way people looked at the fence. She had stopped hoping it would open.

"I can give her one session," he said. "Now. Before the six o'clock."

"Will one help?"

"It'll help today."

The mother did not ask what would happen tomorrow. Teo was grateful for that.

He ran the machine for an hour and fifty minutes. The girl slept through most of it. When she woke up, her color was better, and she asked for water, and her mother cried in the corridor where the girl could not see her. At ten to six Teo unhooked the lines and cleaned the station. The man from crew three came in at six exactly and sat down without noticing anything.

Teo took down the blue ledger.

He wrote the date and *Lina, age about 10, no card.* He wrote *1 hr 50 min.* He wrote the fuel the generator had burned in that time, to the tenth of a liter, because the Board read the book and the fuel was the thing they would count.

Then he came to the narrow column.

He held the pen over it for a long time. He could write *emergency*. That was true, and it was the word the Board liked. He could leave it blank. Blank was safer than a lie.

He wrote: *She was here. The machine was free. Nobody else was waiting for it.*

Marguerite read it over his shoulder. "They'll have your card for that."

"Maybe."

"Then why put it in?"

Teo closed the book. Outside, the queue went down the hill in the dusk. A few people at the bottom had lit a fire in the bus shelter.

"Because in ten years somebody's going to open this," he said. "And they'll want to know who got the machine. And why. If I don't write the why, they'll make one up."

---

## 3. The Tear

*2096 — sixteen years after the Fall*

Pell Garrow was thirteen, and she was digging potatoes in the low field when the air tore open.

She heard it before she saw it. It was the sound of a bedsheet being ripped, except that it was too big and it came from the wrong place. It came from the middle of the field, about thirty paces away, where there was nothing at all.

She stood up with the fork in her hands.

There was a line in the air. It hung there, a little taller than her father, and it was the color of nothing. Not black. Black was a color. The line was like the place your eyes go when you rub them too hard. While she watched, the line widened into a long, ragged slit, and the air around it shivered like the air over a road in August.

"Nico," she said. She did not shout. She did not know why she did not shout. "Nico, come here."

Her brother was seven. He was on his knees at the end of the row with his hands in the dirt and his tongue between his teeth. He looked up at her and then past her. His mouth fell open.

Something came out of the tear.

It did not climb out. It poured, the way wet sand pours out of a bucket. It slid onto the ground in a grey heap the size of a calf. Then the heap pulled itself up into a shape. It had four thick legs, or something that worked like legs. It had a front end that was lower than its back end. It did not have a face. It had a place where a face should go, and the place was turned toward Nico.

Pell's father was at the top of the field with the cart. She heard him shout her name. She heard him start to run.

The grey thing went for Nico.

It was not fast. It moved like a man carrying something heavy, lurching and sure. But Nico was frozen on his knees, and Pell was nine rows away, and her father was a hundred paces off.

Pell dropped the fork and ran.

She did not decide to do it. Afterward she tried to remember deciding, and she could not. She only remembered her legs moving and the dirt flying up and her own breath very loud. She reached Nico two steps before the thing did. She got in front of him. She threw her arms up in front of her face, because that was what you did when something was going to hit you.

The thing hit nothing.

There was a sound like a hand slapping a wet window. The grey thing stopped in the air an arm's length from her. It did not bounce. It just stopped, the way a bird stops against glass, and slid down onto the dirt.

Pell's hands were hot. Her palms burned as if she had grabbed a pot handle. Between her hands and the grey thing, the air had gone thick and bright. She could not see anything there. She could feel it. It was like pushing against the bottom of a full bucket of water.

The thing pulled itself up and hit the nothing again. Pell felt it in her shoulders, in her teeth, in the bottoms of her feet. She dug her heels into the soft dirt and did not move.

"Pell!" Her father was there. He had the long-handled shovel from the cart. He did not understand what he was seeing, and he did not stop to understand it. He swung the shovel flat into the side of the grey thing as hard as he could.

It came apart.

That was the only way to say it. It did not bleed. It did not scream. The shovel went into it, and the shape it had been wearing simply let go. It fell into a heap of fine grey powder, like the ash at the bottom of the stove, and lay there on the dirt, and did not move again.

Behind it, the tear in the air was closing. It shrank to a line. The line shrank to nothing. The ripping sound stopped as if somebody had shut a door on it.

Nico started to cry.

Pell dropped her arms. The bright thick feeling went out of the air at once. Her palms were red. Her legs gave out, and she sat down hard in the potato row beside her brother.

Her father stood over the grey heap with the shovel. He was breathing like a man who had run up a hill. He looked at the powder. He looked at the empty air where the tear had been. Then he looked at Pell, and at her hands, and she saw that he was afraid of something, and she did not know if it was the grey thing or her.

"What did you do?" he said.

"I don't know."

"You stopped it."

"I don't know how."

That night the grey powder was still warm. Her father had shoveled it into a feed sack because he did not know what else to do with it. Pell lay awake with the sack beside her bed, because her mother had said to keep it away from the animals, and there was nowhere else to put it. Some time after midnight, she put her hand on the sack. The warmth went into her palm and up her arm. It was like drinking something hot on a cold day. The burned feeling in her hands went away. She slept until noon.

---

*From the record kept by Aud Fenn, the miller's wife, at Garrow's Ford:*

*Fourth day of the potato harvest, the sixteenth year.*

*A tear opened in Henrik Garrow's low field in the afternoon, at about three by the mill clock. Something came out of it. Henrik says it was grey and had no face. His girl Pell, thirteen, stopped it with her hands. I write that down as she told it and as Henrik told it, because I do not have a better way to say it. She did not touch it. It stopped anyway. Henrik hit it with the shovel and it turned to dust.*

*The dust stays warm. Pell slept better beside it and woke less tired. I do not know what to make of that. I am writing it down.*

*Old Sefton with the hardened radio at the crossroads says it is happening in other places. A tear by the coast road. Two tears up north. A boy over in Ashby who stood in the river in flood since the summer and could not be moved off his feet, and a girl down the valley who can put her hand on a sick cow and tell you where it hurts. All of them about thirteen. Sefton says before the Fall some survey brought a thing back from Mars, a sort of disk, and it has woken up in a vault in the south this spring, and that is what is tearing holes in the air. Sefton also says nobody ever went to the moon, so.*

*The minister says it is a judgment. Henrik says it is a wolf of some new kind. Pell says she does not know what it is, and she does not know what she did, and she would like everyone to stop asking her.*

*I have written down all three. Somebody will know better later.*

---

## 4. The Copy

*2152 — seventy-two years after the Fall*

The pages came up the river in an oilcloth packet, wrapped twice and tied with tarred string, and the man who carried them said they were not to be lost.

"They're a copy," he said, as he handed them over. "Of a copy. The woman who made the first one said the second one had to keep going."

"Going where?" said Oriel Hask.

"Wherever it's needed. She thought it might be needed here."

Oriel was nineteen and the junior clerk at the Lowmarket infirmary. Lowmarket was ten days down the river from Meridian by barge. Meridian was the big station in the tech country, with its walls and its hardened lamps and its medicine. Lowmarket was a market town with a mud wharf, two churches, and an infirmary with nine beds and one healer.

The healer was a hand-door Kindled named Brother Aldous, Fire rank, who could put his palm on a fever and draw it down like water out of a well. He was very good, and very old, and the infirmary's director wanted to know how he did it before he died.

So the director had paid for the pages. And Oriel's job was to copy them.

She spread them on the long table in the records room under the good window. There were forty-one sheets. Some were in one hand and some in another. All of them had been ruled into columns with a straightedge, so carefully that the lines did not waver even where the paper had been folded.

At the top of the first sheet someone had written the headings.

*Exercise. What went wrong. Who was hurt. What it cost. How long to come back. Second go.*

And then, squeezed in at the right edge in a different ink, a seventh column.

*Why.*

Oriel sharpened three pens and began.

It was slow work. Some of the entries were training drills from the north yard at Meridian. A pair of hand-door students holding a ward against a sack swung off a frame. A Kindled boy with an Ember learning to keep his Edge dark until he needed the light. Some were field notes from a crew Meridian called the Home Line, which went out past the walls to answer tears and bring back people who were hurt. There were initials instead of names. *T.V. J. D.W. K.* There were costs in hours and in ash. There were second attempts, and third, and the ones that had worked on the fourth.

There were pages about radiation.

*Recall. Active burden only. Read it, draw it, spend to separate, vent before it lodges. Does not undo what is already done. Stop when the ash is out, or you are next. D.W.*

Oriel read that one three times. Two years ago a scavenger crew had come back down the river from the dead power station at Corbel Reach with their skin peeling. Brother Aldous had tried to help them. He had not known how. He had made himself sick trying.

She copied it very carefully.

The director came in at noon to see how she was getting on. He was a big, tidy man who liked the infirmary to run like a clock. He leaned over her shoulder and read for a while. Then he put his finger on the seventh column.

"Leave that out," he said.

"Sir?"

"The why. Copy the methods. What they did, what went wrong, what it cost. That's the useful part." He tapped the column. "This is commentary. It doubles the ink and the paper and the hours. We are not a library."

Oriel looked at the column under his finger.

*The lane with the crooked posts. Why: because the road isn't straight. S.V.*

"I don't know what half of these mean," she said.

"Then they won't be any use to Aldous, will they?"

He went out. Oriel sat for a while with her pen in her hand.

On the next sheet was a note somebody had squeezed in sideways along the margin. The hand was small and slanting and different from the others.

*Heard from a woman who had it from a man who was there: the valley people, the ones who were burned with their papers, thought the sky had a lid on it. They thought the bombs in the Fall cracked the lid, and that is why the tears come. They measured something. Nobody has their numbers now. Writing it down because nobody else will.*

Oriel did not know what valley that was. She did not know who had burned it. She thought about the word *lid* for a long time, and then she thought that it did not matter whether she believed it. Somebody had thought it was worth carrying ten days up a river and ten days down again.

She dipped her pen.

She copied the methods first, the way the director wanted. Then, in the narrow space at the right of each page, she copied the seventh column too. She made her letters smaller so that it would not waste much paper. It doubled the hours. She stayed after the lamps were lit to make up the time.

*The lane with the crooked posts. Why: because the road isn't straight.*

She did not know what road. She copied it anyway.

When she was finished, she wrapped the old pages back in their oilcloth for the next barge going south. Then she sat for a minute with her new copy in front of her, forty-one sheets with seven columns each, and put her hand flat on top of the pile.

"There," she said, to nobody. "Now you're in two places."

---

## 5. Rotation

*2170 — ninety years after the Fall*

The control building at Corbel Reach still had its clocks. They had all stopped at the same minute, ninety years ago, and they had stayed stopped.

Ines Coyle could see one through the gap in the blast door. It hung over a long grey desk covered in dead switches. She looked at it every time the winch pulled her through. She could not help it.

"Twelve minutes," said Abel Strand behind her. "Not thirteen."

"I know."

"You said that last time, and you came out at thirteen and a half."

"I was holding a binder."

"The binder doesn't care about your marrow," said Abel. "I do."

He was a short, square man with grey in his beard and a healer's soft hands. He wore the same lead-lined apron she did. It did not do much. It was not meant to. The apron kept dust off. The real protection was distance, time, and him.

They stood at the yellow line painted across the old car park, two hundred paces from the reactor hall. Behind them was the camp: four tents, a winch on a sledge, a cart on a cable, and a crate of ash tins that Abel guarded the way other men guarded money. In front of them was the plant. The concrete was streaked black. The cooling tower had a crack down one side like a split egg. Weeds grew out of the roof of the turbine hall.

When Ines was a girl, everyone in the river towns had known the story of Corbel Reach. How the backup generators ran out of diesel in the second winter after the Fall. How the pumps stopped. How the core got hotter and hotter with nobody to cool it, until the steam came up and the wind went east. For eighty years nobody had gone closer than the far bank.

Now Meridian's Home Line had sent four people.

"Dosimeter," said Abel.

Ines held up the hardened meter on its strap. It was an old design, a chamber of gas and a needle and no chip anywhere inside it. The needle sat near the bottom of its arc. Abel wrote the number in his book.

His book was a thick, cheap ledger with a waxed cover. On the left page he kept the rotation: who went in, when, for how long, what the needle said going in and coming out. On the right he kept the ash. Every tin, every pinch, to the dram.

"Go," he said. "Twelve."

The winchman, Dov, threw the lever. The cable took up slack. Ines held the cart rail and walked beside it, past the yellow line, past the second line, across the cracked yard to the blast door. The meter on her chest ticked faintly. She tried not to listen to it.

Inside, the air was cold and smelled of wet concrete. Her lamp lit the long room in pieces. Desk. Panel. A chair still pushed back from the desk, as if someone had stood up quickly. The binders were on a shelf behind the shift supervisor's station. Forty of them, in grey plastic covers gone soft with damp. Operating procedures. Emergency procedures. Maintenance logs. Ninety years of the old world's knowledge about how to keep a reactor from doing what this one had done.

She took three binders and put them in the cart. She took three more. She looked at the dead clock. Six minutes.

On the way back she stopped at the panel. It was the instrument panel for the core. Behind it, through a small window of thick yellow glass, she could see a gauge that still had its needle. She wanted that panel. She wanted to know what the core had been doing in its last hour. She had wanted it since she was fourteen and found her first page of an engineering manual in a box of kindling.

She put her hand on the panel cover. Then she looked at the meter on her chest.

She pulled the cart out at eleven minutes and fifty seconds.

---

Abel was waiting at the yellow line with his sleeves rolled up.

"Sit," he said.

She sat on the upturned crate. He knelt in front of her and put his palms flat on her collarbones, then on her ribs, then on her thighs above the knee. He closed his eyes. His face changed. It went still and listening, the way her grandmother's face had gone when she listened for a kettle.

"There," he said. "It's in your long bones. It's gritty. Like sand in a blanket."

"Can you get it?"

"Some." He opened one of the ash tins with his thumbnail and tipped a measure of grey powder into his left palm. It was fine and pale and faintly warm. He closed his hand around it. "Hold still."

Ines had seen him do this twenty times in two days. She still did not understand it. Abel was hand-door Kindled, Fire rank, trained at Meridian under the new rotation rules. He had told her that recall was not healing. Healing was mending something broken. Recall was finding the part of the poison that was still active, still burning away inside a living body, and pulling it out before it did more damage. It could not undo what was already done. If a bone had already been hurt, it stayed hurt.

What it could do was keep the damage from going on.

She felt it as a drawing ache, deep in her thighs and hips, as if something were being pulled through her by a thread. Then it was gone. Abel's left fist tightened. The ash in it went from pale grey to dark, almost black, and he gasped a little.

He got up, walked to the vent pit, and put both hands down into the wet clay.

The vent pit was a hole dug at the edge of the camp, ringed with orange cord and lined with clay from the river. Abel knelt there for a slow count of thirty. When he took his hands out, they were clean, and he was pale. The spent ash lay in the clay like soot.

"Don't touch that," he said. "Ever. We'll seal it when we go."

He came back and wrote in the book. *Rotation four. I.C. 11 min 50. Out: 41. Recalled to 9. Ash: 3 dr.* Then, on the right page, he subtracted.

"How much is left?" said Ines.

"Enough for you once more. If I clear Linnet after her next pull, there's nothing for me." He did not look up. "Linnet's at her line already. She's done."

Linnet was their second healer. She was lying in the tent with a wet cloth over her face. She had cleared Dov twice that morning when the cable jammed and he had to go out to free it.

"Then you clear me once more," said Ines. "And I get the core panel."

Abel closed the book.

"No," he said.

"Abel—"

"You get the core panel, you come out at forty-five again. Maybe fifty, if the panel sticks. I clear you, and the tin's empty. And then I've got whatever I couldn't separate from you sitting in my own hands, and nothing to spend getting it out." He held up his palms. They were trembling. "Recall's not free. It's paid for. When the ash runs out, it gets paid for out of me. And if I keep going after that, I'm the next one on the cot."

"It's three more minutes."

"It's the line." He tapped the book. "You can see the numbers same as I can. A ward won't help you. Linnet can't push this stuff off you. Nobody can. It's not a thing a ward moves. All we've got is time, distance, ash, and the line. And we're at the line."

Ines looked at the plant. The crack in the cooling tower ran down from the lip like a river on a map. She thought about the binders in the cart, the shift logs, the emergency procedures, the chair pushed back from the desk.

"You've done this before," she said.

"I've done it at Meridian. On scavengers." He looked at her then. "I've also watched a healer go past the line. She cleared three men out of the Ridley mine. She went in for a fourth. We buried her in the spring."

He opened the book again and turned it around so that she could read it.

At the bottom of the right page, under the arithmetic, in his blunt hand: *Abort called, rotation 5, A.S. Ash out. Why: healer at line. Records recovered: 6 binders. Access proven.*

"That's not a failure," he said. "That's the job. We come back in the autumn with forty tins and two more healers. And now everybody knows we can."

Ines read the line twice. Then she nodded.

They sealed the vent pit with a lid of river clay and marked it with orange stakes. In the evening, Ines sat by the lamp in the tent and opened the first binder. The pages were swollen and the ink had run in places, but most of it was there. *Loss of offsite power. Station blackout. Decay heat removal.*

She read until her eyes hurt. Then she turned to a clean page in her own notebook and wrote down the first thing the old world had known that this one did not.

*The core does not need a person to stay hot. It needs a person to stay cool.*

---

## 6. The Weir

*2182 — one hundred two years after the Fall*

Gideon Sayle had not slept properly in four days, and the stranger in the doorway was eating his bread.

"Sorry," said the stranger. He did not look sorry. He looked hungry. "The woman at the gate said I could have it. She said you weren't going to eat it anyway."

"Who are you?"

"Tamar Vey." He finished the crust and dusted his hands on his coat. "I teach letters. And the reasons, if anyone wants them. Most people don't. The Home Line sends me around the work camps in winter. I came to see if anyone here wants to learn to read."

"Everyone here can read," said Gideon.

"Then I'll sit by your stove and be useless," said Tamar. "I'm good at it."

He was a large, slow, untidy man of about fifty, with a limp in his left leg and a beard that had been cut by someone in a hurry. His coat was patched at the elbows with two different colors. He had a satchel over his shoulder that clanked when he put it down. He lowered himself onto the bench by the workshop stove with a long sigh and stretched his bad leg out toward the heat.

Gideon went back to his table.

The workshop at Corbel Reach was a long tin shed against the old turbine hall. Twelve years of recovered binders filled the shelves along one wall. Along another were parts. Valves, pump housings, lengths of pipe, pressure gauges with their glass cleaned. In the middle of the floor, on a test stand, sat the reason Gideon had not slept.

It was an ash cell. A sealed steel can the size of a bread crock, with a pinch of refined ash inside and a coil of copper wound around its waist. When a hand-door Kindled held a pattern on it, the ash gave up its warmth slowly and the copper drew off a thin, steady current. It was enough to run a small pump.

For about nine hours.

Then the ash went to dead grey dust, and the pump stopped.

"That's the problem," said Gideon, to nobody. He had been saying it to nobody for a week. "That's the whole problem."

"What is?" said Tamar from the stove.

Gideon did not want to explain. He was too tired. But the shed was quiet, and the two welders had gone to supper, and the man by the stove was looking at him with plain, patient interest, so he explained.

"The old reactor here melted because the pumps stopped," he said. "Its own heat didn't stop. A reactor keeps making heat for days after you shut it down. Weeks. The pumps carry the heat away. When the power to the pumps went, the heat stayed. And it kept building up until it broke everything."

"I know that story," said Tamar.

"We can build a new one. Small. We have the plans, we have the people, we're getting the fuel. But the first thing anyone at Meridian asks is, what happens when the pumps stop? Because this time there's no grid. If a valve sticks or a cable goes in a storm, there's nobody to call."

"So you want the pumps to run on ash."

"Ash is the only power we have that a person can carry in a can. But it runs out. Nine hours." Gideon pushed his hands through his hair. "If I could make it burn for nine weeks, the reactor would be safe. I've tried thinner patterns. Colder patterns. Finer grades. I've had three Kindled holding one cell at once until they nearly fainted. Nine hours. Ten, once."

Tamar was quiet for a while. Then he said, "My uncle had a mill."

Gideon closed his eyes.

"I'm not changing the subject," said Tamar. "I'm going the long way round. I always go the long way round. It's the leg." He shifted on the bench. "The mill was on the Tarrow, below the ford. There was a millrace off the river. You know what a millrace is?"

"A channel. To the wheel."

"Right. Now, the river had all the strength in the valley. More water went past that mill every hour than my uncle could have carried in his whole life. And he never added a drop to it. Couldn't. What he did, every spring, was go out in his boots and set the stones in the weir. Big flat stones, set just so. The stones told the river where to go. Most of it went on down the valley like always. A little of it went into the race, and turned the wheel, and ground the corn."

"I'm not a miller."

"No. Listen. Here's the part I liked when I was a boy." Tamar held up one finger. "My uncle went home for supper. And the stones kept telling the river where to go. All night. All summer. He wasn't standing there holding them. He'd set them, and they held."

Gideon opened his eyes.

"You keep trying to make the ash be more river," said Tamar. "Maybe it's better at being stones."

"Ash isn't stone. It burns."

"Does it?" Tamar reached for his satchel. "Can I show you something? It's small. It might not work. It didn't, the first time I tried it, and I burned my thumb."

He took out a tin bowl, a short length of copper wire bent into a hook at each end, a twist of paper, and a smooth river stone the size of an egg. He put the stone on the stove top. While it warmed, he filled the bowl with water from the bucket by the door. He hung the copper wire across the bowl so that one hooked end rested on the rim beside the stone and the other end hung over the far rim into a tin cup. Then he went outside, came back with a cupful of snow, and set it under the far end of the wire.

"Hand-door," he said, touching his own chest. "Ward, mostly. I held a line on the Home Line for eight years before my leg went. I'm not much good anymore. But I can still do a little fence."

He took the stone off the stove with his sleeve and set it in the water at the near edge of the bowl. Steam lifted off it. Then he opened the twist of paper and tipped a pinch of ash onto the copper where it touched the stone.

He put his hands around the bowl without touching it and closed his eyes.

Gideon felt nothing. He saw nothing. For a moment, it looked like an old man warming his hands.

Then the steam stopped rising from the water.

The stone sat in the bowl, still hot, and the water around it did not warm. Instead, a thin line of frost began to vanish from the copper wire, from the near end to the far end. Over the tin cup, a drop of water fell off the wire into the snow. Then another. The snow began to sink around the wire's tip.

"I'm putting a fence round the stone's heat," said Tamar quietly. "A little ward. It can't go into the water. It can only go one way. Along the copper."

"You're holding it."

"Yes. Now watch."

He took his hands away. He put them in his armpits and leaned back on the bench.

The fence held.

The water around the hot stone stayed cool. The snow in the tin cup went on melting, drop by drop, from the copper's tip. Gideon counted under his breath without meaning to. Ten. Twenty. Thirty. Tamar sat with his hands in his armpits and his eyes open and did nothing at all.

At about fifty, the pinch of ash on the copper went from pale grey to dull, dead grey. The water around the stone began to steam again. The dripping stopped.

"There," said Tamar. "The ash ran out."

Gideon stared at the bowl.

"It didn't burn," he said slowly.

"No."

"It didn't give off heat. It wasn't the fuel. The stone was the fuel." He stood up. His chair fell over behind him and he did not notice. "It held what you told it. After you let go."

"For about fifty breaths," said Tamar. "With a pinch. I'm not very good." He leaned forward and looked into the bowl, as interested as if he had never seen it before. "I don't know anything about reactors. I couldn't tell you what's inside one. But it seems to me your reactor already knows how to make heat. It's very good at heat. It's too good. What it doesn't have is somebody standing there all night setting the stones."

Gideon looked at the ash cell on the test stand. Nine hours of current. Nine hours of trying to make ash into more river.

"You want the ash to hold the rules," he said. "Around the heat. Not be the heat."

"I don't want anything," said Tamar. "I'm a schoolteacher. I'm asking."

Gideon picked up his chair. He sat down in it. Then he got up again and went to the shelf and took down a binder and opened it and put it back. He did not know where to start. He knew, already, that it would take years. He knew that the pattern would have to be far more complicated than a little fence around a stone. Somebody would have to find out how big a pinch could hold how complicated a rule, and for how long, and what happened when it ran out halfway. Somebody would get burned. Maybe worse. Nothing about the bowl told him how to build anything.

But it told him what to try.

Tamar stayed three days. He taught the younger welder to read the long words in the binders. He told stories at supper that were mostly about his uncle's mill and a goat that had once eaten a ledger. He fell asleep by the stove every night with his mouth open. On the fourth morning he packed his bowl and his copper and his twist of paper and went on down the river road toward the next camp, limping, with Gideon's last loaf of bread under his arm.

Gideon never saw him again.

That night he opened the workshop book to a clean page. He wrote the date. He wrote the question as plainly as he could.

*Q (from T. Vey, Home Line teacher): Can ash hold the rules around the heat, instead of being the heat?*

Under it he wrote: *Answer: unknown.*

Under that: *Begin tomorrow.*

---

## 7. Commissioning

*2195 — one hundred fifteen years after the Fall*

By the time they lit Unit One, there were three hundred people on the hill above Corbel Reach, and most of them had brought lunch.

Gideon Sayle watched them from the control room window. He was sixty-one now, and his knees hurt in the cold. There were families on blankets. There were children sliding down the frosted grass on tin trays. There was a woman from the Meridian news sheet with a pad of paper and a pencil behind each ear. Somebody had brought a fiddle.

"They think it's a festival," said Pia Morrow beside him.

"It is a festival," said Gideon. "They're just early."

Pia was the operator. She was thirty, with short hair and steady hands, and she had trained for six years on a mock control desk built out of wood and painted cardboard. Now she sat at a real one. Every needle on it was hardened. Every switch had a mechanical backup. In front of her, under a hinged cover painted red, was the scram lever. If she pulled it, the control rods would drop into the core on their own weight. No power needed. No pattern needed. Just gravity.

"Board's clear," she said. "Lattice crew?"

"Lattice holding," said the speaking tube from the core hall.

Unit One was small. That was the first thing everyone said about it. It would make enough power for the town of Corbel, the workshops, and the hospital at Lowmarket by wire down the river. The old reactor had been forty times bigger. Nobody wanted to build one that big again, not yet.

The second thing everyone said was the lattice.

It sat around the core like a cage. Forty-eight steel cartridges, each the size of a man's forearm, each packed with graded ash. Over thirteen years, Gideon's people had learned to set patterns in them the way Tamar had set a fence around a stone. Not one fence. Hundreds of small rules, woven together. *Heat may go this way. Not that way. If it builds here, spread it there.* Six Kindled had spent a month impressing the master pattern, two Ward and two still-door and a pair of Sustain healers to read the metal for weak places. A Blaze-rank ward master from Meridian had checked every cartridge by hand and signed every one in the book.

Once it was set, nobody had to stand beside it.

The water still carried the heat to the turbine. The pumps still ran. But if the pumps stopped, the lattice would hold the core's heat in a safe shape and spread it into the great sink of cold water under the floor. It would hold for weeks, while the ash lasted. Long enough for people to come.

That was the idea.

"Withdrawing rods," said Pia. "Ten percent."

The needles moved.

On the hill, the fiddle stopped. People stood up on their blankets. There was nothing for them to see. There was no light, no sound, no smoke. There was only the great grey building sitting in the snow, the same as before. But they stood up anyway, and some of them took off their hats.

Twenty percent. Thirty. The temperature needles climbed in slow, even steps. Steam went to the turbine. In the turbine hall, the big shaft began to turn.

At forty percent, Pia closed the main breaker.

Down the valley, along the one street of Corbel that had been strung with new wire, the lamps came on.

Gideon heard the cheer from the hill through the thick glass. He saw children jumping. He saw the woman from the news sheet writing with both hands. He let out a breath he had been holding, it felt like, for thirteen years.

"Sixty percent," said Pia. "Following load."

It held at sixty for twenty minutes. Everything was even. The lattice cartridges each had a small gauge on the outside of the core hall, forty-eight needles in a ring, and every one of them sat in the green.

Then cartridge thirty-one began to drop.

Gideon saw it before Pia did. It was on the far side of the ring, nearest the hot channel where the coolant came in. Its needle slid from green into yellow. The ones on either side of it did not move.

"Thirty-one," he said.

Pia looked. "It's spending faster than the others."

"Why?"

"I don't know. It's closest to the hot channel. It's doing more work." Her voice was very calm. "Twenty-nine and thirty-three are steady."

The needle on thirty-one kept falling. Yellow. Low yellow.

On the core temperature board, one needle twitched.

It was a single thermocouple, on the hot-channel side. It went up two degrees. Then five. The others stayed where they were.

"It's leaning," said Gideon. He could feel his heart in his throat. "When thirty-one runs out, that part of the fence forgets. And the heat leans into the gap."

"Lattice crew," said Pia into the tube. "Can you hold thirty-one?"

"Trying." The voice from the core hall was strained. "It's deep in the ring. Rao's reaching for it. It's drinking faster than he can—"

The needle on thirty-one touched red.

The hot-channel thermocouple jumped eleven degrees in a breath.

Pia lifted the red cover and pulled the scram lever.

There was a heavy, rattling thud from the core hall, felt through the floor more than heard. The control rods fell. The needles on the board shuddered and began, all together, to fall. On the hill, the cheering faltered. Down the valley, the lamps of Corbel went dark.

For a long moment, nobody in the control room spoke.

Then the hot-channel needle peaked, paused, and began to come down.

The lattice held around the shut core. Forty-seven cartridges in the green, one in the red, and the heat spreading slowly, safely, into the cold water under the floor. Exactly the way it was meant to. Exactly the way the old reactor never had.

Gideon sat down in the chair beside the operator's desk because his legs would not hold him.

"Well," said Pia. Her hands were shaking now that it was over. "That's not in the binders."

"No."

"It wasn't the pattern. The pattern was fine. It was the fuel. One cartridge doing more work than the others, running out first. And once one runs out, its neighbors have to carry its share. And then they run out faster." She looked at the ring of needles. "If we'd been at a hundred percent, we'd have lost three before I saw it."

"Then nobody runs at a hundred," said Gideon. "And nobody lets one cartridge get past half before it's changed. And nobody runs a cartridge without a pair on the other side of it." He was already reaching for the book. "Every cartridge gets its own meter on the desk. Not out in the hall. Here."

He wrote it down. Date. Time. *Unit One, first commissioning. Held 60% for 22 min. Cart. 31 spent ahead of ring (hot channel load). Local heat lean, +18. Scram, P.M. Lattice held core in shutdown. No release.*

Then, in the column on the right, under *Why*:

*Ash is finite and does not spend evenly. A fence with one weak stone is not a fence. Check the pairs.*

Outside, the people on the hill had not gone home. They were standing in the snow, waiting to be told whether to be afraid. Gideon put on his coat and went out to tell them the truth: that the new reactor had found a way to fail on its first day, and had failed safely, and that this was the best possible thing that could have happened, because now they knew.

Some of them believed him. The woman from the news sheet wrote down every word.

Six weeks later, they lit it again. It ran for nineteen years.

Every shutdown code written after that, in every plant from Corbel Reach to the coast, began with the same three words.

---

## 8. First Light on Weighbridge Street

*2247 — one hundred sixty-seven years after the Fall*

The core came on a flat wagon pulled by a lift-truck, and the whole of Weighbridge Street came out to watch it go by.

Bram Oduya rode on the back with it. It was the size of a wardrobe and painted the dull green of everything the Halden Works made, with a brass plate on the side. *Compact Core, Domestic, Class C. Pattern impressed under Halden license. Do not open. Do not move while running. CHECK THE PAIRS.*

"Is that it?" said a boy on the curb. "Is that the reactor?"

"That's it," said Bram.

"It's small."

"It only has to do one building."

The boy looked unconvinced. Bram did not blame him. Across the canal, in the Lantern district, the big houses had had cores in their cellars for twenty years. You could see the Lantern from the Weighbridge at night, a long low wash of steady white light along the far bank. On this side, people still lit their rooms with oil, and with the old solar panels on the roofs, and with whatever ash lamps they could afford to have refilled. The new coal plant upriver had closed in Bram's grandfather's time. The big solar fields outside the city were still there, tilted at the sky, but these days they were mostly backup. Everything worth having ran on cores and cells now.

Everything on the far side of the canal, anyway.

The Pellow Building was five stories of yellow brick with a crack over the door. Its landlord, Mr. Pask, met them on the step in a good coat.

"About time," he said. "I've been paying for that license since spring."

"The core's the core," said Bram. "It comes when the Works says it comes."

It took him and his apprentice all morning to set it in the cellar. They bolted it to the floor. They ran its heavy cables to the induction board on the wall, and from the board up through the building to a small steel cell box in every flat. The core would make the heat. Its pattern, impressed in the Halden yards by a Glory master Bram had never seen and never would, would hold the fission steady and spread its heat to the induction ring. The ring would turn heat into current. The cell boxes in the flats would take that current and store it and give it out, and each one had its own meter, its own little ledger, and its own red handle.

It was not magic in the old sense, the sense that meant a woman with her hands on a sick cow. It was a hundred and fifty years of hard, careful work, and a license fee, and a Works inspector's signature.

That signature was Bram's job.

At noon he went up to the second floor to fit the first cell box. Flat 2C belonged to a woman named Hester Quayle. She opened the door with a wooden spoon in her hand and a small boy hanging off her skirt.

"You're the power man," she said.

"I'm the install man. The power comes later."

"How much later?"

"Today, if everything passes."

She let out her breath. Bram noticed then that the flat was very cold. There was a little oil stove in the corner, not lit. On the windowsill, packed in a bowl of snow, were four small glass vials.

"Insulin," said Hester, when she saw him look. "For Jory. The chemist on Mill Lane keeps it cold for me, but he charges, and in summer I can't—" She stopped. "The cell box. It'll run a cold-cupboard?"

"It'll run a cold-cupboard."

"All the time? Even at night?"

"All the time," said Bram. "That's what it's for."

He fitted the box on the wall beside her stove. Then he went down to the cellar to fetch the building's cells.

The cells were the last piece. Each was a sealed can of refined ash that slotted into a flat's cell box and held the building's reserve, so that if the core shut down, the lights and the cold-cupboards would run for a few days more. Mr. Pask had bought the cells himself. They were stacked in a crate by the cellar door, twelve of them, each stamped on the lid. *Grade First. Clean.*

Bram picked one up.

He was hand-door Kindled, Flame rank, which was not much. But every installer who carried a Works license had to pass a grading test, and Bram had passed his on the first try. He did not need the meter, though he would use it anyway. He closed his hand around the can and felt for the ash inside.

It was wrong.

Clean ash felt like warm sand, smooth and even all the way through. This felt like sand with gravel in it. Lumpy. Dull in places. Somebody had cut the good ash with something else, ordinary stove ash or soot, and then stamped the can *First*.

He tried a second can. The same. A third.

He put the meter on the fourth to be sure. The needle stopped well short of the line.

"Mr. Pask," he said.

The landlord came down the cellar steps. "Problem?"

"Your cells are cut." Bram held one out. "They're stamped First. They grade at Third, maybe lower. There's filler in them."

"Nonsense. I paid First for them."

"Then you were cheated. I'm sorry." Bram set the can down. "I can't sign them into the building."

Mr. Pask's face went red. "Do you know what those cost me?"

"I know what they'll cost the people upstairs." Bram kept his voice level. "Cut ash doesn't hold a pattern evenly. A cell with filler in it can run hot on one side. If the core trips some night and the building goes over to cells, one of these could cook its box. Or start a fire. Or just die at two in the morning when somebody's depending on it."

"Then fit them and I'll replace them later."

"I can't." Bram took out his license book and opened it on the crate. "If I sign it, it's my name. If I don't sign it, the building doesn't pass. That's how it works."

"The building doesn't pass, nobody gets power."

"Nobody gets power until you bring clean cells. A week, maybe. The Works will sell you graded stock."

Mr. Pask said several things then that Bram did not write down. Then he went back up the steps and slammed the cellar door hard enough to shake dust out of the ceiling.

Bram stood in the cellar for a while with his hands on the crate.

He thought about the four little vials in the bowl of snow.

Then he went out to his wagon and opened his own stock box. Installers carried two clean spare cells, Works graded, for testing boards. They were not for selling. They were not for giving away. He took one out, carried it up to the second floor, and knocked on 2C.

"The building doesn't pass today," he said, when Hester opened the door. He saw her face fall, and kept going. "But I can wire your cold-cupboard to a test cell. It's not on the core. It won't run your lights. It'll keep the cupboard cold for about two weeks. By then the building should be on."

She looked at the cell in his hands. "What does that cost?"

"It's a test cell. I'm testing your cupboard."

She did not believe him. He could see that. But she stepped back and let him in.

He fitted the cell. He wired it to the old cold-cupboard in the corner, which had been dead since before Jory was born. He checked the meter. He showed Hester the red handle and where the shutdown was and how to read the needle. Then he closed the switch.

The cupboard shuddered. Somewhere inside it, a small motor began to hum.

Jory let go of his mother's skirt. He walked across the floor and put his ear against the cupboard door.

"It's making a noise," he said.

"That's it working," said Bram.

The boy opened the door. A small yellow light came on inside. He stared at it as if it were the most wonderful thing he had ever seen, and Bram, who had seen a thousand of them, found that he could not look away from it either.

Hester moved the four little vials from the windowsill into the cupboard, one at a time, and shut the door.

Downstairs, in the cellar, Bram opened his license book and wrote it out. *Pellow Bldg, Weighbridge St. Core installed, bolted, tested. Board fitted. 12 cells refused, stamped First, graded Third or under. Not signed. 1 test cell (Works stock, mine) fitted to cold-cupboard 2C.*

Then, in the column on the right:

*Why: child's medicine. Cells come back clean or they don't come in.*

He knew the Works would read it. He knew he would probably pay for the cell out of his own wages. He wrote it anyway, because the next installer to come to the Pellow Building would want to know why the cupboard in 2C ran on a test cell. And because somebody, somewhere, ought to know that the ash said *First* and was not.

---

## 9. The Haul

*2362 — two hundred eighty-two years after the Fall*

The ash lay on the gravel bar in a long grey drift, and Tova Reinholt could see the river coming for it.

"That's a season," said Hob Lanyard over the speaking wire. He was the crew chief. His voice crackled in her ear above the whine of the lift engines. "That's the whole season, Tova. That's two new lifters and every wage on the boat paid through the winter."

"I can see it."

"Then let's get it."

She hung in her harness below the belly of the lift craft, thirty feet over the flooded valley, and looked down. The craft was a long, flat-bottomed thing with four lift rings, one at each corner, humming with the steady blue-white of ash induction. Under it, the Ferren valley had turned into a brown lake overnight.

The dam at the head of the valley was still standing. That was the good news. The bad news was the spillway. A rift had opened in the spillway channel at two in the morning, and something had come through it that the sensing masts at Saltmere had graded Barrel before it was all the way out. It had torn the spillway gates half off their hinges. The water behind the dam had been pouring through the gap ever since.

Tova's crew had killed it at dawn.

She was still shaking from that. It had taken all six of them, two hours, and most of their ash. It had been the size of a barn and it had moved like a landslide. When Kaspar finally put his spear into whatever it used for a heart, it had come apart all at once into the biggest drift of clean ash anyone on the boat had ever seen.

Now that ash lay on a gravel bar in the middle of the valley. The water was rising around it. In twenty minutes, maybe less, it would be under the flood. Ash that went into moving water spread out and settled in the mud, and it was gone. It was worth more than the lift craft. It was worth more than the lift craft twice.

"Fuel?" she said.

"Thirty-one on the ring meter," said Ansel, the pilot. "We can take the ash. It's heavy. Two lifts to the hill and then home on fumes. Or one lift of the ash and some of the rest scraped on the next pass."

"And the rift?"

"Closing," said the sensing operator, Mags, from the back. "Slowly. Masts say it's down to a crack. Another two hours, maybe, and it's shut."

Tova nodded to nobody. She turned in the harness and looked down the valley.

A mile below the gravel bar, the flood had reached the edge of Ferren village. Most of the people had got out in the night. She had seen them on the hills at dawn, wrapped in blankets beside their carts. But on the roof of a farmhouse at the far end of the village, there were three people and a dog. Two adults, one child. One of the adults was waving a red cloth.

The water was at the farmhouse's upper windows.

"Hob," she said.

"I see them."

"They weren't there an hour ago."

"They were inside. Now they're on the roof." His voice was flat. "The Vellen company boat is coming up from Corran. It'll be here in forty minutes."

"The water will be at the roof in thirty."

There was a silence on the wire.

"Tova," said Hob. "Listen to me. I know what the ledger says. I know. Lives first. But the ledger also says clean ash, and that's two hundred barrels of clean ash going into the mud. That's power for half of Saltmere for a winter. That's people too. You just don't see their faces."

She knew he was right. That was the worst part. Every ash cell in Saltmere ran on what companies like theirs brought home. Every hospital, every cold-cupboard, every pump. The company standings counted clean ash for a reason. The people who drew up the standings had not been fools.

She looked at the meter on the ring. Thirty-one.

"Ansel. If we go for the roof, what's the fuel after?"

"Down to twelve, maybe. Fourteen. Enough to get the family to the hill and the boat home. Not enough to come back for the ash."

"And if I go on the harness? Not the boat."

"Your harness is at nine minutes."

Nine minutes of lift. The personal rigs were not made for long flights. They were made for short, hard jumps by people trained to use them. Tova had trained for six years. Fewer than one fighter in twenty in the companies was licensed to wear one. On a good day she could hold a straight line in a crosswind for nine minutes, set down soft, and lift again. She had never carried a child on one.

"I'll go to the roof on the harness," she said. "You bring the boat down over the house. I'll put them on the ladder one at a time. That's less fuel than the boat landing in the water."

"And the ash?" said Hob.

She looked at the gravel bar. The brown water was already lapping at the edges of the grey drift.

"The ash goes in the river," she said.

Hob did not answer. Then the wire clicked.

"All right," he said. "All right. Go. I'll write it."

She went.

The harness was a frame of struts across her back and down both legs, with two small lift rings at her shoulders. She thumbed the throttle, felt the ash in the cells at her hips warm against her skin, and dropped out from under the boat. The wind caught her at once. She leaned into it, found her line, and flew.

It was a mile. She did it in a little under three minutes, low over the water, with the spray coming up at her and the cell heat building at her hips. The farmhouse grew larger. She could see them clearly now. A man in a wet shirt. A woman with a little girl of about five in her arms. A brown dog with its tail between its legs.

She set down on the roof ridge harder than she meant to. Her knee hit the tiles. The man grabbed her arm before she slid.

"Company," she said. "Saltmere. There's a boat coming. You're going up a ladder."

"The baby first," said the woman. "Please. Her first."

The lift craft came down the valley behind her, low and loud. Its downdraft tore at the water and sent spray across the roof. Ansel brought it in over the house as gently as Tova had ever seen him fly, and the rope ladder came down and slapped against the tiles.

Tova took the child. The little girl screamed and grabbed her hair. Tova held her against her chest, got one boot on the ladder rung, and climbed one-handed with the harness throttled up just enough to take half her weight. Kaspar's arms came down and took the child at the top.

She went back for the woman. Then the man. Then, because the man would not climb without it, the dog.

The harness meter was at two minutes when she pulled herself over the edge of the boat's deck. She lay there on her back with the downdraft roaring and the little girl crying somewhere behind her and every muscle in her body shaking.

Ansel lifted them away.

As they turned toward the hill, Tova rolled over and looked up the valley.

The gravel bar was gone. There was only brown water where it had been, and a faint grey sheen spreading out on the current like smoke.

Hob came and crouched beside her. He had the company book open on his knee. He did not say anything for a while. Then he turned it so she could see what he had written.

*Ferren valley. Barrel-grade spawn killed 0615, all six. Rift closing, masts confirm. Spillway gates: lost. Lives: three civilians and one dog lifted from roof, 0702. Clean ash: est. 200 bbl. Lost to flood.*

Under that, in the narrow column:

*Why: they were on the roof.*

"The standings board will dock us for the ash," said Hob. "We'll be fourth this season. Maybe fifth."

"I know."

"The Vellen boat would have been here in forty minutes."

"The water would have been at the roof in thirty."

He looked at her. Then he closed the book.

"That's what I wrote," he said.

---

## 10. The Superseded Stacks

*2603 — five hundred twenty-three years after the Fall*

The committee met on the top floor of the Compact Library, in a room that had been built for ballroom dancing two hundred years earlier and now held a table long enough for nineteen people. Marisol Quenby sat at the far end of it with the minutes, because that was what junior archivists did.

"Item eleven," said the chair. "The reasons column."

He was a pleasant man named Teague Orme, with neat silver hair and a Halden Works pin on his lapel. House Halden had paid for the new edition of the *Standard Lane Manual*. It had paid for the committee. It had paid, very generously, for the library's new roof. Nobody in the room said this aloud, and everybody knew it.

"The current edition," said Orme, "runs to nine hundred and forty pages. Over a third of that is the seventh column. Reasons. Most of them are anecdotal. Some are folklore. Some contradict each other. And none of them change what a student is actually asked to do." He smiled at the table. "We propose to keep the exercises, the failure modes, the costs and the recovery times. All of that is measurable. We'll retire the reasons to an appendix. Or a separate volume, for scholars."

"A separate volume nobody will print," said the woman from the Order of the Even Hand.

Her name was Warden Ottilie Sand. She was in grey, with her hands folded on the table. The Even Hand was a Ward order, and its people trained in a single door for their whole lives. Marisol had heard that some of them did not speak for a year at a time. Warden Sand spoke perfectly well.

"Every one of those reasons was paid for," said Sand. "By someone who got hurt finding it out."

"And we honor them," said Orme. "But a manual is not a memorial. It's a tool. Take this one." He turned a page in front of him. "*The lane with the crooked posts. Why: because the road isn't straight.* With respect, Warden, that isn't a reason. It's a riddle. Students laugh at it."

"Then they should go and find out why it's funny."

"Or we could simply say *retained for ceremonial continuity*," said Orme, "and spare them the trouble."

There was a vote. Marisol wrote it down. Eleven for. Six against. Two abstaining.

The reasons column was retired.

---

Afterward, Warden Sand caught her in the stairwell.

"You're the one who catalogs the Annick papers," said Sand.

Marisol stopped with her arms full of minutes. "Yes, Warden."

"The Order wants them withdrawn."

"They were given to the library by the Halden family. With the house's endowment."

"They were stolen from the Order by a Halden heir eighty years ago and given to the library to make him look generous." Sand's voice did not rise. "Master Annick was ours. Those were private papers. She did not write them for strangers to read."

Marisol did not know what to say. She had been cataloging the Annick papers for three months. Hesper Annick had been the Even Hand's most famous master. She had reached Glory in 2431 and was said to have held a ward over the whole Eastbank flood-wall for nine days without rest. In 2461 she had walked out of the Order's house in the hills in the winter rifts and had not come back. The Order had recorded her as dead long ago. Her papers filled eleven boxes. Most of them were notes on Ward patterns that the Order had been begging to have back for decades.

The Order wanted the papers back so that it could burn some of them.

House Halden had paid to keep every one.

"I'll pass your request to the Keeper," said Marisol.

"Do," said Sand. She started down the stairs. Then she stopped and looked back. "You don't think much of us, do you? The orders. All that devotion. One door for a whole life. While the Halden yards light half the region."

"I don't know enough to think anything, Warden."

"Good answer." Sand almost smiled. "The houses keep the lights on. We keep the reasons. Somebody has to do both. Mind which one you're doing on any given day."

---

That evening, alone in the basement, Marisol did two things that were not in her instructions.

The first was small. The old edition of the *Standard Lane Manual* was being moved to the superseded stacks, where outdated books went to be kept and not read. She found the page with the crooked post on it. Beside the printed reason, in pencil, very small, she wrote: *Retired from standard ed., 2603. Committee vote 11–6. Original reason retained here.* Then she shelved it.

The second was the box marked *Annick: Personal*.

She had been saving it. She knew that the Order wanted this box most of all. She opened it carefully. Inside were letters, a few sketches of hands, and a thin notebook with a cracked green cover. The handwriting was large and quick and slanted hard to the right.

Most of it was about Ward. One entry, near the back, was not.

*Fever again. Fourth time with this dream, so I will write it. Somebody should.*

*They come from every road at once. I do not know them. I know what they are, because I know what I am, and they are that. Each one carries a different light. When they get close enough, their light touches mine and theirs, and none of us can tell any longer where one stops.*

*Above us the sky is not torn. I want to be very clear. I have seen rifts all my life and this is not a rift. The sky is not there. As if someone had taken the roof off a house in the night and the house woke and found itself under the stars. And what is above comes down and in. Not crushing. Full. Like standing under a wave that does not break. I have spent my whole life holding back pressure, and this is not a pressure I could hold back. It is too large. It is paying attention.*

*Every lamp in Meridian answers at once. Some go out. Some go white. One turns a color I have no word for.*

*I do not know if we gathered to hold it shut, or to break it open, or to take it, or only to stand there together when it came. I wake before I find out. Every time.*

*I do not know if it is a memory, or a warning, or only what a fever makes out of a Glory master's fears. I have told no one in the house. They would make it into doctrine by spring.*

*The sky unroofed. That is the only way I can say it.*

Marisol read it twice. Then she sat back in her chair and listened to the library's induction lamps hum in the dark rows.

She could withdraw it. That was what the Order wanted. It would be easy.

Instead, she took a fresh catalog card and wrote: *Annick, H. Personal notebook, c. 2446–2450. Includes fever account ("the sky unroofed"). Status: disputed. Retained.*

She filed the notebook in the box, and the box on the shelf, and the card in the drawer, where it would wait for somebody who needed it.

---

## 11. Three Doors

*2676 — five hundred ninety-six years after the Fall*

The Home Line courier came at seven in the morning, while Kiva Rowan was still trying to make her hair lie flat.

She heard the shop bell downstairs. Then Mara's voice, low and surprised. Then a long silence. Kiva put down the comb and went to the top of the stairs.

Her aunt was standing in the shop doorway in her work apron, holding a small grey case with a seal on it. A woman in a Home Line field coat was walking away down Coper Street toward the lift-tram stop. The morning was bright and cold. Above the rooftops, a pair of lift craft slid along the eastern flight lane toward the station, their rings glinting.

"Who was that?" said Kiva.

Mara did not answer at once. She shut the door. She turned the shop sign to *Closed*, though they were not open yet anyway. Then she set the case on the counter between a half-repaired pressure gauge and a stack of paper ledgers and looked at it as if it might bite.

"It's from Ilse," she said.

Kiva came down the stairs slowly. "Ilse's dead."

"I know she's dead." Mara put her hand flat on the case. "She left instructions with the Home Line. A sealed appointment. It was to come to you on the day of your Kindling and not before." She let out her breath. "She asked me, you know. Years ago. When your mother died. She said she wanted you to have it one day. I told her no."

"Have what?"

Mara broke the seal.

Inside, on a bed of faded green cloth, lay a disk about the width of Kiva's palm. It was dark, almost black, and smooth as river glass. Nine fine grooves ran around it in rings, each a little narrower than the last. There was a cord through a small hole near the edge.

Kiva did not touch it at first.

She remembered Ilse Corran a little. A tall woman with cropped grey hair and a long scar on her jaw, who had come to the flat sometimes when Kiva's mother was alive and laughed very loudly in the kitchen. After the substation fire, she had come once more and sat with Kiva on the stairs for an hour without saying much. Then she had gone back out to the field. Four years later, the Home Line had sent word that she was dead.

"What is it?" said Kiva.

"It's old," said Mara. "Older than the Compact, Ilse said. The Home Line passes it from one bearer to the next. Not in families. By appointment. The one who has it chooses who gets it." Her mouth tightened. "Ilse chose you."

"Why?"

"She didn't say. She didn't say much about it at all. Only that it was entrusted, and that it was yours to keep and not yours to sell, and that you'd understand it better when you were older." Mara looked at the disk. "I'm not sure she understood it herself."

Kiva picked it up.

It was cold. Not the cold of metal in a cold room. Colder than that, like a stone pulled out of a stream. The grooves were very fine under her thumb.

"You don't have to wear it today," said Mara.

"I want to."

Mara looked at her for a long moment. Then she took the cord and put it over Kiva's head herself, and tucked the disk inside her shirt, and pressed her palm flat against it for a second over the cloth. Her hand was rough and warm and smelled of oil.

"Right," she said. "Shoes. We'll miss the tram."

---

The Kindling for the Eastbank wards was held in the Old Yard of the Crooked Line School, which was the oldest part of the Meridian Compact and looked it.

Kiva had walked past it a hundred times. The rest of the Compact was glass and white stone and induction lamps, with flight lanes over the roofs and practice arenas you could hear cheering from three streets away. The Old Yard was a long walled strip of packed earth, with sheds down one side and a line of practice posts down the middle. The posts were black with age. Most were straight. One, at the far end, leaned.

Today there were braziers in the yard and benches for families, and a long table where three assessors in Compact grey sat with ledgers and instrument boxes. Behind the table stood the lit frame, a tall, open cage of fine wires taller than a man. Beside it was a projection board, a sheet of pale glass on a stand where the readings would show.

There were thirty-one children in Kiva's group. All of them were thirteen. All of them were trying to look as if they did not care.

The three stations stood in a row in the middle of the yard.

The first was the brazier. A bed of coals glowed in an iron bowl, and in the coals lay an Ember slab from the Compact's store, flat and water-grey, about the size of a hand. If the Ember door opened for you, the slab took your color when you held it. Then it was yours.

The second was the bowl. It was wide and shallow and full of water drawn from the rift-pool under the Wren House. If the hand door opened for you, the water answered your palms. Sometimes it rippled. Sometimes it rose.

The third was the stance stone, a flat grey slab worn smooth in the middle by six hundred years of feet. You stood on it. An assessor put a hand on your chest and pushed. If the still door opened for you, you did not move.

Most people opened at one station. That was how it worked. Your first door was whatever answered first, and that was who you would be. Ember-door. Hand-door. Still-door. Some people opened at none and had to come back the next year. Nobody opened at more than one.

Kiva went eleventh.

She walked to the brazier. The assessor beside it, a broad man with burn scars up both forearms, handed her a pair of tongs and nodded at the slab.

"Lift it out. Then hold it in your bare hands. It won't burn you if it's yours."

"And if it's not mine?"

"Then drop it fast." He smiled a little. "Go on."

She lifted it. It was heavier than it looked. She set the tongs down and took it in both hands.

It did not burn.

It was hot, deeply and evenly hot, like bread just out of the oven. And then it changed. The water-grey went out of it from the middle, and a color came in. Dull copper. Not bright. The color of an old pan. It spread to the edges of the slab and stayed.

The assessor wrote in his ledger. "Ember door," he said. "Congratulations. Step to the table."

Kiva stepped back from the brazier with the slab in her hands. To reach the table, she had to pass the bowl.

As she went by it, the water stood up.

It rose out of the bowl in a thin ring, a hand high, and leaned toward her like a plant toward a window. It was not touching her. Her hands were full of the hot copper slab, nowhere near the water.

Someone on the benches gasped.

Kiva stopped. The water ring hung there, trembling. Then, slowly, it sank back into the bowl.

The assessor at the bowl, a thin old woman, stood up very slowly from her stool.

"Put your hands over it," she said. "Just over. Don't touch."

Kiva did not want to. She looked back at the benches. Mara was on her feet in the third row, very still.

She set the slab down on the edge of the brazier table and held her palms over the water.

It rose to meet them. It stood up out of the bowl in a column as thick as her wrist and stopped a finger's width below her hands, and stayed there. She could feel it the way you feel heat off a stove. It was cool and close and waiting.

"Hand door," said the old woman. Her voice was not steady. "Hand door as well."

The yard had gone quiet. The other children were staring. The broad man from the brazier had come over. So had the third assessor, a young man with a ledger clutched to his chest.

"That's not possible," said the young man.
