"Write it," said the old woman.

"The stone," said the broad man. He was looking at Kiva the way her mother had once looked at a cell that was humming the wrong note. "Just to be sure. Get on the stone, girl."

Kiva stepped onto the stance stone. The middle of it was worn into a shallow cup. Her feet fit into it as if it had been made for them.

The broad man put his palm flat on her breastbone. "Brace," he said. And he pushed.

He was twice her size. He pushed the way you would push a door that was stuck. She felt it go through her chest and down into her legs.

And something under her answered.

It was not strength. She had never been strong. It was more like the moment on a moving tram when you stop holding the rail and find you have not fallen. Her feet went heavy. Her knees went soft and certain. The push went down through her into the stone and stopped there, as if the stone and the ground under it had agreed to take it for her.

She did not move.

The broad man pushed harder. His boots slid on the packed earth. Kiva stood in the cup of the stone and did not move at all.

He took his hand away and stared at it.

"Still door," he said quietly. "Mother's mercy. All three."

---

They put her in the lit frame.

She stood inside the cage of wires while the young assessor worked the instrument box. The projection board lit up. Columns of pale light rose on the glass, one for each door, the way they did for everyone.

Except that they would not hold still.

The Ember column rose to a quarter and dropped. The hand column jumped up past it and shivered. The still column sat low and steady for a breath and then leapt. They kept trading places. The young assessor tapped the box and swore under his breath. The total at the bottom of the board read a number, then a different number, then a row of dashes.

"It's the rig," he said. "It must be the rig."

"It's not the rig," said the old woman.

Inside the frame, Kiva had begun to hurt.

It started in the middle of her chest, under the disk. It felt like three people pulling on one blanket from three different sides. The pull went up and out toward her left shoulder in three thin, burning lines, as if somebody were drawing on her from the inside with a hot wire. She put her hand to her chest. Her breath went short.

"Get her out," said Mara's voice, from somewhere. "Get her out of that thing."

Kiva stepped out of the frame. Or she tried to.

The disk against her breastbone went cold.

Colder than it had been. Colder than anything. It was so cold it felt like a hole in her chest, and through that hole, something came in.

---

She was standing somewhere very high, and the people were coming.

They came from every direction at once. She could not see their faces. Each one was carrying light, and the lights were different. One was white like a lamp. One was red-gold like the coals in the brazier. One was a color like deep water. They walked toward her, and toward each other, from every road and every door there was. She did not know them. She knew what they were. Her body knew. They were very old and very bright and she could not look at any of them for long.

When they came close, their lights touched.

There was no sound. There was only the moment when one light met another and neither of them stopped.

Then she looked up, and the sky was gone.

It did not break. It did not tear. She had heard about rifts all her life and this was not a rift. There was simply no sky there anymore, no blue, no lid, nothing between her and what was above. And what was above came down.

It was not a weight. It was fullness. It pressed into everything, into the air and the ground and her skin and the spaces between her thoughts. It was enormous. It was paying attention. It did not speak. She did not think it could have been held back by anyone.

Below her, the whole city answered at once.

Every lamp. Every cell in every wall. The great cores sleeping under the districts. The rift-pool under the Wren House. Every Kindled person in Meridian, every open door. All of it rang like a struck bell. Some lights went out. Some went white. Some went a color she had no word for.

She did not know if the bright people had come to hold it shut, or to tear it open, or to take it for themselves, or only to stand together when it happened.

She tried to see their faces.

She woke up on the ground.

---

The packed earth was cold against her cheek. Somebody's coat was under her head. Mara was kneeling over her with one hand on her face and the other flat on her chest, over the disk, and Mara's face was white.

"Kiva. Kiva, look at me."

"I'm here."

"Can you sit up?"

She could. The yard tilted and came back. All around them, people were standing. The assessors were arguing at the table. On the projection board, the columns had gone dark.

"The lamps went out," somebody was saying. "All the lamps in the yard. Just for a second."

"It was the rig. The rig shorted."

"The rig doesn't run the lamps."

Kiva pulled at the neck of her shirt. On the skin of her chest, from the middle of her breastbone up toward her left shoulder, there were three thin red lines. They were not straight and they were not even. They looked like three streaks of heat drawn on her by a careless hand. When she touched them, they were tender.

The disk lay in the middle of them. It was cold. Ordinary cold now, like stone.

"What did you see?" said Mara, very low.

"People," said Kiva. "Coming. And the sky—" She stopped. She did not know how to say the rest. Already it was going, the way dreams go, leaving only pieces. Light touching light. A hole where the sky should be. Every lamp in the city ringing.

"It's all right," said Mara. "You don't have to say."

The old woman from the bowl came over to them. She crouched slowly, with a hand on her knee, and looked at Kiva for a long time.

"We'll write it down," she said at last. "All three. Classification deferred." She looked at Mara. "I'm sorry. I don't know what else to put. There isn't a column for this."

---

They walked out the far end of the Old Yard because it was closer to the tram.

Kiva carried her copper Ember slab in both hands. Mara walked beside her with one arm around her shoulders, which she had not done in public since Kiva was ten. Neither of them spoke. Behind them, the next child was walking to the brazier.

At the end of the yard, the last practice post leaned out of the line.

It was older than the others, or looked it. It was black and split with age and bound with two iron bands. All the other posts stood straight. This one tilted to the left, a little, as if it were listening for something down the lane.

Kiva stopped beside it.

"Why is it crooked?" she said.

Mara looked at it. Then she looked back at the yard, at the assessors still bent over the projection board, at the lit frame and the braziers and the bowl.

"I don't know," she said. "Nobody knows. It's always been like that." She tightened her arm. "Come home. We'll sort the rest out at home."

Kiva did not move for a moment.

She put her hand on the post. The wood was rough and cold. Under her shirt, the three lines on her chest throbbed slowly, in time with her heart, and the disk lay still between them.

Then Mara said her name again, and she let go of the post, and they walked on toward the tram.

When she looked back from the corner, the post was still there at the end of the straight line, leaning a little to the left, as if it were listening for something down the lane.

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-01.md

# Chapter 1 — Kiva: There Isn't a Column

*Four years later — 2680*

The draw meter on the wall of the Measure Hall had a sticky needle.

Kiva Rowan had noticed it twenty minutes ago, while she stood in the queue with three hundred other seventeen-year-olds and tried not to think about her chest. Every time a candidate at the first bench finished a reading, the needle dropped back toward zero, caught at the two, and hung there for half a breath before it fell the rest of the way. Nobody else seemed to see it. The assessors wrote down the numbers. The projection boards lit up. The crowd in the gallery murmured or clapped, depending.

"You're doing it again," said Anwen.

"Doing what?"

"Staring at a meter like it owes you money."

"It's sticking at the two. The pivot's dirty. Or the hairspring's tired."

"Kiva."

"Mara would have it open in a minute."

"Kiva." Anwen Pryce turned her head and looked at her. She had a way of doing that, flat and level, like a carpenter checking a shelf. "That is the least important thing in this room."

It was not, actually. A sticky needle meant every reading at the first bench was a little high on the way down. Probably nobody's rank would change because of it. Probably. But Kiva did not say that, because Anwen was right in the way that mattered. She was staring at the meter because the meter was easier to look at than the boards.

There were four projection boards, one over each rig, and each was a tall sheet of pale glass that lit up with columns when a candidate stood in the frame. Kiva had been watching them all morning. They were simple once you knew how to read them. Four columns. Four axes.

*Rank.* How much of you was lit. The old ladder: Kindled, Ember, Flame, Fire, Torch, and above that the two words people said quietly. Blaze. Glory.

*Hold.* How well you could control what you had. A number out of ten.

*Draw.* How much ash you burned to do a standard piece of work, against a standard. Lower was better. One meant average. A girl from the Weighbridge had pulled a seven-tenths an hour ago, and the gallery had stood up.

*Call.* That column was always dark on Classification Day. Call was judgment. You could not measure judgment standing alone in a cage of wires. You measured it in teams, in trials, under pressure. Today it stayed blank.

Under the four columns, in letters a hand high, the board showed the thing everybody was really waiting for.

*First door.*

Ember. Hand. Still.

"Next," called the clerk at the far rig, and the queue shuffled forward three paces.

Kiva shuffled with it. Her left forearm itched under the leather cuff where her Ember slab sat. The slab was the dull copper it had turned four years ago, in her hands, in the Old Yard. It had never been any other color. Under her shirt, against her breastbone, the disk lay cold.

And under the disk, from the middle of her chest to her left shoulder, the three heat-lines lay quiet. For now.

They were faint these days, most of the time. Three fine pink streaks, not straight and not even, like scratches from a cat that had been thinking about something else. You could only see them if you were close. You could only feel them if the doors began to argue.

They would argue today. They always did in the frame.

"What's your number?" said Anwen.

"Two-forty-one."

"I'm two-thirty-eight. So I go before you." Anwen took a folded paper from her coat pocket and opened it. Kiva saw that it was a list. Anwen always had a list. "Afterward I'm going straight to the Wren House desk to file for the field track. Then lunch. Then I'm going to find out which trial slot I drew. Then I'm going to the Graywater table and asking them how many field placements they're taking this year, because last year they said four and took three."

"You're going to ask a rift company to their faces why they lied?"

"I'm going to ask them to count." Anwen folded the list. "It's different."

---

The candidates ahead of them were mostly from the Compact's own preparatory lanes, which meant they had trained for this since they were thirteen. You could tell. They stood in the frames with their shoulders loose and their faces calm. They knew what the rigs wanted.

A tall boy with close-cut black hair stepped up to the near bench, and the gallery went quiet.

"That's Kellan Renn," said someone behind Kiva.

"I know who it is."

Kiva knew who it was too. Everybody in the Crooked Line School's junior lanes knew Kellan Renn. He had been the best Ember-door candidate in the Eastbank wards for three years running. He had never placed lower than first in a junior Measure. People said Graywater Company had sent someone to watch him spar when he was fifteen.

He did not look like a person who knew any of that. He looked like a person waiting for a bus.

The first rig was the draw bench. A small sealed lamp-cell sat on a steel plate, wired to the sticky meter on the wall. Every candidate did the same thing. You put your Ember, or your hands, or your stance on the plate, depending on your door. You held a standard pattern on the cell for sixty seconds. The cell lit. The meter measured how much ash it burned while you held it.

Kellan laid his left forearm on the plate. His Ember was on a steel cuff, a dark slab gone a deep blue-grey. He did not close his eyes. He just looked at the lamp.

It lit. It lit steadily, a calm white glow with no flicker in it at all. The needle on the wall climbed, found a place, and sat there without twitching.

At sixty seconds, the clerk called time. Kellan lifted his arm. The needle fell, caught at the two for half a breath, and dropped to zero.

*Draw: 0.82.*

The gallery clapped. Kellan nodded once, as if somebody had told him the time, and walked to the hold posts.

The hold posts were three steel uprights with clamps and gauges. Each tested one door. For an Ember-door candidate, it was Edge. You formed your weapon shape and held it inside a ring of sensing wire while a clerk counted. Then, if you could, you lit the edge-light and held that too.

Kellan raised his right hand. The metal came off his cuff in a smooth grey flow, ran over his fist, and closed into a blade. It was long and narrow and single-edged, with a very slight curve and no ornament at all. It looked like the shape a person would choose if they had thought about blades for years and decided to stop being interested in anything else.

"Light," said the clerk.

A line of light came on along the edge. It was thin and blue-white and perfectly steady. It did not shiver. It did not gutter at the tip the way most people's did.

The clerk counted to thirty. Then forty. Then sixty, which was the end of the test.

*Hold: 9.1.*

Then the frame. Kellan stepped inside the tall cage of wires, and the projection board over it rose into four columns. Rank climbed and climbed and stopped, steady, three rungs up.

*Rank: Fire.*

*First door: Ember.*

The applause this time was real. Kiva found she was clapping too.

Fire at seventeen. Most people did not reach Fire until their twenties, if they reached it at all. Fire was real output. Fire was the kind of fighter companies sent to an open rift. And he had done it with a Hold of nine and a Draw better than eight-tenths, which meant he was not just bright. He was careful. He burned less ash than almost anybody in the hall.

He came out of the frame and walked straight toward the queue, because the exit was past it. As he went by, he glanced at Kiva. Then he stopped.

"You're the three-door," he said.

"I'm Kiva."

"I know." He said it plainly, not unkindly. "Everyone knows. You've been read four years running."

"This is the fourth."

"Anything changed?"

"I don't know yet."

Kellan looked at her a moment longer. He had very dark, very steady eyes, and he did not seem to be in any hurry to stop using them.

"I hope they find a category for you," he said. "Honestly. Then we'll know what we're comparing."

He walked on. Kiva watched him go.

"Was that rude?" said Anwen.

"I don't think so." Kiva thought about it. "I think he meant it."

"That's worse."

"I know."

---

Anwen's reading took six minutes. She was hand-door and always had been. On the draw bench she held her palms an inch above the lamp-cell and lit it with a slow, warm, amber light that wavered a little at the start and then steadied. *Draw: 0.95.* At the hold posts she raised a ward the size of a dinner plate between her palms and kept it there, faintly shimmering, while a clerk tapped it with a padded rod. *Hold: 7.4.* In the frame, her rank column stopped two rungs up.

*Rank: Flame.*

*First door: Hand.*

Anwen walked out of the frame, wrote something on her list, and went to wait by the wall without looking at the gallery once.

"Two-forty-one," called the clerk.

Kiva went to the draw bench.

She could feel the gallery shift as she came up. It was not loud. It was like the change in a room when a cell starts to hum the wrong note, and everybody who knows cells looks up at once. She heard her name. She heard *three-door* and *Rowan* and *that's the one from the Old Yard*. She heard someone say *the disk*, and she kept her face very still.

The clerk at the bench was a young man with spectacles. He checked her number against his ledger and frowned.

"Which door do you want to read on?"

"Ember," said Kiva. "The standard is Ember first, then hand, then still. That's what they did last year."

"Right." He did not look happy. "Ember first."

She laid her left forearm on the plate.

The copper slab touched the steel, and the lamp-cell under it woke. She felt it the way she always felt cells, a faint warm hum in her wrist bones, like a cat purring through a wall. She knew this cell. Not this exact one, but this kind. Mara serviced a hundred of them a year for the municipal lamps. It had a three-ring induction coil and a standard Grade First charge, and somebody had tuned it a little cold.

She held the pattern.

The lamp lit. It lit a dull orange, which was the color of her Ember's light and always had been. It flickered. She steadied it. It held.

Then her hand door woke up to see what she was doing.

It did not ask. It never asked. It was like a dog that heard the cupboard open in another room. She felt it lift inside her, cool and close, reaching for the cell through her palm because the cell was right there and the hand door liked cells. And the instant it reached, the Ember door pushed back.

The heat-lines on her chest lit up.

Not visibly. Nobody else could see them under her shirt. But she felt all three at once, thin and hot, like wires drawn tight from her breastbone toward her shoulder. The lamp flared yellow. Then orange. Then it stuttered, and the needle on the wall jumped and swung.

She shut the hand door. It went, sulkily. The lamp settled.

At sixty seconds the clerk called time.

*Draw: 3.1.*

Somebody in the gallery laughed. Not cruelly. Just surprised. Three point one meant she had burned more than three times the ash a standard candidate would, for the same minute of light.

"It's the needle," Kiva said. "It sticks at the two. It reads high on the drop."

The clerk looked at the meter. Then at her. "It's not two. It's three."

"I know. I'm just saying it sticks."

He wrote down 3.1.

---

The hold posts were worse, and better, and then worse again.

At the Ember post, she raised her right fist and asked for Edge. The metal came off her cuff and ran over her knuckles, and formed the shape it had formed every single time for four years.

It was not a blade, exactly. It was a short, flat, square-ended bar about the length of her forearm, with an edge on one side and a blunt back on the other, like a cleaver someone had run over with a cart. It had no point. It had no curve. It was ugly in a way that made people politely look somewhere else.

She had tried for four years to make it into anything else. A knife. A short sword. A spear-point, once, which had lasted half a second and gone out. Every time she asked for a different shape, the metal hesitated, guttered, and fell back into the bar.

"Light," said the clerk.

The edge-light came on along the bar's one sharp side. Dull orange. It shivered at the corner. She held it. It guttered, caught, held.

*Nine,* counted the clerk. *Ten.* At eleven it went out.

*Hold (Edge): 3.0.*

At the hand post, she made a ward. It came up between her palms the size of a tea tray, faint and silvery, and the clerk tapped it with his rod, and it rang like a pan and held for six taps before it popped. *Hold (Ward): 2.6.*

At the still post, there was a steel arm on a spring. It pressed against your chest with a slowly increasing weight while you stood on a plate that measured how far you slid. Most hand-door and Ember-door candidates slid at once. Still-door candidates sank their weight and held.

Kiva stood on the plate. The arm came forward and touched her breastbone, right over the disk.

She did the thing she had done in the Old Yard. She stopped holding the rail.

Her feet went heavy. Her knees went soft and sure. The push went down through her into the plate as if the floor had agreed to take it for her. The arm pressed harder. The gauge climbed. She did not slide.

It was the one thing she could always do. She did not know why. She had never trained still-door properly, not in a school. She had trained it in Mara's shop, carrying crates of cells up the cellar stairs, and on the trams, standing without holding the rail because Mara said it was good for her balance.

*Hold (Stance): 6.2.*

Six point two was respectable. Six point two was a still-door candidate on a decent day.

The gallery murmured. She heard someone say *that's not bad* in a tone of surprise that she decided not to mind.

Then the frame.

She stepped into the tall cage of wires. The air inside it always felt faintly thick, like the air before a storm. The clerk at the instrument box threw his switches. Above her, the projection board woke.

Four columns.

Rank rose. It rose in the Ember band, one rung, and paused. Then the hand band beside it lit, and rose, and the Ember column dropped as if somebody had kicked it. Then the still band surged up past both of them.

And the heat-lines caught fire.

That was what it felt like. Three hot wires inside her, drawn from her breastbone toward her left shoulder, pulled tight. Three doors open at once, each pulling on the same blanket from a different side. Her breath went short. Her hands curled at her sides.

On the board, the columns would not hold. They traded places. They climbed and fell. The Rank total at the bottom flickered between *Ember* and *Flame* and a row of dashes. Under *First door*, the letters stuttered. *Emb— Han— Sti—*

"It's the rig," said the clerk.

"It's not the rig," said a woman's voice.

Kiva knew the voice. Everybody in the Compact knew it. She looked up through the wires and saw Ysra Crest walking across the floor from the assessors' table.

---

Ysra Crest was the Placement Chair of the Meridian Collegiate Circuit, which meant that every roster in every division of every school in the Compact passed across her desk before it was posted. She was a tall woman of about sixty, with iron-grey hair cut very short and a long, plain face. She wore Compact grey with no pins. She was Torch-rank, hand-door, a Wren House ward-holder before she had gone into administration. People said she had once held a ward across a broken water main for forty minutes while a crew got three children out from under it, and had walked into the hospital afterward on her own feet.

She did not look at the gallery. She looked at the board.

"Shut it down," she said.

The clerk threw the switch. The board went dark. Inside the frame, the thick air thinned. The three hot wires in Kiva's chest loosened, slowly, and lay down.

"Step out, please, Miss Rowan."

Kiva stepped out. Her legs were a little unsteady. She put her hand flat on her chest over her shirt and pressed, the way Mara had taught her, until the burning faded to an ache.

Ysra Crest picked up the clerk's ledger and read the morning's numbers. Draw 3.1. Hold 3.0, 2.6, 6.2. Rank: unsettled. First door: none recorded.

"Same as last year," she said.

"The still number's up," said Kiva. "It was five-eight last year."

Ysra looked at her over the ledger. It was not an unkind look. It was the look of a person checking a weight against a scale.

"So it is," she said. "Four tenths. Well done." She closed the ledger. "And your draw is down, from two-nine. And your Edge hold is the same. And when the frame wakes all three doors at once, the board cannot tell me what you are." She handed the ledger back to the clerk. "Classification deferred. Fourth year. Write it, please."

The clerk wrote.

"Come with me, Miss Rowan," said Ysra Crest. "There's someone who wants to argue with me, and I'd rather she did it with you in the room."

---

The someone was Orla Dane.

She was waiting in a small office off the main hall, sitting on the edge of a desk that was not hers, with her bad left leg stretched out straight in front of her and her stick leaning against the wall. She was a compact, weathered woman in her fifties, with a crooked nose and grey-blond hair tied back in a knot. She taught mixed-path method at the Crooked Line School. She had been a Home Line field officer for twenty years before her knee went. And she had been teaching Kiva in the evenings, quietly, three nights a week, for four years.

She stood up when they came in. Kiva saw her take in the way Kiva was holding her chest, and saw her mouth tighten.

"The frame again," said Orla.

"The frame again," said Ysra. She sat behind the desk. "Sit down, both of you."

Kiva sat. Orla stayed standing.

"You know what I'm going to say," said Orla.

"I know what you're going to ask." Ysra folded her hands. "And you know what I'm going to answer. So let's say it for Miss Rowan's sake, because I don't think anyone has ever said it to her plainly." She turned to Kiva. "Do you know why you can't be placed?"

"Because I don't have a first door."

"That's what the board says. It isn't the reason." Ysra leaned back. "The Circuit is not a game, Miss Rowan. It looks like one from the gallery. Flags and standings and people cheering. But every roster I post decides what a young person is allowed to do with live ash, near other people, under pressure, in public. A first door tells me which rules to put around you. Which events you may enter. Which cells you may touch. Which teammates you can be trusted beside when something goes wrong."

"I can touch cells. I touch cells every day. My aunt—"

"Your aunt's shop filed an incident report three years ago," said Ysra quietly. "A cell ran hot under your hands and burned her palm. And last spring, in a training lane at this school, your Ember slab discharged when you tried to ward and stride at once. Nobody was hurt that time. The lane's sensing mast was."

Kiva felt her face go hot. She looked at the floor.

"I'm not saying that to shame you," said Ysra. "I'm saying it because those are the reasons. Not a board that can't spell. Three open doors that pull against each other, in a person who cannot yet stop them from doing it. And a set of rules built for people who have one." She paused. "Your rank is not the problem. If anything, it's too early to say whether your rank is a problem at all. Your control is the problem. Rank and control are different things. You know that."

"I know that."

"Good." Ysra looked at Orla. "Now you."

Orla took a breath. "The combined trial," she said. "The Cellhouse. Nine days from now. Candidates without a standard placement can be entered on a lecturer's request. The board rules on the result. I want her in it."

"You want her in it," said Ysra. "On a lecturer's request."

"Yes."

"Not a sponsorship."

Orla's jaw set. "I'm a lecturer. I don't have sponsoring standing. You know that."

"You could have it," said Ysra. "A senior lecturer with twenty years of Home Line field record can apply to sponsor any candidate she likes. It takes one form and a hearing. And then her name goes on the roster beside the candidate's, and if the candidate burns down a cellhouse, her name goes on the incident report too." She looked at Orla steadily. "You've had four years. You haven't applied."

There was a short silence. Kiva looked from one woman to the other. She had never seen Orla Dane lose an argument. She had never seen her not have the next sentence ready.

Orla did not have it now.

"Request granted," said Ysra at last. She pulled a form from a drawer and began to fill it in. "Miss Rowan enters the combined placement trial at the Cellhouse on the twelfth. Team assignment by the board. Ruling by the board." She wrote for a while. "Two conditions. One. A candidate under eighteen who has an ash-incident record needs her guardian's countersignature to enter any trial involving live cells. Mara Rowan signs, or Miss Rowan does not run." She signed the form and turned it around. "Two. Miss Rowan, if you wake more than one door in that trial, and anything goes wrong, you will be withdrawn and your file will be closed for the season. Do you understand?"

Kiva looked at the form. Her name was on it. Under *Supervising lecturer*, it said *O. Dane*. Under *Sponsor*, the line was empty.

"I understand," she said.

"Good luck, then," said Ysra Crest. "I mean that. I'd very much like to know what you are."

---

Anwen and Perrin were waiting on the Compact steps.

The steps ran down from the Measure Hall to the plaza in long white flights, and on Classification Day they were packed. Families sat in clumps eating lunch out of paper. Recruiters from the chartered rift companies stood under striped awnings at the bottom, each with a table, a banner, and a board of last season's standings. Kiva could see the Graywater banner, slate blue with a white wave on it. Saltmere's green. The black and gold of the Halden companies, which were not rift companies exactly, but which bought half the ash the rift companies brought home. Over the plaza hung the long anniversary banners the city had put up in the spring. *SIX HUNDRED YEARS. WE WROTE IT DOWN.*

Perrin Cade was sitting on the third step with a meat bun in each hand.

He was big. He was the biggest person Kiva knew who was still technically a teenager, broad and soft-looking in a way that was completely untrue, with sandy hair and a sunburned nose. He was still-door, Stonehand Hall, Flame rank and pushing at the top of it. He had been coming to Orla's Thursday-night lanes for a year, because Orla let anyone come, and he had decided on the second night that Kiva and Anwen were his friends and that there was nothing they could do about it.

He held out one of the buns.

"I got you lunch," he said. "Because I knew you'd be in a room. With important people. Being important."

Kiva took it. "It wasn't like that."

"It was a little like that." He bit into the other bun. "Ysra Crest. In a private room. Your own personal Orla Dane, arguing for you. I got a number on a board." He chewed. "It was a good number. Six-eight on hold. Flame, upper band. Nobody took me into a little room about it."

"Perrin," said Anwen.

"I'm not complaining. I'm observing." He swallowed. "I'm observing with my whole heart and a small amount of bitterness."

Kiva sat down beside him. The bun was hot and greasy and she had not eaten since six. She ate half of it before she said anything.

"Classification deferred," she said. "Fourth year. They're putting me in the Cellhouse trial. On Orla's request."

"Request?" said Anwen. "Not sponsorship?"

"Not sponsorship."

Anwen said nothing. She took out her list, wrote something on it, and folded it again.

"What did you write?" said Kiva.

"Nothing."

"Anwen."

"I wrote *ask Orla why.*" Anwen put the list away. "I drew the Cellhouse too. Field track. Afternoon slot. Graywater's taking four placements this year. They said four last year and took three. I asked them why." She looked out over the plaza. "They said one candidate dropped out. I asked who. They wouldn't tell me. I don't believe them."

"You think they'll do it again?"

"I think they'll take three again if three are good enough. And I intend to be one of the three." She glanced sideways at Kiva. "I'm not running for you, in that trial. You know that? If they put us on the same team, I'm running for the field track. I'll help. I'm not carrying you."

"I know."

"I just want it said."

"It's said," said Kiva. And then, because Anwen was still looking at her, "Good. I don't want to be carried."

Perrin finished his bun and licked his fingers. "I drew the Cellhouse," he said. "Morning slot. If I score above the line, I get the Hall bursary. If I don't, I go back to the tram yards with my father and fix brake shoes for the rest of my life. Which is a fine life. My father is very happy. He tells me so every morning while he fixes the brake shoes." He looked at the crowd. "Six kids in our flat. The bursary's the only way any of us gets a Compact place. So I'll be thinking about that. Instead of you."

"Good," said Kiva.

"Good," agreed Perrin. Then he bumped her shoulder with his, quite hard, the way he did when he was finished being honest and wanted to be friends again. "You'll be fine. You've got the disk."

Kiva stopped chewing.

"Don't," she said.

"What? Everybody knows you've got it."

"It doesn't do anything."

"Right, but it's *old.* It's Home Line. Somebody picked you." He shrugged. "Nobody ever picked me for anything. I'm only saying."

Kiva put her hand flat against her chest, over the disk, without meaning to. It was cold. It was always cold. In four years it had never once been warm, not when she slept with it, not when she held it in her fist, not on the worst nights when she woke up with the heat-lines burning and the pieces of the dream sliding away from her in the dark. It did not hum like a cell. It did not answer like a door. It was a cold dark circle with nine grooves cut in it, and she did not know what it was for.

She knew what people thought it was for.

She sat on the step and watched the recruiters under their striped awnings, the Graywater blue and the Saltmere green and the Halden black and gold, and the Fire-rank boy with the steady eyes shaking hands at every table. And she decided something she had been circling around for most of a year.

She was not going to wait any longer for a board to tell her what she was. She was going to go into the Cellhouse and do something that a board could count.

"I'm going home," she said, and stood up.

---

The Eastbank tram was crowded and warm and smelled of other people's lunches.

Kiva stood without holding the rail. She did it without thinking now. The car swayed through the Compact Quarter, past the white arenas and the glass-fronted lecture halls, and over the river on the long iron bridge. Below, the water was brown with spring. Above, on the flight lanes, the lift craft moved in their slow lines toward the station, their rings glinting blue-white in the sun. A Saltmere boat. A medical lifter. A long freight hauler with a Halden stripe.

At the front of the car, over the driver's seat, hung the car's cell meter. The needle sat at three-quarters. Every tram in Meridian ran on ash induction, from cells charged at the Eastbank core and tuned every morning by the yard Kindled. Kiva had once sat with Mara for a whole afternoon while she rebuilt one of those meters from a box of parts. She knew every jewel and spring inside it.

She watched the needle all the way across the river. It did not stick.

The tram let her off at Coper Street. The shop was halfway down, between a laundry and a bakery. The sign over the door said *ROWAN INSTRUMENTS & RECORDS* in faded gold. In the window, a row of repaired meters sat on a shelf with paper tags tied to them, waiting for the municipal van.

Through the glass, she could see Mara at the bench.

Her aunt was bent over a district pressure gauge with its face off, working on something inside it with a pair of fine tweezers. Her reading glasses were pushed up into her hair. Beside her elbow, a stack of paper ledgers sat under a brass weight, and a cup of tea had gone cold without being drunk. The light from the bench lamp lay across her right hand.

Kiva could see the scar from the door. It ran across the palm and up the inside of the wrist, shiny and pale and slightly puckered. Mara never hid it. She had never once, in three years, said a word about it.

The form was in Kiva's bag. *Guardian's countersignature.*

She stood on the step with her hand on the door and did not open it.

Inside, Mara looked up. She saw Kiva through the glass. She put down the tweezers, pulled off her glasses, and reached under the ledgers.

She drew out a long envelope with the Compact seal on it, already opened, and laid it flat on the bench.

Kiva went in. The bell over the door rang.

"They sent me a copy," said Mara. "By the noon post. Very efficient, the Compact." She did not look at the envelope. She looked at Kiva. "Fourth year?"

"Fourth year."

"And this trial."

"The Cellhouse. Nine days." Kiva took a breath. "Orla asked for it. Ysra Crest granted it. It needs you to sign."

Mara was quiet for a long moment. In the back of the shop, a cell on the test rack ticked as it cooled. Out in the street, a lift-truck rolled by.

"No," said Mara.

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-02.md

# Chapter 2 — Kiva: A Cell Keeps What You Leave in It

"No," said Mara again, because Kiva had not moved. "I'm not signing it."

Kiva stood in the middle of the shop with her bag still on her shoulder. She had known it was coming. She had known since the noon post, probably, though she had not known about the noon post. She had known since Ysra Crest said the word *countersignature*. Knowing did not help.

"You haven't even read it," she said.

"I read it twice." Mara tapped the envelope with one finger. "It's a live-cell trial. Rescue problem, mock substation, real Grade Second charge in the cell. A bottle-rift on a chute with spawn released on a timer. Candidates in teams of four, assigned by the board." She looked up. "I know what the Cellhouse is, Kiva. I fixed the meters in it for eleven years."

"Then you know it's safe. They've run it for thirty years."

"I know it's safe for people with one door."

That landed where Mara meant it to. Kiva felt it go in under her ribs.

"That's not fair," she said.

"No. It isn't." Mara pushed her reading glasses back up into her hair. Her voice did not rise. It never rose. "None of this is fair. It wasn't fair four years ago in the Old Yard, and it wasn't fair three years ago at that bench, and it isn't fair now. But I'm not signing a form because it isn't fair. I'm signing forms because they're true or I'm not signing them."

"What isn't true about it?"

Mara held up her right hand.

She did not do it to make a point. Kiva could tell. She did it the way she would hold up a gauge to the light to show you where the crack was. The scar ran across her palm from the base of the first finger to the heel of the hand, and up the inside of her wrist, shiny and pink-white.

"That," said Mara. "That's true."

"That was three years ago."

"Has anything changed?"

Kiva opened her mouth. She thought of the draw bench this morning. The lamp flaring yellow when the hand door reached in. The needle swinging. The three hot wires in her chest. *Draw: 3.1.*

She shut her mouth.

"Put your bag down," said Mara, more gently. "Come here. I want to show you something."

---

The thing on the bench was a district regulator.

It was a squat steel drum about the size of a soup pot, with a gauge on the front and four heavy bolts around the lid. It had a paper tag tied to one handle. *Tallis St. sub. Runs fast. Hot on load. Check.* Under that, in a different hand: *Third time this year.*

"Municipal van brought it at ten," said Mara. "The substation at Tallis Street has been tripping its breakers every evening at the peak. The yard crew swapped the cell twice. It keeps happening. So they sent the regulator to me."

"Is the cell bad?"

"The cell is fine. It's Grade First. I tested it." Mara put on her glasses and began to undo the lid bolts with a short wrench. "Open the book for me."

The book was the regulator's cell book. Every serious ash device in Meridian had one, by law, and it was paper. Not a tape, not a slate, not a number in a public ledger somewhere. Paper, in a sealed sleeve, chained to the device itself or kept with it. It had been that way for six hundred years. The first thing every child in the city learned about the Fall, after the bombs and the dark, was that paper had still worked in the morning.

Kiva opened it on the bench. It was half full of entries in a dozen hands. Date. Operator. Cell grade. Charge. Tune. Hours. Meter reading at start and end. Shutdowns. Faults. And at the right edge, a narrow column for notes.

"Read me the last three tunes," said Mara.

"Fourth of this month, operator Brannock, Grade First, standard charge, tune at oh-six-forty. Eleventh, same operator, same everything, tune at oh-six-fifteen. Eighteenth, same operator, oh-five-fifty." Kiva looked up. "He's coming in earlier every week."

"He is," said Mara. "And?"

Kiva looked at the note column. On the fourth, there was nothing. On the eleventh, in small, cramped letters: *Late for the core shift, quick tune.* On the eighteenth: *Short-staffed. Two subs before seven.*

"He's rushing," she said.

"He's rushing." Mara lifted the lid off the drum.

Inside, nested in a bed of grey felt, sat the cell. It was a sealed can of dull steel, ringed by three copper coils. A pair of heavy leads ran from the coils to the regulator's terminals. On the far side of the can, set into the drum wall, was a red handle with a wire seal on it, and below the handle, a small steel box with a hinged lid.

The quench.

Every cell had one. If a cell ran away, if its pattern drifted or its ash went bad or it started pulling more than its coils could carry, you broke the seal and pulled the red handle. The handle opened a gate in the bottom of the can. The ash dropped through the gate into the quench box, into a bed of wet salt that killed any pattern it touched. The cell went dead. The lights went out. Nobody got burned.

You were supposed to try everything else first. A quenched cell had to be refilled and retuned from nothing, and the ash in the quench box was ruined. But if it came to it, you pulled the handle. Every apprentice in Meridian learned that before they learned anything else.

"Put your hand on it," said Mara. "Your left. Just your hand door. Nothing else."

Kiva looked at her.

"I'm here," said Mara. "My hand's on the quench. Go on."

Kiva put her palm on the side of the can.

She felt the cell at once. She always did. It was like putting her hand on the side of a sleeping animal. Inside the steel, the ash lay packed and warm, and through it, holding it, ran the pattern somebody had pressed into it. The pattern was what made a cell work. Ash on its own was only fuel, warm and patient and ownerless. It did not want anything. It did not know how to do anything. A Kindled operator laid a pattern on it, a shape of intention, and the ash held that shape and released its warmth into the coils at the rate the pattern told it to.

And the ash kept holding the shape after the operator went away. That was the whole trick of it. That was why a cell could run a tram for a day, or a street of lamps for a week, without a Kindled person standing beside it.

This pattern was a good one. It was a standard municipal tune, even and clean. Kiva could feel the shape of it the way you can feel the shape of a key in the dark.

But it leaned.

It was faint. Under the clean shape, there was something else. A tightness. A sort of forward tilt, like a person standing on the edge of a step. The ash was releasing a little faster than the pattern meant it to. Not much. Enough. On a quiet afternoon it would not matter. At the evening peak, when every lamp and stove and pump on Tallis Street came on at once, it would pull harder and harder until the breakers tripped.

"It's in a hurry," said Kiva. She heard her own voice, surprised. "The tune. It's in a hurry."

Mara smiled. It was a small, satisfied smile, the one she kept for when a fault behaved exactly the way she had thought it would.

"That's Brannock," she said. "Two substations before seven. He's a good operator. He's never made a bad tune in his life. But he's tired, and he's late, and he's thinking about the next one while he does this one. So he leaves a little hurry in it. He doesn't mean to. He doesn't even know he's done it." She took her hand off the quench and laid it on the can beside Kiva's. "A cell keeps what you leave in it. That's the whole trade. The ash doesn't know the difference between what you meant and what you were feeling. It holds all of it."

Kiva took her hand away. She looked at the can.

"So if you were angry," she said slowly.

"It'd run hot. If you were frightened, it'd run jumpy. If you were three people at once—" Mara stopped.

Kiva looked at her.

"Go on," she said. "Say it."

Mara let out a breath. "If you were three people at once," she said, "it would try to hold all three. And it can't."

---

Kiva did not remember all of the day three years ago. She remembered pieces. She had been fourteen. It had been raining. A cell from the Linden Row pumping station had come in stuck, refusing to take a new tune, and Mara had been fighting it for an hour. Kiva had been sitting on the stool by the window doing her school sums and watching.

She remembered thinking she could feel what was wrong with it.

She remembered getting off the stool and putting her hand on the can while Mara was turned away for the wrench. Just her hand door. Just to see. And the stuck pattern had moved under her palm, like a knot coming loose, and she had felt a jump of pure delight.

Then the Ember door had woken up to see what she was doing.

She remembered the heat-lines lighting in her chest. She remembered the cell humming, then whining, then screaming. She remembered Mara's hand closing on her wrist and tearing her palm off the steel, and the smell, and Mara not making a sound.

Mara had pulled the quench with her left hand. The ash had dropped into the salt with a hiss. Then she had sat down on the floor of the shop with her right hand held against her chest and said, quite calmly, "Go next door and get Mrs. Pryce. Tell her to bring the burn salve and the cold cloths. Walk, don't run."

She had never said anything else about it. Not once.

"Was it my fault?" said Kiva now. It came out smaller than she meant.

Mara was quiet for a while. She was rebolting the regulator lid, one bolt at a time, in a star pattern so the lid would seat evenly.

"No," she said at last. "You were fourteen and you were trying to help. It wasn't nobody's fault either." She tightened the last bolt. "Cells don't care whose fault it is. That's what I'm trying to tell you. That's what I've been trying to tell you for three years, only I'm not good at telling. A cell doesn't care if you meant well. It holds what you put in it. If what you put in is three doors arguing, it'll hold the argument."

"Then I won't touch a cell. In the trial. I'll take a different role."

"You don't pick your role in the Cellhouse. The board does. Then the team does. Then the problem does." Mara set down the wrench. "And when the problem goes wrong, and it will, because that's what it's for, you won't get to pick anything. You'll do what you do when it goes wrong. And I've seen what you do."

Kiva stared at the bench.

"My sister did everything right," said Mara.

It was so quiet that Kiva almost did not hear it.

She looked up. Mara was not looking at her. She was looking at the regulator, at the red quench handle with its wire seal.

"Ione did everything right," Mara said. "She was the best substation tech on the Eastbank grid. She'd have spotted Brannock's hurry from across a room. And the night the Tallow substation went, she did every single thing the book says. She called it. She cleared the crew. She went for the quench." Mara's left thumb moved over the scar on her right palm, back and forth. "The automatic had failed. Somebody had sold that substation cut ash stamped First, and it ran hot on one side and warped the gate. She had to pull it by hand. She got it. The cell died. The fire didn't."

Kiva had been six. She remembered the knock on the door. She remembered Ilse Corran on the stairs. She did not remember much else.

"So when you ask me to sign a form," said Mara, "that says my girl can go and stand next to a live cell in a mock substation while things go wrong on purpose, and when the woman who wants her there won't even put her own name on it—" She stopped. She picked up her cold tea, looked at it, and put it down again. "I'm not saying never. I want you to hear that. I'm saying not this. Not nine days from now. Not like this."

The bell over the door rang. A woman came in with a broken kitchen meter in a cloth bag and stood politely by the counter.

Mara stood up and took off her glasses.

"Go upstairs," she said. "I'll be up for supper."

---

The shop closed at seven. Mara did not go up for supper.

She sat at the bench under the lamp with the day's ledgers and a pencil. That was the other half of the work. Rowan Instruments repaired the city's meters and gauges, but it also reconciled them. Every week the municipal van brought her the paper cell books from forty devices, and every week she checked each one against the tape from its meter, line by line, looking for the places where the two did not agree.

Mostly they agreed. When they did not, it meant a tired operator had written a number wrong, or a meter had drifted, or somebody had sold the city cut ash and stamped it First and hoped nobody would notice. Mara noticed. The city paid her to notice. It was not a large amount of money, but it was steady, and it kept the shop open, and it kept a roof over the girl upstairs.

The countersignature form lay at the edge of the bench where she could see it.

Under it lay another letter. That one had come in the winter, from the Municipal Works Licensing Office, on heavy paper with a red stripe across the top. Mara had not shown it to Kiva. She took it out now and read it again, though she knew what it said.

*Grade Two Tuning License, M. Rowan. Household condition noted, 2677. One further incident filing within the license household will trigger automatic review.*

Automatic review meant her license suspended for a season while they looked at her books. No license, no municipal contract. No contract, no shop.

She had told no one. Not Kiva. Not Orla Dane, who had come to this shop four years ago with her stick and her crooked nose and asked if she could teach the girl in the evenings. Mara had said yes, on one condition. No cells. Orla had kept to it. Mara had to give her that.

She put the license letter away.

Then she sat for a long time with her hands flat on the bench, looking at nothing.

What she saw, when she looked at nothing, was the Old Yard. Not the doors. Not the water standing up out of the bowl, or the big assessor pushing and pushing and the girl not moving. Those had frightened her, but she had understood them. They were the kind of strange that had rules.

What she saw was the moment after the lamps went out. Kiva on the ground. Her eyes open and looking at something that was not there, very far away. And then the look on her face when she came back. Mara had seen that look before, once, on a man pulled out of a collapsed cell vault after nine hours in the dark. As if he had been somewhere very large, and could not get the size of it out of his eyes.

Kiva had never told her what she saw. Not properly. *People. Coming. The sky.* Pieces. Mara had stopped asking, because asking made the girl go still and careful in a way Mara hated.

She picked up the pencil and pulled the countersignature form toward her.

She did not sign it.

She turned it over and wrote on the back, in the neat square capitals she used for fault notes.

*METER.*

*SHUTDOWN PATH.*

*BOOK.*

She looked at the three words for a while. Then she put the form in her apron pocket, turned off the bench lamp, and went up to see whether the girl had eaten anything.

---

The municipal van came at seven the next morning, and Kiva went with it, because it was Thursday and Thursday was the north round.

She liked the north round. She liked it more than she would have told anyone. The van was old and slow and smelled of machine oil, and the driver, a silent man named Tobin who had worked for the city for thirty years, let her sit in the front. Mara rode in the back with the tool cases. They went out across the river and up through the old districts toward the north wall, stopping at every municipal device that was due for its quarterly check. Pump meters. Lamp boards. The big regulators at the substations. And on the edge of the city, where the old north yard had once been, the listening masts.

The listening masts stood along a road called the Sag Line. It ran along the top of an old earth berm on the north edge of Meridian, past warehouses and sheds and a long strip of allotment gardens. Six hundred years ago, before Meridian was a city, it had been the edge of a medical station's yard. People said the ground there had never quite settled. Rifts still opened along the Sag Line a little more often than anywhere else in the region. Not many. One or two a year. Enough that the city had put up masts.

They were tall steel poles with a cage of fine wire at the top, each wired to a box at its foot. Inside each box was a sensing cell, tuned by a Wren House operator to feel the pressure that built in the air before a rift opened. The box drew the pressure onto a slowly turning paper drum, a single inked line. When the line jumped, the box rang a bell at the nearest company station, and somebody came running.

Most of the time the line was flat.

Kiva stood at the foot of mast six with the box open while Mara read the drum. The morning was cold and bright. Out over the allotments, someone was burning weeds. A lift craft from the Graywater station on the Brickfields road went over low and loud, its rings whining, and Kiva looked up at it until it was gone.

"Look at this," said Mara.

She had the old drum rolls out of the box's storage drawer. Each covered a quarter. She laid four of them side by side on the box lid, weighting the corners with her wrenches. Last spring. Last summer. Last autumn. This winter.

"What am I looking at?" said Kiva.

"The baseline. The flat part. Where it sits when there's no rift."

Kiva looked. The inked line ran across each roll in a long, slightly wavering stripe. On last spring's roll it sat on the second grid mark. On last summer's, it sat a hair above the second mark. On last autumn's, a little more. On this winter's, it was almost halfway to the third.

"It's creeping up," she said.

"It's creeping up." Mara rolled the drums back up carefully, one by one. "Every mast on the Sag Line is doing it. Has been for years. The city's had this one recalibrated three times in ten years. Every time, the Wren House sends someone out to retune the sensing cell, and the baseline drops back to the second mark. And every time, it starts creeping again."

"So the mast's drifting?"

Mara put the rolls back in the drawer and shut it.

"The mast's fine," she said. "I've had it apart twice. The cell's clean. The drum's true. The ink's the same ink."

Kiva looked up at the wire cage at the top of the pole, black against the sky.

"Then what's drifting?"

"I don't know," said Mara. "Nobody's asked me. I just write down what the needle does." She closed the box, sealed it, and signed the date on the tag in her square capitals. "There's people at the Wren House who'll tell you it's the filter. People at the Halden Works who'll tell you it's the masts. People on the street who'll tell you it's the six hundredth year and the sky's going to fall in at the anniversary." She shrugged. "That's three stories. I've got four drum rolls. I know which I trust more."

She wrote in the mast's cell book. Date. Device. *Baseline elevated, consistent with prior quarters. Cell clean. Drum true. No fault found.* And in the narrow column at the right: *Recal. would mask trend. Not recalibrated.*

"You're not supposed to do that," said Kiva, reading over her shoulder. "The book says recalibrate if the baseline's off."

"The book says recalibrate if the *mast* is off." Mara closed it. "The mast's not off. Something else is. If I make the line go flat again, nobody sees it." She handed the book to Kiva to put back in its sleeve. "That's the job. Write down what's there. Not what's supposed to be."

They climbed back into the van. Tobin started it without a word. As they pulled away, Kiva looked back at the masts standing in their line along the old berm, each with its little cage of wire, each patiently drawing its line on its slow paper drum.

She thought about a pressure building in the air before a rift opened. She thought about a sky with nothing in it.

She did not say anything about that. She never did.

---

Orla Dane came to the shop at six that evening, and Mara made her stand.

"You can come in," said Mara, holding the door. "But I've only got the one stool, and I'm sitting on it. I'd like you to be a little uncomfortable."

"That's fair," said Orla. She came in leaning on her stick and stood by the counter with her bad leg straight. She looked around the shop, at the meters on the shelves, the gauges in their tagged rows, the stacks of paper ledgers. Her eyes stopped on the regulator from Tallis Street, now standing by the door with a fresh tag. *Retuned M.R. Clean. Advise operator re: rest.*

"You found Brannock's hurry," said Orla.

Mara's eyebrows rose a fraction. "You know Brannock?"

"I taught him. Twelve years ago. He was always in a hurry." Orla looked at Kiva, who was sitting on the stairs. "Did she find it too?"

"She found it first."

Orla nodded slowly. She did not look surprised. She looked like a woman putting a number in a column.

"Kiva," said Mara, without turning around. "Go upstairs."

"No," said Orla. "Let her stay. She's the one you're deciding about. She ought to hear it."

Mara turned on her stool and looked at Orla for a long moment. Then she looked at Kiva on the stairs.

"Stay, then," she said. "But you don't talk."

Kiva stayed. She did not talk.

"You want her in the Cellhouse," said Mara.

"Yes."

"With live cells."

"It's a Grade Second charge in a sealed can with a tested quench and three assessors on the gallery with a master stop. The Wren House has run it for thirty years. It's the safest live-cell problem in the region."

"For people with one door."

"For people with one door," agreed Orla. "Yes. Nobody's run it with three. Nobody's run anything with three."

"Then why are you asking?"

Orla shifted her weight off her bad leg. She did not answer at once. When she did, she spoke carefully, as if she were laying out tools on a cloth.

"Because she's seventeen," she said. "Because if she doesn't place this season, she doesn't get a Compact place. Because without a Compact place she can't train in a sanctioned lane, which means she trains with me at night in a yard that isn't supposed to be used after dark, or she doesn't train at all. And if she doesn't train, those three doors don't go away. They keep opening. They keep arguing. And one day they'll argue at the wrong time near the wrong cell, with nobody there who knows what she is." Orla looked at Mara. "I'd rather it happened in the Cellhouse. With a quench and a master stop and three assessors watching. Than in your shop."

Mara's left thumb moved over the scar on her right palm.

"And your name?" she said. "On the sponsor line?"

The shop was very quiet.

"You asked for her," said Mara. "You didn't sponsor her. Ysra Crest made a point of it, I hear. So I'm asking too. If you think she's ready to stand next to a live cell, why won't you stand next to her?"

Orla did not look away. But it took her a long time to answer.

"Because I've put my name next to a student's before," she said at last. "A long time ago. Somebody I thought I understood. And it went wrong. And what happened to my name afterward was nothing compared to what happened to her." She stopped. "That's all I'll say about it tonight. It's not a good reason. I know it's not. But it's the true one."

Kiva sat very still on the stairs.

Mara looked at Orla for a while longer. Then she reached into her apron pocket and took out the countersignature form, folded in half. She did not unfold it. She laid it on the counter between them, back side up, so that the three words in square capitals showed.

*METER.*

*SHUTDOWN PATH.*

*BOOK.*

"Every cell I sign into this city," said Mara, "has three things. A meter, so somebody can see what it's doing. A shutdown path, so somebody can stop it before it hurts anyone. And a book, so the next person knows what was done and why." She turned on her stool and looked at Kiva on the stairs. Her face was not hard. It was something worse. It was hopeful, and trying not to be. "Show me hers. All three. Before the twelfth. And I'll sign."

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-03.md

# Chapter 3 — Kiva: Sequence, Not Stack

The Old Yard was not supposed to be used after dark.

There was a sign on the gate that said so, in brass letters gone green. *CROOKED LINE SCHOOL. OLD YARD. CEREMONIAL USE ONLY. CLOSED AT SUNSET.* Orla Dane had a key to the gate, and a hand lamp, and a very loose idea of what *ceremonial* meant.

"Every time I teach something," she said, unlocking the padlock, "it's a ceremony. Ask anyone."

She swung the gate open with her stick and went in. Kiva followed with Anwen and Perrin behind her.

It was Friday, the day after the north round. Seven days to the Cellhouse. The yard was dark and cold and smelled of old earth. Orla hung her lamp on a hook on the first shed, and its yellow light spread down the long strip of packed ground, over the line of black practice posts, to the far end where the last post leaned out of the line and into the shadow.

Kiva had been here on Thursday nights for four years. She had been here on Mondays and Wednesdays too, when it was just her and Orla. She knew every rut in the ground. She knew which post had a nail sticking out of it at knee height and which shed roof dripped when it rained.

Tonight it felt different. Tonight there was a form folded in Mara's apron pocket with three words on the back of it.

"Right," said Orla. She lowered herself onto an upturned crate by the shed and stretched her bad leg out in front of her. "Your aunt is a better teacher than I am. Did you know that?"

"She doesn't teach," said Kiva.

"She just did. She told you exactly what you need to learn in seven days, in three words, on the back of a form." Orla held up one finger. "Meter. You need a way to see what your doors are doing before they do it." A second finger. "Shutdown path. You need to be able to close a door on purpose, cleanly, when it opens without being asked." A third. "Book. You need to write down what happened, every time, so the next attempt starts from what you know and not what you hope." She lowered her hand. "Every serious cell in this city has those three things. Your aunt wants you to be at least as safe as a street lamp. That's not too much to ask."

"It's in seven days," said Perrin.

"It is in seven days," agreed Orla. "So we'd better start."

---

She started with the thing Kiva already knew. She always did.

"Rank," said Orla. "Tell me what it is."

"How much of you is lit."

"And Hold?"

"Control. How well you can manage what's lit."

"Those are the board's words. Give me yours."

Kiva thought about it. She thought about the shop, and the cells on their racks, and the flat above it with its six rooms.

"Rank is how many rooms in the house have the lamps on," she said. "Hold is whether you can carry a lamp from one room to another without dropping it."

Orla smiled. It made her crooked nose more crooked. "Good. And you?"

"I've got three rooms lit," said Kiva slowly. "And every time I try to carry one lamp, I try to carry all three. And I drop them."

"That's it. That's your whole problem, in one sentence." Orla leaned forward on her stick. "Your rank isn't small, Kiva. The board can't read it properly, but it isn't small. Three doors open at thirteen is three doors. That's more light than most people in this city will ever have. But the board doesn't post you on light. It posts you on what you can carry. And you can't carry anything yet without the other two lamps jumping into your hands."

"I know."

"Then here's what you don't know. Or what you know and won't do." Orla put her stick across her knees. "You keep trying to use them together. Ward and Edge at once. Stride and stance at once. You think if you could just stack them, you'd be strong. You'd be faster than Kellan Renn and steadier than Perrin and you'd ward like Anwen while you did it." She shook her head. "That's not a skill you have. It might be one day. It isn't now. Right now, every time you stack, they fight."

"So what do I do?"

"Sequence," said Orla. "Not stack. One door. Close it. Next door. Close it. Next." She tapped the ground with her stick three times, slowly. "It'll be slower. It'll look stupid. People will laugh. It's still better than a cell screaming in your hands."

Kiva looked at the ground where the stick had tapped. Three marks in the dirt.

She had heard this before. Orla had been saying some version of it for two years. *One at a time. Slower. Clean before fast.* And Kiva had tried, and it had been slow, and it had looked stupid, and in every sparring bout she had ever fought, somebody faster with one good door had walked straight through the gap while she was still closing the first.

So she had gone back to trying to stack. Because stacking, when it worked for half a second, felt like flying.

"You're thinking about the gap," said Orla.

Kiva looked up.

"You're thinking that the gap between one door and the next is where everybody hits you." Orla nodded. "It is. It's going to be. That's the price. But you can't make the gap smaller by pretending it isn't there. You make it smaller by closing cleaner. And you can't close clean until you can see what's opening." She leaned back. "Which brings us to your aunt's first word."

---

"You've had a meter all along," said Orla. "You just don't read it."

She pointed her stick at Kiva's chest.

Kiva put her hand there without thinking. Under her shirt, under the disk, the three heat-lines lay quiet.

"They light up when the doors compete," said Orla. "Yes? Every time. In the frame. At the draw bench. That night in the lane when your slab discharged and took the mast out."

"Yes."

"Do they all light at once?"

Kiva opened her mouth to say yes. Then she stopped.

She thought about the draw bench yesterday. The lamp lit orange. The hand door waking up to look. And then the heat. But it had not been all three at once. There had been a moment, very short, when it was only one. The bottom line, the one nearest her breastbone. It had gone warm first. Then the second. Then the third, at her shoulder, and then the lamp had flared.

"No," she said slowly. "The bottom one goes first."

"How long before the next?"

"I don't know. Not long. Half a breath?"

"Half a breath," said Orla, "is a long time. You can do a lot in half a breath. You can close a door in half a breath, if you see it coming." She pointed at Anwen. "Anwen. Stand there, please. About a pace off her left side. Have a ward ready, but don't raise it unless I tell you."

Anwen went and stood where she was told. She held her hands loosely in front of her, palms in, not quite touching.

"Perrin. Behind her. Hand flat between her shoulders. If her stance goes, I want you to feel it before she does."

Perrin moved behind Kiva and put his big warm palm on her back. "I'm feeling it," he said. "I'm very present."

"Kiva," said Orla. "Edge. Just Edge. Hold it. Don't light it. And the moment you feel that bottom line go warm, before anything else happens, you say *one*. Out loud. That's all. You don't have to stop it. You just have to say it."

Kiva raised her right fist and asked for Edge.

The metal came off the copper slab on her left forearm and flowed across to her right hand the way it always did, as if it were going downhill. It ran over her knuckles and closed into the bar. Short, flat, square-ended. Ugly. She held it out in front of her, dark.

Nothing happened. She held it. The yard was quiet. Somewhere out in the city a tram bell rang.

Anwen shifted her weight beside her.

And the hand door woke up.

Kiva felt it lift, cool and curious, toward Anwen. Toward the faint shimmer of the ward Anwen was holding ready between her palms. The hand door liked wards the way it liked cells. It wanted to see.

And the bottom line went warm. Then the second.

"One," said Kiva. Too late. The second line was already hot, and the third was starting, and the bar on her fist jerked as if someone had bumped it.

"That was two," said Orla. "Again."

They did it again. Kiva held the dark bar. Anwen stood with her ward ready. Perrin breathed slowly behind her. This time Kiva did not look at the bar. She did not look at anything. She listened to her chest the way Mara listened to a cell.

The hand door lifted.

The bottom line warmed.

"One," said Kiva.

The second line warmed a moment later. But she had said it first. Before. She had felt the first wire go hot and named it before the second.

"That's one," said Orla. "Good. Again."

They did it eleven times. She got it early on the second try, and late on the third, and late on the fourth, and early on the fifth and sixth. By the tenth her chest ached the way it did after a long day at the shop, a dull, used ache. By the eleventh she could feel the bottom line move before the hand door had quite finished waking up. It was like hearing the click in a tram's brakes before the car begins to slow.

"That's your meter," said Orla. "It's always been there. You've just been reading it after the needle's already swung."

Kiva let the bar fall back into the slab. Her right hand was shaking a little.

"Is it always going to hurt?"

"I don't know," said Orla honestly. "Probably, while they're fighting. Maybe less when they stop." She shifted her leg. "Rest five minutes. Then we'll learn how to close."

---

Closing was harder.

Each door shut differently, and Orla could only teach one of them from the inside. She was Ember-door. She had been Ember-door for forty years, Torch rank, which was as far as one door went, and she had used it on a hundred rifts with the Home Line before her knee gave out. She could feel Edge. She could not feel a ward or a stance any more than she could feel someone else's toothache.

So she made the others teach.

"Ember first," she said. "I know that one. Form the bar."

Kiva formed it.

"Now let it go."

Kiva let it go. The metal ran back over her fist and into the slab.

"No," said Orla. "You dropped it. Do it again, and this time put it down."

"What's the difference?"

"When you drop it, it goes back to the slab, but you're still holding the door open behind it. The door's still awake. It's just got nothing in its hands. So it looks around for something else to hold." Orla held up her own right fist. Her Ember was a dark iron-grey slab on a worn steel cuff. The metal ran from it over her knuckles and closed into a short, broad, leaf-shaped blade, old-fashioned and very plain. She held it. Then the metal ran back. It did not hurry. It went back into the cuff the way water goes back into a bucket when you tip it gently, and then, after it was gone, Orla let out a slow breath through her nose.

"There," she said. "Did you see that?"

"You breathed out."

"After. After it was home. That's when I shut the door behind it. Not while it's going. After." She looked at Kiva. "You've been closing the door on the metal while it's still in the doorway. That's why it catches."

Kiva formed the bar. She let the metal run back. She waited until it was all the way home in the slab, the copper warm and heavy on her forearm. Then she breathed out and imagined a door closing.

It closed. She felt it go quiet, like a cell powering down.

"Better," said Orla. "Anwen. Your turn."

Anwen came and stood in front of Kiva. She did not like teaching. She had told Kiva so many times. She said it was like being asked to explain how you walk. But she did it the way she did everything, plainly and thoroughly, as if she were writing it down.

"You make a ward like a tray," she said.

"I make a ward like a ward."

"You make it like a tray. Flat. Out in front of you. With your arms stiff." Anwen raised her own hands, and a ward came up between them, faintly silver, the size of a dinner plate. It was not flat. It curved a little, like the side of a bowl. "A ward's not a thing you hold. It's a door you hold shut. You lean on it. And when you want to put it down, you don't drop it. You fold it." She brought her palms slowly together. The silver curve folded in on itself like a closing hand and was gone. "If you drop it, it splashes."

"Splashes?"

"Everything you were holding back comes through at once. Like taking your thumb off a hose." Anwen looked at her. "You splash every time. That's why the hand door doesn't close after. It's still got water all over it."

Kiva raised a ward. It came up between her palms, tray-sized and flat.

"Curve it," said Anwen.

She tried. It wobbled. It curved, a little.

"Now fold."

She brought her palms together. The ward folded halfway, and then the hand door, startled, let go of the rest of it all at once. There was a soft pop, and a puff of cool air in her face.

"Splash," said Anwen. "Again."

The fourth time, it folded. Not neatly. But it folded, and the hand door closed after it without reaching for anything else, and Kiva felt it go quiet.

"Perrin," said Orla.

Perrin rubbed his hands together. "Right. Stance." He came around in front of Kiva. "You're good at stance. You're better at stance than you should be. But you never come out of it. I've watched you. You sink down into it like a bath, and then you just sort of stay there, and the still door's still open under you, holding the ground."

"So how do I get out?"

"You give the ground its weight back." He planted his feet. Kiva felt him do it. It was not visible, exactly. He did not move. But something about the way he stood changed, the way a bench changes when someone heavy sits on it. "You took it, when you went into stance. You borrowed it. So when you come out, you give it back. Lift your heels a tiny bit. Breathe in, not out. And sort of—" He made a face. "Say thank you."

"Say thank you?"

"To the ground. Not out loud. In your knees." He shrugged. "That's what my first teacher at the Hall told me. I thought it was stupid. Works, though."

She went into stance. She felt the heaviness come up through her feet and settle in her knees. Then she lifted her heels very slightly, and breathed in, and thought *thank you* at the packed earth of the Old Yard.

The still door eased. It did not close all the way. But it eased.

"Good," said Perrin. "Weird, isn't it?"

"Very."

"Now," said Orla, from her crate. "All together. Sequence. Edge. Close it properly. Then ward. Fold it. Then stance. Give it back." She tapped the ground three times with her stick. "Slowly. I don't care how long it takes."

---

The first time, it went wrong in the first gap.

Kiva formed the bar. She held it. She let the metal run back toward the slab, and she waited for it to get home, and she waited, and it was taking so long, and the ward was the next thing, and she could feel Anwen standing there, and she reached for the ward.

The metal was not home yet.

The bottom heat-line flashed. Then all three. The hand door opened straight into the Ember door's doorway. The last of the metal, still running back over her wrist, jerked and spat a hot orange spark from its edge, though she had never lit it. Her slab went hot on her arm, hot enough to hurt. Somewhere down the yard, on the shed at the far end, the lane's little sensing mast gave a sharp electric ping.

Anwen's ward came up between them before Kiva could blink. The spark hit it and went out.

Kiva stood very still, breathing hard. Her chest burned.

"What happened?" said Orla.

"I didn't wait."

"What else?"

"I was thinking about the next one. While I was closing the first one." She thought of the tag on the Tallis Street regulator. *Runs fast. Hot on load.* "I was in a hurry."

Orla said nothing. She did not need to.

"Again," said Kiva.

The second time, she waited. She let the metal run all the way home. She felt it settle in the copper. She breathed out and closed the door behind it, and felt it go quiet. And then she counted, deliberately, *one, two*, before she reached for anything else.

Then she raised the ward.

It came up flat. Tray-shaped. But it came up into an empty doorway, and nothing pulled against it. The bottom heat-line stayed cool. She curved the ward a little. She held it.

Then she folded it. It splashed a little at the end. But it folded.

She stood for a moment with her palms together. Two breaths. Then she went into stance.

The weight came up through her feet. She held it. She gave it back.

Then she stood in the dark yard with her hands at her sides, and her chest did not hurt at all.

"Eleven seconds," said Orla. "From Edge to the end of stance."

Perrin whistled. "That's a long time."

"It's a very long time," said Orla. "Kellan Renn would cut you in half three times in eleven seconds. Any Riftspawn bigger than a Handful would walk through you. You'd fail every timed lane in the Circuit." She looked at Kiva across the lamp-lit yard, and her face was very still. "And it's the first time in four years I've seen you go through all three doors without a single line lighting."

Kiva had not realized it until Orla said it. She put her hand to her chest. The lines were there, under her shirt. Quiet. Cool.

"So that's a sequence," said Perrin.

"No," said Orla. "That's a drill. It's three doors one after another, with nothing passing between them. Each one starts from standing still." She tapped the three marks in the dirt again. "A real sequence is when the first door hands its work to the next one without dropping it. The speed of a Stride going straight into a stance, so you stop where you mean to and nobody moves you. The stop of a ward going straight into a strike. The work carries across. Nobody I know can do that across doors. I've seen it written about. I've never seen it done." She looked at Kiva. "This is the drill that gets you there. If anything does."

She understood something then that Orla had been telling her for two years, and that she had not been able to hear. The gap was not the enemy. The gap was where the closing happened. If she rushed the gap, the doors fought. If she let the gap be as long as it needed to be, they did not. It was slow because it was clean. It would get faster only by getting cleaner first.

She had always thought it was the other way around.

"Again," she said.

---

At ten o'clock, Orla called a stop. She reached into the canvas bag at her feet and took out three small books.

They were plain field books, the kind the Home Line still issued to its officers, bound in waxed grey card with a pencil tied to the spine on a string. She handed one to Kiva. When Kiva opened it, she saw that every page had been ruled into seven columns.

*Exercise. What went wrong. Who was hurt. What it cost. How long to come back. Second go. Why.*

"Your aunt's third word," said Orla. "Book."

Kiva turned the pages. They were all blank. All ruled.

"You ruled these yourself?"

"Last night. My eyes aren't what they were. Some of the lines aren't straight."

"Where's this from? These columns?"
