"The Home Line case files. Field officers have kept them like this since before there was a Compact." Orla shrugged. "Some of the other schools use six columns now. The standard manual dropped the last one before I was born. The Home Line never did."

Kiva looked at the last column. *Why.*

Perrin was looking at the book in Kiva's hands. Then at the one Orla was holding out to Anwen. Kiva saw his face do something complicated.

"Can I have one?" he said.

It came out more sharply than he probably meant. Orla looked at him.

"You can have one," she said. "You could have had one last year. You didn't ask."

"I didn't know they were for asking."

"Everything's for asking." She took the third book out of the bag and held it out to him. "I made three. I thought you might."

Perrin took it. He turned it over in his big hands. For a moment he did not say anything at all, which was rare enough that Anwen looked up from her own book.

"Thanks," he said. And then, because he was Perrin and could not leave anything alone, "It's not a disk, but it's something."

"Perrin," said Anwen.

"I'm joking. Mostly." He put the book in his coat. Then he looked at Kiva, and his face was honest and a little ashamed. "I'm jealous. You know that. I've been jealous since I came here. You get the private nights and the old Home Line teacher and the mysterious necklace and Ysra Crest in a little room, and I get to stand behind you with my hand on your back." He shrugged. "It's not your fault. I just wanted it said. Like Anwen does."

"It's said," said Kiva.

"Good." He bumped her shoulder. Harder than usual. "Now write in your stupid book."

Kiva sat on the crate beside Orla and opened the book on her knee. The hand lamp threw its yellow light over the ruled page. She took the pencil off its string and wrote, slowly, in the first row.

*Exercise:* Sequence. Edge, close, ward, close, stance, close.

*What went wrong:* First try, rushed the gap. Reached for ward before metal home. All 3 lines lit. Spark. Mast pinged.

*Who was hurt:* Nobody. Anwen caught the spark.

*What it cost:* Slab burned my arm a bit. Lines ached ten min.

*How long to come back:* Ten min.

*Second go:* Waited. Counted two in each gap. No lines. 11 sec total. Too slow.

She stopped at the last column. *Why.*

She did not know why. She did not know why waiting worked. She did not know why the doors fought in the first place, or why there were three of them, or why a stance she had never trained was better than an Edge she had trained for four years. She sat with the pencil over the column for a long time.

"Leave it blank," said Orla quietly, beside her. "If you don't know. Blank's better than a lie. Somebody will fill it in later. Maybe you."

Kiva left it blank.

---

They walked the lane before they left. Orla always made them. It was a habit from the Home Line, she said. You walked the ground at the end of the night so that your feet would remember it.

The lane ran down the middle of the Old Yard between the two rows of practice posts. It was rutted and narrow and not quite level. They walked it in single file, Kiva first, then Perrin, then Anwen, with Orla bringing up the rear on her stick and the lamp swinging from her hand.

At the far end, the last post leaned.

Kiva stopped beside it. It was the same post. The same black, split wood, the same two iron bands. Four years ago she had put her hand on it after the lamps went out. She put her hand on it now.

"Why is it crooked?" she said.

She had asked Orla before. She had asked everyone. She asked the way you press on a bruise.

"The standard manual says *retained for ceremonial continuity*," said Orla.

"That's not a reason."

"No," said Orla. "It isn't."

Kiva looked at her. Orla was looking at the post, and there was something in her face that Kiva had not seen there before. Not quite sadness. Something more like a person looking at a page with a line torn out of it.

"There used to be one," said Orla. "A reason. It's not in the manual anymore."

"Do you know what it was?"

Orla was quiet for a moment. "Not for certain," she said. "And I won't guess at it in the dark." She lifted the lamp. "Go home. Get some sleep. Monday we'll do it again, and you'll do it in ten seconds, and you'll hate every one of them."

---

When they had gone, Orla Dane sat on the crate in the empty yard and let her knee hurt.

It hurt every evening now. Twenty years on the Home Line had done it. Twenty years of Stride, of landing hard on broken ground with the struts locked and the whole weight of a fall going through one joint. The healers at the Wren House had done what they could. She had another five years of teaching in it, maybe, before she needed the stick for everything.

She did not mind. Mostly.

She sat and looked down the dark lane at the crooked post.

She had told Mara Rowan the truth. She had put her name beside a student's once. Twenty-one years ago, when she was newly off the Home Line and full of herself, she had sponsored a twin-kindled boy from the Brickfields named Jessamy Hart. Ember and hand, both at thirteen. Not three doors. Two. It had seemed like enough of a miracle at the time.

She had thought she understood him. She had thought she could teach him the way she taught everyone, with patience and the old lane and her own field sense. She had put him into a Circuit ashcraft trial in his second year, because he had asked, and because he was ready, and because she wanted the board to see what she saw.

His doors had argued over a live cell in front of four thousand people.

The cell had run away. The quench had worked, but not before the backwash went up his arms. He had kept one hand. Two other students had been burned trying to pull him clear. The Compact board had closed his file and sealed the incident, and Jessamy Hart had left Meridian at nineteen with one hand and no category. Orla had heard, years later, that he was fixing pumps somewhere on the south coast. She had never written to him. She had not known what to say.

They had suspended her sponsoring standing for ten years. It had been restored for eleven. She had never used it.

She thought about that, and about the girl who had stood in this yard tonight and gone through all three doors in eleven seconds without a single line lighting.

Eleven seconds was nothing. Eleven seconds was a beginner's number, a number to be ashamed of in any sanctioned lane. And Orla had sat on this crate and watched it and felt something rise in her chest that she had not felt in twenty-one years.

She knew what it was. It was the same thing she had felt about Jessamy.

That was what frightened her.

She thought, too, about the disk. She had seen it before. Not on Kiva. On Ilse Corran, twenty-five years ago, at a field camp on the Ferren levee, the night after a bad rift. Ilse had taken it out from under her shirt and turned it over in her fingers by the fire and said nothing about it at all. Orla had known Ilse for six years by then, and had never asked. You did not ask a bearer. That was the oldest Home Line rule there was. The disk went by appointment, sealed, from one bearer to the next. It was not owned. It was carried.

Ilse had carried it for thirty years. And then, without a word to anyone, she had appointed a six-year-old girl whose mother had just died.

Orla did not know why. She had wondered about it for eleven years.

She knew one other thing, and she had told no one. Not Kiva. Not Mara. Not Ysra Crest.

Years ago, in the Compact Library, looking for something else, she had found a catalog card in the disputed drawer. *Annick, H. Personal notebook. Includes fever account ("the sky unroofed"). Status: disputed. Retained.* She had read the notebook in the reading room, out of curiosity, because Hesper Annick had been a famous name once. It was a Glory master's fever dream. People walking from every road at once. A sky with no roof. Every lamp in Meridian answering.

She had put it back and not thought about it again until four years ago, when every lamp in the Old Yard went out and a thirteen-year-old girl lay on the ground with her eyes open, looking at something very far away.

Kiva had never told her what she saw. Orla had never asked. She did not know if there was any connection at all. A fever was a fever. A Glory master's nightmares were not a map. She had no proof of anything, and she had been on the Home Line too long to mistake a feeling for a fact.

But she had not forgotten the card.

Orla looked at the post for a while longer. Then she got up, stiffly, and took the lamp off its hook, and locked the gate behind her.

In the bag over her shoulder, where it had been for four years, was a sponsorship application from the Compact office. She had filled in the first page the week after the Old Yard. She had never filled in the second.

She did not take it out. She walked home with it.

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-04.md

# Chapter 4 — Kiva: The Terrace Ring

By Tuesday night she could do it in nine and a half seconds.

She wrote that in the book. She wrote everything in the book now, in the seven ruled columns, in pencil that smudged when her hand sweated. Monday: ten seconds, then ten again, then twelve because she rushed the second gap and the bottom line lit and she had to stop and wait for it to cool. Tuesday: ten, nine and a half, nine and a half, and then a long, embarrassing fourteen, because Perrin had sneezed in the middle of her stance and she had lost the whole thing laughing.

*What went wrong:* Perrin.

*Who was hurt:* My dignity.

*Why:* Perrin.

Orla had read that page and snorted, and Anwen had read it and said, "That's not what the column's for," and written something in her own list.

Mara had not read any of it yet. Kiva had not offered. The book sat on the kitchen table every night while they ate supper, closed, with the pencil on its string lying across the cover, and neither of them mentioned it.

On Wednesday morning, Orla said, "Open lane today. Stonehand Hall. I've put your name down."

Kiva put down her toast.

"You can't go into the Cellhouse having only done this in an empty yard with your friends," said Orla. "Your aunt's not going to sign for a shutdown path that's only ever been tested in the dark. You need to do it with someone trying to hit you."

"Who?"

"The draw decides," said Orla. "Unless somebody asks for you."

---

Somebody asked for her.

Kiva found out when she got to Stonehand Hall at noon and saw the board. The open lane was a Circuit tradition for the last week before placement trials. Anyone who was going into a trial could come and fight anyone else who was going into a trial, under a Circuit referee, in one of Stonehand's sanctioned rings. The bouts were logged. The numbers went into your file. The gallery was free, and in placement week it was usually full, because recruiters liked to watch people fight when it did not count yet. They said you saw more.

The board was a long slate wall outside the rings, chalked with names in pairs. Most of the names had been drawn by lot. A few had a small star beside them, which meant one of the pair had requested the other.

*Ring Three, 2nd bout: K. Renn ★ — K. Rowan.*

"He asked for you," said Perrin. He was standing beside her with his hands in his pockets and a bruise already coming up on his cheekbone. He had fought his own bout an hour earlier, against a Saltmere-sponsored Ember-door girl, and won it three to two. "He asked for you on purpose. In front of everybody. That's either very rude or very flattering, and I can't work out which."

"Both," said Anwen. She was reading the board with her list in her hand. "He wants to know what he's comparing. He said so on Monday. Now he's measuring."

"I'm going to lose," said Kiva.

"Probably," agreed Anwen. "So decide what you want to learn while you're doing it."

---

The Terrace Ring was the oldest of Stonehand Hall's practice rings, and the one Kiva liked least.

It was round and sunken, like a bowl cut into the floor of a great stone hall. Around the top ran a broad stone terrace with a rail and benches behind it for the gallery. From the terrace, three wide steps went down to the floor of the ring, each step knee-high and about a pace and a half deep, all the way around. The floor at the bottom was sand. Coarse, pale, raked smooth before every bout, about twelve paces across.

Stonehand built it that way on purpose. Their whole discipline was the body: stance, contact, load, footing. They did not believe in flat ground. Flat ground, they said, taught you nothing about falling.

The rules were simple. Both fighters began on the top terrace, on opposite sides. Edge-light stayed dark. A dark Edge was blunt metal, not a blade, and the referee would stop you if you lit it. A clean contact on the body or head counted a point. A contact on an arm or leg counted half. Three points won. Any door was allowed. A sensing mast stood on the rim of the ring, a short steel pole with a cage of wire on top, wired to a draw board where the referee could see it. Before the bout, each fighter settled a single standard dram of clean ash into their reserve. The draw board measured how much of it they spent. It went into the file.

Kiva sat on the bench at the top of the terrace and settled her dram.

It came in a small sealed glass vial. She broke the seal and tipped the grey powder into her left palm. It was fine and faintly warm, like flour from a mill. She closed her hand around it and let it go in. That was the only way to describe it. The warmth went into her palm and up her wrist and spread into her chest, and the ash in her hand went pale, then dull, then dead. It was like drinking something hot on a cold morning. It settled. It filled the bottom of something inside her that she could feel but not name.

Across the ring, Kellan Renn did the same. He did not close his eyes. He just looked at his hand until the ash in it went dead, and then brushed it off onto the stone.

The referee was a Stonehand master, a short, heavy woman with grey hair in a braid and a voice like a dropped crate. "Renn," she called. "Rowan. To your marks."

Kiva stood up and walked to the edge of the terrace.

From up here, the ring looked very deep. The three steps went down like the sides of a bowl. The sand at the bottom was pale and perfect. On the other side, twenty paces away, Kellan stood at his mark with his hands loose at his sides and his Ember cuff dark on his left forearm.

The gallery was full. Kiva could see Perrin and Anwen at the rail. She could see Orla on a bench at the back, her stick across her knees. Further along, under a slate-blue banner, she could see two people in Graywater coats, a man and a woman, sitting with their arms folded.

She looked away from them.

*What do you want to learn?*

She knew what she wanted. She wanted to win. She wanted to stand in the middle of that sand and be unmovable and let him break himself on her.

She had a plan for that. She had been thinking about it since the board.

"Fighters ready," said the referee. "Begin."

---

Kiva went down the steps fast.

She did not try to Stride. She just went down them like stairs, three long drops, and landed on the sand in the middle of the ring. Then she planted her feet.

Stance came up through her the way it always did, heavy and certain, the floor agreeing to take her weight. At the same time she reached for Edge. The metal flowed off her slab and over her fist and closed into the bar.

Two doors. Stance and Edge. Stacked.

The bottom heat-line warmed. She ignored it. She held both. She stood in the middle of the sand, rooted, with the ugly bar raised in front of her, and waited for him to come down to her.

He did not come down.

Kellan walked to the edge of the top terrace and stepped down onto the second step. Only that far. He stood on it, a pace and a half above the sand, and looked at her. His blade had formed on his right fist while he walked, long and narrow and dark. He held it low.

She waited.

He waited.

It went on for a long time. Long enough for someone in the gallery to laugh. Kiva stood in the sand with both doors open and felt the bottom heat-line grow warmer, and then the second one begin. Holding two doors was not like holding one. It cost more. She could feel it draining out of the dram she had settled, a slow, steady pull, like water out of a cracked jug.

He was making her pay for standing there.

She understood that a moment too late. Then he moved.

He did not come straight down. He came along the second step, quick and light, circling to her left. She turned to follow him. Turning in stance was like turning a cart. Her feet were heavy. Her knees were heavy. She swung the bar around and he was still above her, on the step, and the step put his blade at the height of her head while hers was pointed at his knees.

He feinted down at her. She raised the bar to block.

And the hand door woke up.

It wanted a ward. She was blocking, and blocking was what the hand door was for, and it lifted inside her, cool and quick, reaching to help. She felt the bottom line go hot. Then the second. Then the third.

She said nothing. She had forgotten to say anything.

The ward came up in front of her face, flat and wobbly, at the same moment the bar came up and the stance held, and all three doors pulled on her from three directions at once. The bar jerked in her fist. The ward splashed. Cold air burst against her face. For one instant, she could not see.

Kellan's blade tapped her right shoulder. Lightly. A touch.

"Half," said the referee. "Renn."

He was already back on the step, two paces along, his blade low again.

Kiva stood in the sand and breathed. Her chest burned. All three lines were lit and throbbing. She let the ward go completely. She let the stance go too, badly, without giving anything back, and her knees nearly folded.

*Stupid,* she thought. *Stupid, stupid.*

She had stacked. She had told herself she was going to stack two doors, stance and Edge, and hold them. And the moment there was pressure, the third had come in too, because it always did, because she had never in four years been able to hold two without the third deciding to join. She had known that. She had planned around it. And it had happened anyway.

The step. He had used the step. He had made her look up at him, and made her turn heavy, and made her pay to hold two doors while he paid for nothing at all.

She looked up at him.

He was not smiling. He was watching her.

---

She decided to go up after him.

It was the wrong decision and she knew it while she was making it. She made it anyway, because she was angry, and because standing in the sand had cost her half a point and most of a dram and she did not know what else to do.

She still had the bar on her fist. She went at the first step at a run, and as she reached it, she asked for Stride.

Stride was the Ember's other shape. It was struts of metal down the backs of the calves and along the soles of the feet, for a burst of speed or a hard jump. Kiva could hold it for three steps, maybe four. She had never been good at it. But she needed to get up a knee-high step fast, and onto the second step before he moved, and Stride was the only way.

The trouble was that Edge and Stride were the same door.

She knew that. Everyone knew it. An Ember held one shape at a time. Blade or struts. Never both. Not below unbound mastery, and nobody in the Terrace Ring that afternoon was within a hundred miles of unbound mastery.

She asked for both anyway.

The metal tried. She felt it try. It started to flow from the bar on her fist back toward her slab and down toward her calves, and at the same time it tried to stay a bar, and for a moment it was neither. The bar went soft and lumpy on her fist. The struts started down her right leg and stopped at the knee.

Her right foot hit the edge of the first step with no strut under it and no weight behind it.

Her toe caught the stone lip. She went down.

She landed on her left knee on the edge of the step. It hurt more than anything had hurt in a long time, a white flash straight up her thigh. The half-formed metal ran back into her slab in a rush. She rolled onto her side on the step with her hands empty.

Kellan's blade touched her ribs.

"One," said the referee. "Renn leads, one and a half."

Kiva lay on the step and looked at the stone ceiling of the hall. Somebody in the gallery was laughing. Not cruelly. Just the way people laugh when somebody falls over.

She heard Perrin's voice say, "Shut up," quite loudly, and the laughing stopped.

"Up," said the referee. "Or yield."

Kiva got up.

Her knee was bad. Not broken. She could stand on it. But it was going to swell, and every step was going to tell her about it. She limped back down onto the sand and stood there with her hands at her sides and her chest aching and her breath coming hard, and looked at Kellan Renn on the second step above her.

He had not moved. He had not needed to.

---

She made herself stop.

Not stop fighting. Stop *doing*. She stood in the sand and let him stand on the step, and she did not open any doors at all, and she watched him.

She had been watching his blade. Everyone watched his blade. It was long and narrow and steady and fast, and it was the thing that hit you. But Mara had taught her that if you wanted to know what a machine was doing, you did not look at the part that moved the most. You looked at the part that moved the least, and you waited for it to change.

So she looked at his feet. And his left forearm, where the Ember cuff was. And his breath.

He came down the step toward her. He came the way he had come before, quick, along the step and then sharply down onto the sand at her left side. And as he came, she saw his blade go.

It did not vanish. It flowed. The long dark metal ran off his right fist and back up his arm to the cuff, smooth and fast. Then, for a sliver of a moment, his right hand was empty. Then the struts came down his calves, and he took two long, light, springing strides across the sand to her left, and the struts ran back up to the cuff, and his hand was empty again for a sliver, and then the blade was back on his fist.

And he breathed out.

Each time. At each change. A small, sharp breath out through his nose, after the metal was home and before the next shape began. The same breath Orla took. The breath that meant *door closed*.

Kiva stood in the sand and felt her whole idea of him turn over.

She had thought he was fast because he could do everything at once. She had watched him for three years in the junior lanes and thought that. Blade and struts and blade, so quickly that it looked like one thing. She had thought that was what being good meant. All of it, together.

It was not together. It was one thing, then a gap, then another thing. Blade, empty, struts, empty, blade. He sequenced. He sequenced so cleanly and so fast that the gaps were almost invisible. But they were there. She could see them now. She had been looking at the wrong part.

He was not fast because he stacked. He was fast because he never did.

He was doing exactly what Orla had been telling her to do for two years. He was just doing it about twenty times better.

He came at her from the left, blade back on his fist, and she turned to meet him, and as she turned she felt the bottom heat-line warm.

"One," she said, under her breath.

And she closed.

She did not reach for a ward. She did not reach for stance. She had the bar on her fist, the Ember door open, and she left it at that. One door. When the hand door lifted inside her to help, she did not let it in. She felt it at the doorway and she kept the door shut, the way you keep a door shut against a dog that wants to come in, gently, with your foot.

The bottom line stayed warm. The second line stayed cool.

His blade came at her ribs. She brought the bar across.

And she found out what the bar was for.

It was not a good blade. It had no point and no curve and it could not reach. But it was broad. It was flat and wide and heavy, as wide as her forearm, and when his narrow blade met it, his blade did not slide off. It stopped. It stopped dead against the flat of the bar as if it had hit a door.

He cut again, at her head. She brought the bar up. It stopped again.

He stepped back. For the first time all bout, he looked surprised.

---

The sand helped her. She did not plan that. She only noticed it.

He Strode away from her across the sand, two long springing steps toward the far steps, and on the second step his struts dug in.

The sand was coarse and deep. It had been raked smooth, but it had not been packed. When Kellan's strut-feet came down on it with all the force of a Stride behind them, they sank. Not much. A finger's depth. But his third stride was a fraction late, and when he reached the bottom step and pushed off it, he had to push twice.

He knew it too. She saw him glance down.

After that, he did not Stride on the sand. He came at her on his own feet, with just the blade. And his own feet, on the sand, were not as quick as his struts.

They fought for a long time after that. Longer than Kiva would have believed she could last.

It was not pretty. He was still much better than she was. He had a dozen ways to come at her and she had one way to stop him, which was to get the bar in the way. He came high and she got it there. He came low and she got it there, mostly. He came at her left side, where the bar was slow to reach, and she twisted and got the edge of it there, and the impact shook her to the elbow.

Every time the hand door woke, she kept it out. Every time she felt the bottom line warm, she said *one* under her breath and stayed where she was. One door. Only one.

It cost her. Not in heat. In everything else. Without stance, she could not root. Without a ward, she could not stop anything she could not reach with the bar. She was slow and heavy and one-handed and she could not hit him back, because hitting back meant opening the bar up, and every time she started to, he was already somewhere else.

But he could not hit her either.

She did not know how long it went on. A minute. Two. Her knee screamed at every step. Her arms ached to the shoulder. Her breath sawed in and out. The gallery had gone very quiet.

Then he changed.

She saw it a moment before he did it. He stopped coming at her bar. He came around her left side instead, wide, on the sand, where she had to turn heavily to face him. Once. Twice. She turned, and turned, and the bad knee buckled a little on the second turn.

He had seen the knee. Of course he had. He was learning her just as hard as she was learning him.

The third time, he came wide again, and she turned, and her heel hit stone.

She had backed all the way to the edge of the sand. The bottom step was right behind her. She felt its edge against her heel.

For an instant she wanted stance so badly it hurt. She wanted to drop into the still door and plant her feet and be unmovable. The still door was right there, open under her, waiting. The bottom line went hot.

She did not open it.

Instead she put her heel back against the riser of the step. Hard. She set her back foot flat against the stone, so that the step itself was behind her. It was not stance. It was not anything. It was just a wall to push against.

Kellan came in. He came low, at her left hip, the side the bar could not reach.

She did not try to block. She shoved off the step with her back foot, all her weight, and swung the bar not at his blade but at his arm behind it. Flat. Hard.

It hit his right forearm with a dull, heavy smack.

"Half," said the referee. "Rowan."

The gallery made a noise. It was not a cheer. It was more like a whole room of people drawing in a breath at once.

Kellan stepped back. He looked down at his forearm. Then at her.

Then the struts came down his calves, and he was not on the sand anymore. He took one long Stride straight up onto the bottom step to her left, where the stone was solid under him, and a second Stride along it, and came down off it behind her before she could turn.

His blade touched her back, between the shoulders.

"One," said the referee. "Renn wins, three to a half."

---

Kiva sat on the bottom step with her leg stretched out in front of her and breathed.

Her knee was already swelling. Her arms felt as though someone had beaten them with a mop handle. Under her shirt, the bottom heat-line ached, but only the bottom one. The other two had not lit since she fell on the step.

Kellan came over and stood on the sand in front of her. His blade had gone back into his cuff. He was breathing hard too, which pleased her more than she would have admitted.

"You stopped stacking," he said.

"You noticed."

"Halfway through." He rubbed his right forearm. There was a red mark on it already. "You were slower after that. And it was harder to hit you." He frowned, as if the two things did not fit together in his head, and he did not like it. "You should be easier to hit when you're slower."

"I know," said Kiva. "I thought so too."

He looked at her for a moment longer. Then he looked at the draw board on the rim of the ring, where the referee was writing up the numbers.

*Renn: dram spent, 0.31.*

*Rowan: dram spent, 0.88.*

Nearly three times as much. And most of it, Kiva knew, had gone in the first minute, standing in the sand with two doors open, paying to stand still.

"Your draw's terrible," said Kellan.

"I know."

"It's going to be terrible in the Cellhouse. The cell in there is Grade Second. If you stack on it, you'll burn through your dram in a minute and the cell's charge in three." He said it flatly, as if he were reading out a meter. "I'm not saying that to be cruel. I'm saying it because I've been put in teams with people who didn't know their own numbers. It's worse than being put in teams with people who are bad."

"I know my numbers."

"Good." He nodded once. Then he hesitated, which she had never seen him do. "The half point," he said. "Off the step. Was that a plan?"

"No," said Kiva honestly. "I wanted to stance. I didn't let myself. So I used the step instead."

He was quiet for a moment. Then he said, "Huh," very softly, and walked away across the sand.

---

Kellan Renn walked out of Stonehand Hall with his forearm throbbing and could not stop thinking about the step.

He went down the long front stairs into the afternoon light and did not stop at the Graywater table, though the woman in the slate-blue coat raised a hand to him. He did not stop at the Halden table either. He walked to the end of the plaza and sat on the low wall by the fountain and looked at the red mark on his arm.

It was a good mark. Clean. Square. The exact width of that ridiculous bar.

He had not been touched in a sanctioned bout since the autumn. Not once. He did not say that to anyone, because it sounded like boasting and it was not. It was a number. Numbers mattered to Kellan more than he could easily explain to people who had not grown up where he had.

His mother graded ash at the Halden refinery on the east bank. She had graded ash there for twenty-two years. She sat at a long bench with a meter and a set of sample cups and decided, can by can, whether a batch was First or Second or Third. It was one of the most important jobs in the city and almost nobody knew it existed. If she got it wrong, a cell somewhere in Meridian ran hot.

When Kellan was nine, a supervisor at the refinery had stamped a cut batch First to make his quarter. When the batch failed in a hospital in the Lantern district, he had tried to say Kellan's mother had graded it. She had not. She had flagged it. She had written the true number in the grading book, in ink, with the date and her initials, and the supervisor had changed the stamp after she went home.

The book had saved her. The number in the book. Nothing else would have. Not her word against his, not her twenty years, not the fact that everyone on the floor knew who was honest. Only the number, written down, by the person who had measured it.

Kellan had believed in measurement ever since. Not because it was perfect. Because it was the only thing that protected people who were not in charge.

The Circuit was built on measurement. Rank, hold, draw, call. Boards and numbers and logged bouts and files. It was not perfect either. He knew that. But it meant that a refinery grader's son from the east bank could stand in the Measure Hall and have a board read him Fire, and nobody could say it said anything else. It meant that when he went to Graywater or Saltmere or any of them, his number would be his. Not a favor. Not a family name. His.

And Kiva Rowan did not fit on the board.

That was what bothered him. Not that she was strange. Strange was fine. What bothered him was that the board could not read her. And if the board could not read her, then sooner or later somebody would have to decide what she was. Somebody in a little room. And then her place on the roster would be a ruling, not a number. And every ruling like that made the numbers mean a little less.

He believed that. He had believed it walking into the Terrace Ring.

But he had not expected to learn anything from her.

He had expected her to stack. Everyone said she stacked. He had watched her in the junior lanes, all doors and no control, burning ash like a broken cell. He had stood on the step and waited for her to burn out, and she had nearly done it. And then, halfway through, she had stopped. She had picked one door and kept it, and every time he hit at her, that stupid broad bar was in the way.

And then the step.

She had wanted the still door. He had seen it in her face. And she had used the stone instead. She had used the ring itself.

He had not thought of that. In three years of fighting in the Terrace Ring, he had used the steps to stand on, to get above people, to Stride off. He had never once thought of backing against a riser to get leverage without spending anything at all.

It was not a door. It would not show on any board. And it had cost him half a point.

Kellan sat on the fountain wall and rubbed his arm.

He did not like it. He did not like the idea that the thing that had touched him today was a thing no rig could measure. But he was honest with himself, the way his mother had taught him to be. He had learned something in that ring, and he had learned it from somebody the board could not read.

He took out the pencil he kept in his jacket and a scrap of paper, and wrote on it: *Rowan. Bar blocks narrow blades flat. Use risers for leverage. Slow when single door but harder to hit. Draw 0.88.*

He looked at it for a while. Then he folded it and put it in his pocket, and went to find out which team the board had put him on for the Cellhouse.

---

Mara read the book that night.

Kiva did not offer it. She came home limping, with her knee wrapped in a cold cloth from the Stonehand infirmary, and sat down at the kitchen table, and put the book down in front of her plate the way she always did. And Mara, instead of not mentioning it, picked it up.

She read every page. She read them slowly, the way she read cell books at the bench, with one finger moving down the columns. Kiva watched her eat nothing and turn pages.

At the last page, Mara stopped.

*Exercise:* Open lane bout, Terrace Ring. K. Renn.

*What went wrong:* Stacked stance and Edge at the start. Third door came in. All 3 lines. Splashed ward. Half point. Then tried Edge and Stride at once, same door, can't. Fell on step. Knee. One point. Then single door (Edge only). Kept hand door out. Held for about 2 min. Took half off his arm from the step. Then he Strode behind me. Lost 3 to ½.

*Who was hurt:* Me. Left knee. Kellan, right forearm (bruise).

*What it cost:* 0.88 of a dram. Mostly first minute.

*How long to come back:* Knee, a week? Lines, an hour.

*Second go:* Single door, said "one," closed. Harder to hit. Can't hit back. Too slow off the sand.

*Why:* He doesn't stack. Ever. He sequences, fast. I thought fast meant all at once. It means one at a time, clean, with no hurry in it.

Mara read the last line twice.

Then she put the book down and looked at Kiva across the table.

"Show me," she said. "Not a door. Just show me what you do. When the line goes."

Kiva stood up. She stood in the middle of the kitchen, away from the wall cell and the stove and the cold-cupboard, and raised her right fist and formed the bar. It came, ugly and dark. She held it. Then she let the hand door lift a little on purpose, just enough. The bottom line went warm.

"One," she said aloud. And she held the hand door at the threshold and did not let it in, and let the bar run home into the slab, and breathed out, and felt both doors go quiet.

"That's my meter," she said. "The bottom line. I say it out loud. That's my shutdown path. One door home before the next one starts. And that's my book." She nodded at it. "Every time. Even when it's stupid."

Mara sat looking at her for a long time.

"Now tell me what you can't do," she said.

Kiva had not expected that. She stood in the kitchen with her empty hands and her bad knee and thought about it honestly, because Mara would know if she did not.

"I can't stack," she said. "Not two doors, not three. Not on purpose and not safely. If I try, they fight, and I lose control of all of them." She took a breath. "I can't go fast. A full sequence takes nine seconds. In a fight, I can only use one door and keep the others out. I can't tune a cell. I can read one. I can't tune one, not while the hand door and the Ember both want it. If something goes wrong near a cell, I can't fix it with a door. I'd have to use the quench, the same as anyone." She stopped. "And I can't promise nothing will go wrong. I can only promise I'll say it out loud when it starts."

Mara looked at her for a moment longer.

Then she got up, went to the hook by the door where her apron hung, and took the countersignature form out of the pocket. She smoothed it flat on the kitchen table beside the field book. She took the pencil off its string.

Under *Guardian*, in her square capitals, she wrote *M. ROWAN*. Then she signed it.

She did not hand it to Kiva at once. She sat holding it.

"Those are good limits," she said quietly. "Know them in the Cellhouse. Don't let anyone talk you out of them. Not Orla. Not your team. Not that boy with the steady eyes. Not the gallery. And not yourself, when you want to show them all."

"I won't."

"You will," said Mara. "You'll want to. Everybody does." She held out the form. "When you do, say it out loud."

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-05.md

# Chapter 5 — Kiva: The Cellhouse

The board posted the teams on Thursday afternoon, and Perrin did not speak to her for an hour.

Kiva found out why when she read it. The list was pinned up on the Cellhouse gate under a sheet of glass, forty teams of four, and most of them were the same as the draw from Classification Day. Hers was not.

*Team Seven. Afternoon, second run.*
*K. Renn — Ember.*
*A. Pryce — Hand.*
*P. Cade — Still.*
*K. Rowan — Unclassified.*

Under it, in small print, a note in Ysra Crest's hand. *Composition by board ruling. Each door represented beside the unclassified candidate.*

"They moved me," said Perrin, when he finally spoke. They were sitting on the Cellhouse steps with the late sun on them. "I was morning. I was with three Stonehand people I've trained with for two years. I knew exactly what we'd do. Now I'm afternoon, with the best Ember-door in the Eastbank, a girl who wants a Graywater placement more than she wants to breathe, and you." He did not look at her. "They moved me so there'd be a still-door standing next to you. In case you need one."

"I didn't ask them to."

"I know you didn't. That's not the point." He picked at a splinter on the step. "The point is my bursary's riding on a team the board built around somebody else. I don't get to be on my own team. I get to be on yours."

Kiva did not know what to say to that. It was true.

"I'll do my job," said Perrin. "Whatever it is. I'll do it properly. I'm just telling you I'm angry about it, so it doesn't come out sideways in the middle of the run." He stood up and brushed off his trousers. "That's what Anwen would do. She'd tell you."

"I'm here," said Anwen, from the step above. "And yes. I would."

---

On Friday morning, Mara walked her to the tram.

She did not usually. She had the shop to open and the municipal van coming at nine. But she put on her coat and walked down Coper Street beside Kiva, slowly, because of the knee, and stood with her at the stop under the anniversary banner.

The knee was better. Not good. Kiva had iced it for two nights and the swelling had gone down, and she could walk on it without limping if she thought about it. Running would hurt. Landing hard would hurt a great deal. She had written that in the book too.

*What it cost:* Knee. Don't land on it.

"You've got your book," said Mara.

"I've got it."

"Your dram?"

"They issue it at the gate."

"Right." Mara looked down the street, at the tram coming over the bridge with its rings glinting. She put her hands in her coat pockets and took them out again. "I'm not coming to watch."

"I know."

"I'd want to stop it. Every time something went wrong. I'd be on my feet in the gallery shouting about quench handles." She almost smiled. "Orla will be there. She'll tell me."

The tram pulled in. The doors folded open.

Mara put her hand flat on Kiva's chest for a second, over the coat, over the disk, the way she had in the shop four years ago. Then she took it away.

"Say it out loud," she said.

"I will."

Kiva got on the tram. She stood without holding the rail. When she looked back, Mara was still standing at the stop in her work coat with her scarred hand at her side, watching the tram go, and she did not wave, and she did not leave.

---

The Cellhouse had been an ash refinery once, two hundred years ago, before the Halden yards on the east bank made every other refinery in the region unnecessary. Now it belonged to the Compact. From outside it was a long, tall brick building with a sawtooth roof and a row of high windows. Inside it was the most watched room in the Collegiate Circuit.

The candidates' waiting room was a long whitewashed corridor along the north side. Through a thick glass window at the end, you could see straight down into the main hall. Kiva stood at the window with Anwen and Perrin and looked at it while the first afternoon team ran.

It was a big room. About forty paces long and fourteen wide, two stories high, with steel trusses across the roof and the old refinery's crane rails still running along the walls. The floor was concrete, painted with faded yellow lanes. There were props scattered across it to make it look like a real street after something had gone wrong. A toppled cable reel. Stacked crates. A section of broken railing.

At the west end, nearest the waiting room, was the Gate, a big roll-up steel door where the teams came in. Just inside it, a stripe of green paint crossed the floor from wall to wall. The green line. Get the casualty over that, and the extraction was done.

At the east end, forty paces away, was the substation.

It was a raised steel platform, a mezzanine, about twice a man's height off the floor. A steel stair ran up to it along the south wall, twelve steps, narrow, with a rail on the open side. On top of the mezzanine sat the cell. Kiva could see it from here: a regulator drum like the one from Tallis Street, only bigger, with a big round meter on the front and a red quench handle on its side. Thick cables ran from it across the platform to a board on the east wall covered in rows of small lamps. The load board. Each lamp stood for a house, or a shop, or a streetlight. The whole board was the district. When the cell was running, the district was lit.

Underneath the mezzanine was the pump room. It was a low space enclosed in steel walls, with one wide doorway facing west into the hall. Across the doorway, a heavy steel fire shutter had come down two-thirds of the way and jammed there, leaving a gap about knee-high. An electric winch was bolted above it, its cable running up into the shutter's housing. Inside, in the dim light of a single lamp, Kiva could just see a shape lying on the floor.

The casualty. A weighted training dummy dressed in a pump-crew coverall. Its legs were pinned under a fallen pump housing.

And halfway along the south wall, set into the brick, was the chute.

It was a heavy steel hatch, square, about chest-high. Behind it was a ramp that went down into the dark. Below the Cellhouse, in a sealed vault in the old refinery basement, was the Kennel.

The Kennel was a bottle-rift. A real tear, very small, held open in a lattice that the Wren House maintained and a Glory master had impressed sixty years ago. It made husks. Small ones, the smallest kind of Riftspawn there was, Handful-grade. The Wren House kept them in the vault and let them up the chute on a timer, two or three at a time. Every collegiate team in Meridian trained against them. Every professional company had, once.

"They're coming up," said Perrin quietly.

Down in the hall, the first afternoon team was running. Four Wren House and Crooked Line candidates Kiva half knew. The chute hatch swung open with a clang. Something grey poured out of it onto the concrete.

It was about the size of a large dog. It pulled itself up into a shape. Four thick, stubby limbs. A low front end. No face. It turned toward the nearest of the candidates, a hand-door boy holding a ward across the pump room door, and lurched at him.

Kiva had seen husks before. Everyone had. They came through on the Sag Line once or twice a year, and there were drawings of them in every schoolbook. But she had never been this close to the Kennel while it was running. She watched the husk go at the boy's ward and stop against it, the way a bird stops against glass. She watched an Ember-door girl come in from the side and put her lit blade through the middle of it.

It came apart. There was no blood. It just let go of its shape and fell into a heap of fine grey ash on the concrete.

"Why does it go for the ward?" said Perrin. "Not the girl?"

"Worked power," said Anwen, without looking away. "They go at whatever's lit. The more lit, the more they want it."

"Like moths."

"Like moths that weigh as much as you do."

---

Ysra Crest briefed them herself.

She came into the corridor at two o'clock with a clipboard and stood in front of Team Seven while the others waited at the far end. She looked at each of them in turn.

"You've watched it," she said. "So I'll be brief. Four objectives. One. The casualty is pinned in the pump room. Get it free and over the green line. The standard is four minutes from the Gate. Two. The cell is running at evening peak and it has a fault in it. I won't tell you what. Bring it to a safe state, either by stable tune or by controlled shutdown. Controlled means you call it, you pull the quench yourself, and you write it down. If the cell goes into red and trips its own breaker, that is uncontrolled. Uncontrolled is a fail on objective two." She turned a page. "Three. Contain the spawn. No husk reaches the casualty. No husk crosses the green line. Four. Call. Declared roles, said aloud, before you start. Any change of role is said aloud before you make it. After the run you fill in your team ledger card. I read every one."

She looked up.

"Violations. A lit Edge on any person. An undeclared role change. Overspending your dram. The board on the gallery watches all four of you, and so do I. If I think the run has become unsafe, I hit the master stop, the cell quenches, the chute locks, and the run is over." Her eyes went to Kiva and stayed there a moment. "Questions?"

"The cell," said Kellan. "What's the charge?"

"Grade Second. Standard municipal tune, with one fault."

"And the winch on the shutter?"

Ysra raised an eyebrow, very slightly. "It runs off the cell. Same circuit as the load board."

Kellan nodded. Kiva saw Anwen write something on her list.

"Settle your drams," said Ysra. "You have ten minutes to declare roles. Then the Gate."

---

They argued about the roles for nine of the ten minutes.

It started the moment Ysra walked away. Kellan turned to face the other three, and Kiva saw at once that he had done this before. Many times. He did not ask. He laid it out.

"Spawn is me," he said. "I hold the chute side and clear anything that comes up. Pryce, you're the casualty. You've got the Sustain read and a ward. You get under that shutter, read the dummy, ward it if anything comes near. Cade, you're the shutter. It's jammed. The winch is weak. You hold it so it doesn't come down on Pryce while she's under it, and you lift the pump housing off the legs." He turned to Kiva. "Rowan. You're the cell."

"I can't tune it."

"I know you can't tune it. You read it. You tell us what it's doing. If it goes wrong, you quench."

"That's what I was going to say."

"Good." He nodded once. "Then we're done."

"No," said Perrin. "Wait."

Kellan looked at him.

"If she quenches," said Perrin slowly, "the cell goes dead."

"Yes."

"And the winch runs off the cell. Crest said so."

"Yes."

"So if she quenches while I'm under the shutter," said Perrin, "the winch dies and the whole weight of that thing comes down on me. And on Anwen. And the dummy."

There was a short silence.

"You're still-door," said Kellan. "You can hold it."

"Can I? How heavy is it?"

"Heavy," said Anwen. She was looking through the window at the shutter. "Steel. Four hundred kilos, maybe. More, with the housing."

Perrin let out a breath. "I've held three hundred in the Hall, on a rack, for thirty seconds. Ready. Planted. Not lying on my back halfway under a door."

"Then don't be under it when she quenches," said Kellan.

"Then she has to know where I am."

"She'll be on the mezzanine. She can see the door from there."

"Then the cell has to last," said Anwen, "until Perrin and I are out. Which means the cell is the clock." She turned from the window. "If the cell has a fault in it and it's drifting, then we need to know how fast. If it's going to trip in two minutes, we've got two minutes to get the casualty out. If it's going to trip in one, we have to do it differently."

Kellan was quiet for a moment. Kiva saw him take the information in, the way he had taken in the step. He did not like it. He did not argue with it.

"Rowan," he said. "Can you tell how fast?"

"I can read it," said Kiva. "I can't tell you until I've got my hand on it."

"Your hand." He frowned. "With a door?"

"No. Just my hand. I can feel cells without opening anything. I always could." She hesitated. Then she said the rest, because she had promised. "I can feel it. I can't fix it. If I open a door near it, they'll fight, and the cell will take the fight. That's what cells do. So I'm not opening anything. I'm reading it, and calling it, and if it goes, I'm pulling the handle with my hands. Same as anybody."

Kellan looked at her for a long moment. "You're sure?"

"I told my aunt. I'm telling you."

"Fine." He looked at the others. "Then that's it. Renn, spawn. Pryce, casualty. Cade, shutter. Rowan, cell. Read, call, quench. No doors near the cell." He looked at Kiva. "That's what goes on the card."

"That's what goes on the card," said Kiva.

Anwen wrote it on the team ledger card in her small neat hand. Then she looked at it, and looked at Kiva, and added one line underneath.

*Rowan: calls clock to Pryce/Cade.*

"So we know," she said.

They settled their drams. Kiva tipped the grey powder into her left palm and closed her hand around it. It went in warm and settled, and the ash in her hand went dead. Beside her, Kellan brushed his off onto the floor. Anwen held hers in both hands and closed her eyes. Perrin swallowed his like medicine, with a face.

A bell rang at the end of the corridor.

"Team Seven," called a clerk. "To the Gate."

---

From the floor of the hall, the gallery looked very high.

It ran along the whole north wall above them, a long balcony behind thick glass. At the near end sat the assessors' booth, where Ysra Crest stood with two others in front of a bank of instruments and a big brass lever with a red grip. The master stop. Beyond the booth, the public seats rose in tiers behind the glass, full to the back row. Kiva could see faces pressed close. Somebody had a sign. Further along, there was a section with better chairs, and the slate blue of Graywater coats, and the green of Saltmere, and the Halden black and gold.

She found Orla in the third row of the public seats, sitting very straight, with her stick across her knees.

Kiva looked away and did not look up again.

The Gate rolled down behind them with a long rattling crash. They were inside.

The air smelled of old brick and hot metal and, very faintly, of something else. Something like the air before a storm. That was the Kennel, below the floor. Kiva could feel it through her boots, a low pressure, the same pressure the masts on the Sag Line drew on their slow paper drums.

A bell rang overhead.

"Go," said Kellan.

They went.

Kiva did not run. She had decided that on the tram. The knee would not take it, and the cell would still be there in ten seconds. She walked fast down the long hall, along the north wall under the gallery, past the crates and the cable reel. To her right, Kellan was already halfway to the chute side, moving in a long, light stride with his blade forming on his fist as he went. Anwen and Perrin passed her at a run, heading for the pump room.

She reached the bottom of the steel stair, put her good leg on the first step, and climbed.

Twelve steps. Narrow. The rail on the open side was cold under her hand. At the top she stepped out onto the mezzanine, and the cell was in front of her.

It was a big drum, bigger than Tallis Street, waist-high, bolted to the platform. The meter on its face was as wide as a dinner plate. The needle sat at six, just inside the yellow. Beyond the drum, the load board on the east wall glowed with its rows of little lamps, a whole district at evening peak, every light lit. From the top of the board, a thick cable ran down the wall and along the underside of the mezzanine to the winch over the shutter.

On the side of the drum was the quench. A red handle with a wire seal through it. Below it, the steel quench box with its hinged lid.

She put her right hand flat on the drum. Her hand. No door.

She felt it at once.

It was a good tune. Standard municipal. Even and clean. But it leaned. Under the clean shape, there was a tightness, a forward tilt, like someone standing on the edge of a step. The ash was releasing a little faster than the pattern meant it to. And faster. And faster.

She knew it. She knew it exactly.

"It's a hurry," she said aloud. She did not mean to say it. It came out.

Somebody had put Brannock's hurry in it. On purpose. The assessors had tuned the cell the way a tired man tunes it at six in the morning with two more substations to do before seven. And now it was running at evening peak, with the whole board lit, and the hurry was feeding on itself.

She looked at the needle. Six and two-tenths. She counted. In ten seconds it was six and three.

A tenth every ten seconds. Faster as it went.

The breaker would trip at the red line, at eight and a half.

She did the sum. She did it twice. Then she turned and leaned over the mezzanine rail.

Below her, at the pump room door, Perrin was on his back. He had slid under the jammed shutter feet-first and planted both hands flat against the bottom edge of it. Kiva could feel his stance from up here, the still door open under him like a held breath. Anwen had gone in under the gap beside him and was kneeling over the dummy with her hands flat on its chest.

"Clock!" Kiva called. "It's drifting fast. The needle's at six and three. It's rising a tenth every ten seconds and getting faster. It'll trip in about two minutes. Maybe less."

"Two minutes," said Anwen's voice from inside, muffled. "Copy. Perrin, the housing."

"Working on it," said Perrin through his teeth.

At the chute, the hatch swung open with a clang.

---

The first husk came out at thirty seconds.

Kiva heard it hit the concrete. She turned and saw it pour out of the hatch and pull itself into shape, grey and heavy and faceless, about the size of a large dog. It swung its low front end toward the pump room. Toward Perrin's open still door. Toward Anwen's Sustain.

Kellan was already there.

He came in from the side in two long Strides, the struts running down his calves and back up as he landed, and the blade on his fist before his second foot touched. He lit it. The thin blue-white line ran along the edge, perfectly steady. He put it through the middle of the husk at the height where a heart would be on a dog. The husk came apart into a heap of grey ash on the concrete.

He did not look at the ash. He stepped back to the chute side and waited, blade low, already watching the hatch.

The needle on the drum read six and six.

Kiva looked at the ash on the floor. It lay in a fine pale heap, clean as flour. Clean ash. In a real rift it would be money, a dram's worth at least. The scoring sheet gave points for clean ash recovered.

Kellan had not even glanced at it.

"Six and seven," she called. "Ninety seconds."

"Copy," said Anwen.

Under the shutter, she could see Perrin's arms trembling. He had got his shoulder under the edge of the pump housing on the dummy's legs. He was lifting the housing and holding the shutter at the same time, his still door holding the ground under his back while his arms did the work. It was not stacking. It was all one door. But it was a lot to ask of one door.

"Housing's heavy," he said. "It's caught on the frame. I need another thirty."

"You'll have it," said Kiva.

The needle read six and eight.

The chute clanged. Two husks this time, one after the other. Kellan met the first as it came out of the hatch, before it had finished pulling itself together, and it came apart in a spray of ash. The second got past him. It went sideways along the south wall, low and quick, toward the bottom of the mezzanine stair.

Toward her.

Kiva felt it before she understood it. She was the only thing on the mezzanine. She was standing next to a cell running hot. The cell was worked power, a pattern held on ash, burning faster and faster. It was the brightest thing in the room.

The husk was not coming for her. It was coming for the cell.

Kellan saw it too. He Strode after it in two long bounds and caught it at the foot of the stair and put his lit blade through its back. It came apart on the first step. Ash ran down between the steel treads.

"It's the cell," Kellan called up. He did not sound worried. He sounded as if he had just read a number off a meter. "They'll keep going for the cell as it runs hotter. I'll hold the stair."

"Six and nine," said Kiva. "Seven in ten seconds. Sixty seconds. Maybe less."

"Perrin," said Anwen.

"I know."

The needle crossed seven.

---

Kiva stood with her hand on the drum and felt the hurry in it and knew how to fix it.

That was the worst of it. She knew exactly. She could feel the tilt in the pattern under her palm, the lean of it, as clearly as she had felt Brannock's in Mara's shop. If Mara had been standing here, she would have laid her hand on the drum and smoothed it out in twenty seconds. Ten. She would have found the edge of the hurry and pressed it flat, the way you press a wrinkle out of a sheet, and the needle would have stopped climbing.

And then the cell would last. And the winch would run. And Perrin would have all the time he needed.

The hand door was right there. It had been there since she put her palm on the drum, lifted and curious, at the threshold. It wanted the cell. It always wanted cells.

*No doors near the cell.* That was on the card. She had said it to Kellan's face.

Seven and one.

She looked down at the pump room. Perrin's whole body was shaking now. His face was red and his teeth were bared. The housing was half off the dummy's legs. Anwen was pulling at the coverall, trying to slide the dummy out sideways.

Seven and two.

"Thirty more," said Perrin. "I need thirty more."

He did not have thirty more. At this rate, the needle would be in the red in forty seconds. Then the breaker would trip, and the winch would die, and the shutter would come down with its whole weight on a boy who was already holding all he could.

*One door,* thought Kiva. *Only one.*

She had done it in the Terrace Ring. She had kept the hand door out for two minutes while Kellan Renn tried to hit her. She could keep the Ember out now. She could keep the still door out. She could open only the hand door, only that, and lay it on the cell, and press the hurry flat, and close it again. One door. Sequence. Not stack.

It was not on the card.

"Changing role," she called, loudly, so the gallery could hear it, so Kellan could hear it. "Rowan. Cell. Tuning. Hand door only."

"*What?*" said Kellan from the bottom of the stair.

"I can feel the fault. It's a hurry. I can press it out. One door."

"You said no doors!"

"I'm saying it out loud!"

She did not wait for him. She closed her eyes, and opened the hand door, and laid it on the cell.

---

It was like putting her hand into warm water.

The cell was right there, all of it. The ash packed in its steel can. The pattern holding it. And the hurry, running through the pattern like a crease in cloth. She found the edge of it. She pressed.

The hurry gave.

It did not go all at once. It went like a knot coming loose, slowly, a little at a time. She felt the tilt in the pattern ease. She felt the ash, which had been rushing, begin to slow.

She opened her eyes. The needle read seven and two.

It read seven and two. It read seven and two.

It had stopped.

"It's holding!" she called down. "Perrin, it's holding, you've got time—"

The bottom heat-line was cool. The second was cool. She had the hand door open and only the hand door, and the Ember was shut behind her like a door with her foot against it, and the still door was quiet under her. She was doing it. One door, on a live cell, in the Cellhouse, with the gallery watching.

Something leapt in her chest that was not a heat-line. It was joy.

Below, the chute clanged.

Three husks.

They came out of the hatch in a grey heap, all at once, faster than the others had. Kellan met the first. He put his blade through it and it came apart. The other two were already past him.

They did not go for the pump room. They did not go for Kellan. They went straight along the south wall toward the stair.

Toward the mezzanine. Toward the cell.

Toward her.

She understood it in the same instant she saw them move. The cell had been bright before. Now it was brighter. Now there was a person standing over it with a door open, laying worked power into it with both hands. She had made it the most lit thing in the whole Cellhouse.

She had made it a lamp. And the moths were coming.

Kellan Strode after them. He caught one at the foot of the stair. The other was already on the steps. It was climbing. It went up the narrow steel stair the way a dog climbs, all four stubby limbs scrabbling, heavy and sure, and the treads rang under it.

Kiva saw it coming up the stair at her.

And she was afraid.

She did not decide to be. It went through her like cold water. And the Ember door, which had been shut behind her with her foot against it, felt her fear and came open on its own, the way a dog comes through a door when it hears you shout.

The bar came up on her fist.

The bottom heat-line went hot. Then the second. Then the still door, under her, startled, opened too.

And all three doors went into the cell.

She felt it happen. She still had the hand door on the drum, open, and the other two came through behind it before she could stop them. Edge and stance and ward, three doors pulling from three sides, and the cell under her hand took all of it. It did not care what she meant. It held what she put in it.

The heat-lines blazed.

The needle on the drum swung from seven and two to seven and eight in a single breath.

The husk reached the top of the stair.

Kiva tore her hand off the cell. She swung the ugly bar around and hit the husk flat in its front end as it came onto the platform, hard, with all her weight behind it. It was not a cut. The bar was dark. But the husk was small and she hit it hard, and the shape went out of it, and it came apart into ash and slid back down the stair in a grey spill.

She stood on the mezzanine with the bar on her fist and her chest on fire, breathing in short, hard gasps.

She had let go of the cell.

She looked at the drum.

The needle read seven and nine.

Eight.

It was still climbing. Faster than before. Much faster. Her hand was off it. The doors were off it. She shut the hand door, hard, and felt it close. The Ember was still open, the bar still on her fist. The still door was half open under her. But none of them were touching the cell.

The cell did not care.

She put her palm back on the drum, just her hand, no door, and felt it.

It was not the hurry anymore. The hurry was gone. She had pressed it out. In its place was something else. Something she had never felt in a cell in her life. Three shapes at once, pulling against each other. An edge, and a wall, and a weight. Fighting. Twisting round and round inside the pattern like three people pulling on one blanket.

Her own doors. Her own fight. She had put it in there.

And it was still going, after she had let go.

A cell keeps what you leave in it.

Below her, the chute clanged. She heard more husks come out onto the concrete. She heard Kellan shout something. She did not hear what. She was looking at the needle.

Eight and one.

And she could feel it now, through her palm. The cell was not only running hot. It was calling. The three-door noise she had left in it was ringing out of the drum like a struck bell, louder and louder, and every husk in the Cellhouse could hear it.

"I've stopped," she whispered. "It hasn't."

Eight and two.

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-06.md

# Chapter 6 — Kiva: What the Cell Kept

Eight and two.

Kiva stood on the mezzanine with her palm flat on the drum and felt her own three doors fighting inside it, round and round, and knew what she had to do.

The quench handle was right there. A hand's width from her hip. Red, with its little wire seal. All she had to do was break the seal and pull, and the ash would drop through the gate into the wet salt below, and the pattern would die, and the fight she had left in the cell would die with it. The calling would stop. The needle would fall. It would be controlled. She would call it, and pull it, and write it down.

And the winch would die.

She leaned over the rail and looked down at the pump room.

Perrin was still on his back under the shutter. His hands were flat against its bottom edge and his shoulder was under the pump housing, and he was holding both. His face was dark red. Inside, on her knees, Anwen had both arms around the dummy's middle and was dragging at it. Its legs were still under the housing. Kiva could see the edge of the housing lifted a hand's width off them. Not enough.

If she quenched now, the winch would let go of the shutter. Four hundred kilos of steel would come down onto Perrin's hands. And then onto Perrin. And then onto Anwen, and the dummy, all three of them in the gap.

"Out!" she shouted. "Perrin, Anwen, I need to quench. Get out!"

"Not out," came Anwen's voice. It was strained and flat. "Legs still pinned. Ten seconds."

"I haven't got ten seconds!"

"Then you've got whatever you've got," said Anwen. "I'm not leaving it."

Eight and three.

The chute clanged. Kiva turned her head.

Three husks poured out of the hatch onto the concrete. They came out faster than any of the others, and they did not stop to find their shape. They found it as they moved, grey and heavy, pulling themselves into legs as they ran. Two went along the south wall toward the stair. Toward Kellan, who was standing on the fourth step with his blade lit and his back to her.

The third did not go to the stair.

It went straight across the floor, in a line, toward the mezzanine. Toward the cell. And the shortest way to the cell, for a thing with no face and no sense and only one want, was not up the stair. It was underneath.

Under the mezzanine. Under the cell. Through the pump room.

Through the only gap in the pump room wall, which was a knee-high space under a jammed steel shutter, with a boy lying in it.

Kiva saw it the way you see a cup start to tip off a table. All of it at once. The line the husk was taking. The gap. Perrin's red face. Perrin's hands on the shutter. Perrin's still door, wide open under him, holding the floor. Worked power, lit and bright, right there in the way.

He could not move. If he let go of the shutter, it would come down on him. If he let go of the housing, it would come down on the dummy. He was holding everything, and the husk was coming for the thing behind him, and he was in the way.

She did not decide.

She put her left hand on the rail and went over it.

---

The bar was still on her fist. The Ember door was still open. She let the bar go, and the metal ran back up her arm and down, the same door, a different shape, into struts. They came down the backs of her calves and along her soles as she fell.

It was two and a half meters to the floor. She landed on the struts.

They took most of it. They were made for this. The metal flexed and caught her and drove her forward in a long, low bound, and for a moment she felt what Kellan must feel every time he did it, the whole fall turned into speed. Then her left foot came down on the second bound and the knee went.

It did not give way. It screamed. A white flash straight up her thigh, the same as the step in the Terrace Ring, worse. She took the third bound on her right leg only and came down two paces in front of the pump room door, between the gap and the husk.

It was five paces away. Coming.

She needed the still door.

She had one breath to get it. She knew how it was supposed to go. Orla had said it in the yard. *The speed of a Stride going straight into a stance, so you stop where you mean to and nobody moves you.* Not drill. Sequence. The Ember's work handed to the still door without dropping it.

Nobody Orla knew could do that.

Kiva reached for it anyway.

She let the struts go. She felt the metal start to run home toward the slab. She felt the still door under her, open, ready. And the husk was four paces off, three, and the metal was not home yet, and she could not wait for it.

She went into stance with the Ember still in the doorway.

The bottom heat-line lit. The second.

The hand door lifted to help. It wanted to ward. It always wanted to ward. She felt it come up cool and quick inside her, reaching, and for one instant the third line began to warm.

"*One,*" she said, through her teeth, and shut the hand door on it.

She held it. Two doors open. Still and Ember. The struts half-melted on her calves, the stance coming up through her feet. Two lines burning. The third line warm and not lit. The hand door shut, with her foot against it, while everything else fought.

The husk hit her.

---

It was like being hit by a sack of wet cement thrown from a cart.

It hit her square in the chest and left side, low and heavy. The force of it went into her and down. Down through her ribs and her hips and her knees, the good one and the bad one, down through her feet into the concrete. And the concrete took it.

She did not move.

The husk did not understand that. It had no way to understand anything. It shoved at her, all four stubby limbs scrabbling on the floor, and she stood in its way like a post set in the ground. Behind her, she could hear Perrin breathing in great ragged gasps. She could feel his still door and hers, side by side, both holding.

And the cell rang.

She felt it through the stance. Through the ground. Through her own body. Above her, on the mezzanine, her own three-door fight was still twisting inside the drum, and it was calling out louder than ever, and something inside her answered it.

It came back down the heat-lines.

She had felt backwash before. Every Kindled person had, once or twice, when a cell kicked. It was like a burn from the inside. This was worse. This was her own fight coming home. The cell held three doors pulling against each other, and it was pulling on the three doors in her, and the lines on her chest went from hot to white.

They burned past her shoulder. They burned down the top of her arm. She had never felt them go past her shoulder before. She felt them now, three wires drawn tight from her breastbone over her collarbone and down toward her elbow, and the skin along them felt as if somebody had laid a poker on it.

The husk shoved. One of its front limbs came up and dragged across her left forearm, hard, scraping the skin off it. She had put her left hand up without thinking, the way you put up a hand to stop a door from slamming. It was the hand that wanted to ward. She had not let it. So it was just a hand.

The limb ground across the back of her left hand and over the first two fingers. They went numb. Not hurt. Numb. As if they had been in snow.

"*Kiva,*" said Perrin behind her. It was half a word and half a groan.

Above them, the needle on the drum crossed the red line.

There was a sharp, flat crack from the mezzanine. The breaker.

The load board on the east wall went dark. Every little lamp in the district went out at once. The lamp in the pump room went out. And the winch above the shutter, which had been humming quietly all through the run, stopped.

The shutter dropped.

It did not fall far. It could not. Perrin's hands were under it. Kiva heard it come down onto his palms with a sound like a dropped anvil, and she heard Perrin make a noise she had never heard a person make, deep and short and not quite a scream.

Then she heard nothing at all from him.

For one terrible moment she thought it had crushed him.

Then, behind her, through the stance, she felt his still door. It was open. It was wider than she had ever felt it. It had gone down into the floor under him like roots, and it was holding four hundred kilos of steel on two hands, and it was not moving.

"Holding," said Perrin, in a whisper. "Holding. Holding."

---

The cell was still calling.

Kiva could feel it. The breaker had cut the load. The board was dark. The winch was dead. But the cell itself was still running. The pattern was still in it. Her pattern. Her fight. It was not driving anything now. It was just ringing, louder and louder, like a bell with nobody to stop it. And the husk on her chest was still pushing toward it, and the two at the foot of the stair were still climbing at Kellan.

The breaker did not stop a pattern. Only the quench did that.

The quench was on the mezzanine. Two and a half meters over her head, on the far side of a steel rail. She could not get to it. She was holding a husk off a boy holding a shutter off a girl holding a dummy, and if she moved one step, all of it came down.

She turned her head and shouted.

"*Kellan!*"

He was on the stair. She could see him past the edge of the husk, on the fifth step, with his lit blade and his back to the wall. One husk was coming up the stair at him. The other was in pieces on the bottom tread.

"Quench!" she shouted. "The red handle. It's still calling. The breaker doesn't stop it. You have to pull it. It's mechanical. Just pull it!"

He did not look round. The husk on the stair was almost on him.

"*Kellan, the quench!*"

---

Kellan Renn heard her the first time.

He did not look round, because a husk was two steps below him and coming up. He had been fighting on this stair for twenty seconds, and he had learned it in the first five. It was narrow. One pace wide, maybe less. Brick wall on his right. A steel rail on his left with a drop to the floor. The treads were open steel grating, and the husk ash from the last one had run down between them and made every step slick.

You could not Stride on a stair. He had known that before he stepped on it. Struts were for open ground. On a stair they caught on the treads and threw you. So it was the blade, and only the blade, and his feet.

He liked it, a little. He would not have said so. A stair took everything away except the thing he was best at.

The husk came up. He thrust down at it, straight, because there was no room to cut sideways without hitting brick or rail. His blade went into its front end. Not deep enough. It shoved up against the blade, and he felt the weight of it come into his arm, and his front foot slid on the ash.

"*Kellan, the quench!*"

He looked at the mezzanine.

It was above him and to his left. The stair went up seven more steps to the platform. At the top, the drum. On its side, the red handle. He could see it from here. He could see the meter, too, its needle pinned past the red line.

And he could feel it.

He had felt it for the last thirty seconds and not had time to think about it. A ringing. A wrongness. The cell up there was not running like any cell he had ever stood beside. It was loud. Not to his ears. To the part of him that his Ember door opened onto. It was like standing beside a bell that was being struck over and over.

The husk on his blade was pushing toward it. So was the one on Rowan's chest. So, he realized, had every husk that had come out of that chute since she put her hand on the drum.

She had said it would call them. In the corridor. She had said it plainly. *If I open a door near it, they'll fight, and the cell will take the fight.*

She had been right.

She had been right about the clock, too. *A tenth every ten seconds. Two minutes. Maybe less.* He had heard every number she called. He had checked them, without meaning to, against the meter whenever he glanced up. She had been right every time, to the tenth.

His mother would have liked her numbers.

The husk shoved. He held it on the blade. He had two choices and he could see both of them clearly, the way he saw everything clearly when it mattered.

He could finish this husk. Turn it, free the blade, put it through the knot of the thing, and then turn and finish whatever came up next. He would score. Two kills on a stair, clean, with the gallery watching. Graywater would write it down. That was his role. Spawn. It was on the card.

Or he could leave it.

Leave a live husk on the stair behind him. Break off. Get to the quench. Do the thing that was not on the card, because the girl who had broken the card twice already was shouting at him from the floor that it was the only thing that would work.

He did not believe in people. He believed in measurements.

Her measurements had been right all run.

He made the decision. He did not like it. He made it anyway.

"Renn!" he shouted, loud enough for the booth. "Changing role. Taking quench!"

He yanked the blade free. The husk lurched forward on the slick step, off balance, and he let it. He went up two steps backward. Then he turned, and put his left hand on the rail, and asked for struts.

Not to Stride. To jump.

The metal ran off his fist and down his legs in one smooth flow. The blade was gone. He felt the empty instant, the gap he never let anybody see. He breathed out. Then he put his right foot on the rail and pushed off it with everything the struts had.
