The rail was not wide. It rang under his boot. For a moment he was in the air beside the stair, over the drop, with nothing under him.

Then his hands caught the edge of the mezzanine.

He hauled himself up onto the platform. His knee hit the steel plate. He was up. He was at the drum. The ringing was so loud here that it made his teeth ache.

He put his hand on the red handle and broke the wire seal with his thumb.

He pulled.

---

Kiva heard the quench go.

It was a heavy clank, and then a long, rushing hiss, like water thrown on a stove. The gate in the bottom of the drum had opened. The ash was pouring through it into the box of wet salt.

And the ringing stopped.

It did not fade. It stopped, the way a bell stops when you put your hand on it. One moment her own three doors were twisting round and round inside the drum and calling to everything in the room. The next moment there was nothing in the drum at all. Just steel and dead salt.

The husk on her chest went slack.

It did not fall apart. It just stopped pushing. It stood against her with its limbs braced, heavy and grey, and seemed to forget what it had been doing. Like a dog when the whistle stops.

The heat-lines did not stop. They kept burning. But the white went out of them. They went back to merely hot.

"That's it," she heard someone say. It was Anwen, inside the pump room. "That's it, Perrin, it's moving, it's off—"

There was a grinding scrape. Kiva could not turn to look. She could hear Anwen hauling, and the dummy's coverall dragging over concrete, and Anwen's breath hissing through her teeth.

Something came down the stair behind the husk. Fast.

Kellan dropped off the bottom step onto the floor with his blade back on his fist. It was dark. He had let the light go out. He came at the husk on Kiva's chest from the side, and he did not cut. He drew the dark blade back and brought it down flat on the middle of the husk's back, as hard as he could, like a man splitting a log with the back of an axe.

The husk came apart.

It came apart all over her. Grey ash, fine as flour, poured down her front and over her arms and into her collar and her hair. It was faintly warm. She stood in the middle of it, still rooted, with her arms out and ash running off her fingers, and could not quite believe it was gone.

"Don't light near people," said Kellan. He was breathing hard. "It's a violation."

"I know," said Kiva.

"I know you know." He was already turning. "Stair husk's still up there. I'll get it."

He went.

---

Anwen brought the dummy out under the gap feet-first.

It was a seventy-kilo bag of sand in a coverall, and she was not big. She had it by the collar with both fists and was walking backward on her knees, hauling it a hand's length at a time. Kiva felt the dummy's boots scrape past her heels.

"Kiva," said Anwen. "Move. I need the room."

Kiva gave the ground its weight back.

She did it badly. She did it with two doors open and both of them hurting, and the bad knee nearly folded when the stance let go. But she lifted her heels and breathed in and thought *thank you*, and the still door eased. Then she let the Ember close. The half-formed struts on her calves ran home into the slab.

She stepped to the side. Her left leg would barely hold her.

Anwen hauled the dummy clear of the doorway and stood up and got her arms under its shoulders and began to drag it. Toward the green line. Forty paces down the long dark hall.

"Perrin," said Kiva.

He was still under the shutter. She could see his hands on its bottom edge. They were shaking. The whole of his arms were shaking. The shutter was resting on his palms and the pump housing was resting on his shoulder, and his face had gone from red to grey.

"Can't—" he said. "Can't let go. If I let go it drops on my legs."

"Then let it down slowly."

"Can't do slowly. I'm too tired to do slowly."

Kiva crouched beside him. Her knee screamed. She did not care. She put both hands flat on the shutter beside his, her right hand and her numb left hand, and she did not open any door. She could not have lifted it anyway. But she could feel him through it.

"Give it back," she said. "Like you told me. Heels. Breathe in. Give it back a little at a time."

He looked at her. His eyes were wet.

"That's stupid," he said.

"I know. It works."

He breathed in. She felt his still door ease, a fraction. The shutter came down a finger's width onto his palms. He held it. He breathed in again. Another finger.

"Roll," said Kiva. "When I say. Roll out to me."

The shutter came down. A finger. Another. It was at his elbows now. At his shoulder. He let the pump housing slide off his shoulder onto the concrete inside with a heavy clang. Then he took his hands away, all at once, and rolled toward her, and the shutter came down the last of the way behind him with a crash that shook the floor.

He lay on his back on the concrete beside her with his eyes shut and his hands lying palm-up on his chest. They were purple across the palms. They were shaking so badly that his fingers twitched.

"I held it," he said, without opening his eyes.

"You held it."

"Four hundred kilos. On my hands. On my back." He laughed. It came out wrong, high and cracked. "Nobody's going to believe me."

"Everybody saw."

"Nobody's going to believe me anyway."

Down the hall, Anwen dragged the dummy over the green line.

A bell rang overhead. Then a long horn.

The run was over.

---

It was very quiet after the horn.

The lamps in the hall came back on, the ordinary overhead lamps that ran off the Cellhouse's own core, not the trial cell. The chute hatch was shut and locked. The load board on the east wall stayed dark.

Kiva sat on the concrete beside Perrin with her back against the pump room wall and looked at herself.

She was grey from her chin to her knees. The ash had got everywhere. Into her collar, down her sleeves, into the creases of her hands. Her left forearm was scraped raw from wrist to elbow, and the raw part had ash in it. Her left hand was numb across the back and the first two fingers. She tried to bend them. They bent. She could not feel them bending. It was like moving someone else's fingers.

And there was something hanging against the front of her shirt.

The disk. It had come out of her collar when the husk hit her. It lay on its cord against her chest, dark and grey with ash, the nine grooves in it full of powder. She closed her good hand around it.

It was cold.

Of course it was cold. It was always cold. It had been cold through everything. Through the hurry and the joy of fixing it and the three doors going in. Through the jump and the landing and the stance with two doors open. She had done the thing Orla said nobody could do, or tried to, and it had not been right, and the disk had not cared one way or another.

She tucked it back inside her shirt. Ash came with it.

A Wren House medic came across the floor with a bag and knelt beside her. He was young and brisk and had the calm, slightly bored face of someone who had seen a great many Cellhouse runs. He looked at the forearm first, then the knee. Then he looked at the way she was holding her left shoulder.

"Open your collar for me."

She did. He looked at the heat-lines.

She had not seen them yet. She looked down now. They were not pink anymore. They were red, angry red, and raised along their length like welts, and in two places the skin had gone shiny and tight over a blister. They ran from the middle of her breastbone, over her collarbone, past the point of her shoulder, and down the outside of her upper arm. Halfway to her elbow.

They had never gone past her shoulder. In four years. Not once.

The medic's face did not change, but he was quiet for a moment.

"Backwash," he said. "From the cell?"

"From the cell. Through me."

"Mm." He laid two fingers lightly beside the lines, not on them. "These are new? This far down?"

"New."

He did not say anything. He took a jar of burn salve out of his bag and began to spread it along the lines, very gently, with a flat wooden blade. It was cold and it stung. Then he took her left hand and turned it over and pressed his thumb into the tips of her first two fingers, one at a time.

"Feel that?"

"No."

He pressed the third finger. "That?"

"Yes."

He pressed the first two again, harder. She felt a faint, dull pressure, very far away.

"Nerve irritation," he said. "It follows the same line as the backwash. Down the arm into the hand. Those two fingers are on the same nerve." He let go of her hand. "It should come back in a few hours. A day, at most. If it doesn't, you come to the Wren House and you ask for Healer Aske, and nobody else." He looked at her. "It'll come back this time. Next time it might not. Do you understand me?"

"I understand."

"Good." He stood up. "Ice the knee. Don't use that arm for a week. And stay away from live cells until somebody with more sense than me says you can."

---

The numbers went up on the gallery board ten minutes later.

The board was a long sheet of pale glass along the bottom edge of the gallery, facing down into the hall, so that the teams on the floor could read it. When it lit, the whole hall went quiet. Kiva could hear the gallery behind the thick glass go quiet too.

*TEAM SEVEN.*

*Objective 1 — Extraction: 4:41. (Standard 4:00.) PARTIAL.*

*Objective 2 — Cell: Breaker trip 3:38, uncontrolled. Quench 3:44, Renn. FAIL.*

*Objective 3 — Containment: No spawn reached casualty or line. PASS.*

*Objective 4 — Call: Role changes declared — Rowan (tune), Renn (quench). Undeclared role change — Rowan (cell to floor, 3:31). One violation.*

*Run result: FAILED.*

Kiva read it twice.

She had known. She had known since the needle crossed the red. But it was different, seeing it on the board in letters a hand high with three thousand people reading it at the same time. *FAILED.* And under it, her own name, twice. Once for the role she had declared and broken. Once for the one she had not declared at all.

She had not said anything when she went over the rail. She had not said *leaving cell*. She had not said *protecting Cade*. She had not said anything at all. She had just gone.

Mara had told her. *You'll want to. When you do, say it out loud.*

She had not said it out loud.

Kellan came and stood in front of them. He had ash down one side of his face and a long scrape on his right knee where it had hit the edge of the mezzanine. He looked at the board. Then he looked down at Kiva.

"You said no doors," he said. His voice was quiet. Not angry. Something more careful than angry. "In the corridor. To my face. And it went on the card."

"I know."

"And then you opened one anyway."

"I declared it."

"You declared it. And it worked, for about thirty seconds. And then it turned the cell into a lamp, and every husk in the building came for it, and the run failed." He did not raise his voice. "If I'd known you were going to do that, I'd have held the stair differently. I'd have been up top. I'd have been next to you, instead of at the bottom with my back to it."

Kiva did not say anything. There was nothing to say. He was right.

"And then," said Kellan, "you went over the rail without a word. And held a husk off Cade with two doors open and your lines burning. And called the quench to me from the floor. And you were right about that too." He stopped. He looked at the board again, at his own name next to the word *Quench*. "I don't know what to do with that."

"Neither do I," said Kiva.

He looked at her for a long moment. Then he nodded, very slightly, as if they had agreed on a number.

"Fill in the card," he said. "Properly. All the columns."

He walked away toward the Gate.

---

They filled in the card sitting on the concrete, all four of them, while the clerks raked the ash off the floor around them.

Anwen held it on her knee and wrote. She did not ask anyone what to put. She wrote what had happened, in order, in her small neat hand, and then she held out the pencil to each of them in turn for the last column.

Perrin took it first. His hands were still shaking. He wrote *Why: shutter weight on hands when winch died. Held with still door. Gave it back slowly. Rowan told me how.* Then he gave the pencil back and lay down flat on the floor again and shut his eyes.

Kellan was gone. Anwen wrote his for him, from what he had said. *Why: cell still calling after breaker. Quench is mechanical. Rowan's numbers right all run.*

Then she held the pencil out to Kiva.

Kiva took it. Her right hand was steady enough. She looked at the column.

She thought about the cell under her hand. The hurry in it, and the joy of pressing it out. The fear, when the husk came up the stair. The three doors going in. The ringing that would not stop after she let go.

She wrote: *Why: a cell keeps what you leave in it. I left all three doors in it. Then I took my hand off and it kept them.*

She looked at it. It was true. It was not all of it.

Under it, smaller, she wrote: *Went over the rail without declaring. Because Perrin was in the way. Should have said it.*

She gave the pencil back to Anwen.

Anwen read it. Then she folded the card and stood up to take it to the booth. She stopped beside Kiva and looked down at her, at the ash and the salve and the raw forearm and the arm held close against her side.

"You protected him," said Anwen.

"I failed the run."

"You did both." Anwen held the card against her chest. "I didn't come out. When you called for us to get out. You know that? I heard you, and I didn't come out. I kept working on the legs."

"You were right not to."

"I know I was right. That's not what I'm saying." Anwen's face did something complicated. "I'm saying I chose the casualty. Over getting out. Over you. Because it was my objective, and it's what the field panels score, and I wanted it." She paused. "And you were holding a husk off us while I chose it. I want that said."

"It's said," said Kiva.

Anwen nodded. Then she went up the stairs to the booth with the card.

Kiva sat with her back to the pump room wall and Perrin lying beside her and looked up at the gallery.

Ysra Crest was standing at the glass in the assessors' booth, looking down. Kiva could not see her face clearly from here. She could see her right hand. It was resting on the brass lever with the red grip. The master stop. It had been resting there, Kiva realized, for a long time. Maybe since the needle crossed eight.

She had not pulled it.

Ysra lifted the hand from the lever and leaned down to the speaking tube on the booth wall. Her voice came out of the horn over the hall, dry and level.

"Miss Rowan. My office. Tomorrow at nine. Bring your book."

Further along the gallery, in the section with the better chairs, a woman in a slate-blue Graywater coat had leaned forward and put her hand flat against the glass. She had a white streak through her dark hair. She was not looking at the board.

She was looking at Kiva.

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-07.md

# Chapter 7 — Kiva: Fever

Orla took her home on the tram.

She did not ask. She came down from the gallery when the hall was being cleared and stood at the Gate with her stick and waited, and when Kiva limped out, grey with ash and white with salve, Orla fell in beside her and said nothing at all. They walked to the stop. They waited. They got on.

Kiva held the rail this time. She did not think she could stand without it.

The tram went over the river in the late light. Below, the water was brown and fast. Above, the evening lift craft moved along the flight lanes with their rings lit. Kiva watched them without seeing them. Her left arm hung at her side in a sling the medic had tied. Under it, the heat-lines throbbed slowly from her breastbone to her elbow, the way a burn throbs a few hours after, deep and steady. The first two fingers of her left hand still felt like they belonged to somebody else.

"You're going to ask me what I thought," said Orla at last.

"No."

"You're going to want to."

Kiva looked out the window. "What did you think?"

Orla was quiet for a while. The tram rocked on the rails.

"I thought you tuned a live cell with one door on it," she said. "In front of three thousand people. I've watched you try for four years to keep the hand door off a cell for ten seconds. You held it there for thirty, and you pressed a planted fault out of a municipal tune by feel, and the needle stopped." She shifted her bad leg. "I thought that was the best single-door work I've ever seen you do."

"And then."

"And then you were frightened, and the Ember came in behind it, and you'd taught the cell to call every husk in the building." Orla did not soften it. "And you'd told your team you wouldn't touch it. And you went over the rail without a word. And you did the thing in the landing that I said nobody could do, and you did it wrong, and it cost you that." She nodded at the sling. "And you held. And you called the quench. And the boy pulled it because he believed your numbers."

Kiva closed her eyes.

"That's what I thought," said Orla. "All of it. At once. I'm not going to pick one."

---

Mara was standing in the shop doorway.

She had closed early. The sign said *Closed* and the bench lamp was off. She was standing on the step in her apron with her arms folded, and she had been standing there, Kiva thought, for some time.

She looked at Kiva coming down Coper Street. At the ash in her hair. At the sling. At the way she was walking.

She did not run. She did not say anything. She unfolded her arms and came down the step and met them on the pavement, and looked at Kiva's face for a long moment, and then at Orla's.

"Is it broken?" she said.

"No," said Orla. "Knee's strained again. Forearm's scraped. Backwash down the heat-lines, past the shoulder. Two fingers numb. The Wren House medic thinks they'll come back."

"*Thinks.*"

"Thinks."

Mara looked at Kiva again. Then she put her hand under Kiva's good elbow, gently, the way she would lift a meter with a cracked face.

"Did you say it out loud?" she said.

Kiva had known she would ask. She had been dreading it the whole way across the river.

"Once," she said. "I said it when I changed to tuning. I didn't say it the second time. When I went over the rail." She swallowed. "I didn't say anything. I just went."

Mara's face did not change.

"Why?"

"Because the husk was going for Perrin and he couldn't move, and there wasn't time."

"Was there time to say two words?"

Kiva thought about it. Honestly. The rail under her hand. The husk on its line across the floor. The bar running down her arm into struts.

"Yes," she said. "There was time. I didn't think of it."

Mara nodded slowly. She did not say anything else about it. She turned to Orla.

"Thank you for bringing her."

"I'll go," said Orla.

"You'll come in," said Mara. "And sit on my stool. And have a cup of tea, and tell me what happened. All of it. In order." She looked at Kiva. "And you'll go up and get in the bath. I'll be up to do your hair. You've got half the Kennel in it."

---

Anwen Pryce did not go home after the Cellhouse. She went to the Graywater table.

It was under its slate-blue awning at the foot of the Cellhouse steps, where it had been all week. The afternoon runs were finished. The crowd was thinning. Most of the recruiters were packing their boards. But the Graywater field officer was still sitting at the table with a ledger, because Anwen had written to him on Wednesday asking for ten minutes after her run, and he was the kind of man who answered letters.

His name was Doran Mace. He was about fifty, bald and brown and soft-spoken, a hand-door field medic who had run Graywater's ward line for twelve years before he took the placement desk. Anwen had read everything she could find about him. She had a list.

"Miss Pryce," he said, and pointed at the chair. "Sit."

She sat. She held her list in her lap and did not look at it.

"You stayed in the pump room," he said. "When the cell was going. When your teammate on the mezzanine called for you to get out."

"Yes."

"Why?"

She had thought about how to answer this for an hour. She had written three versions on the back of her list. She did not use any of them.

"Because the legs were still pinned," she said. "And the housing was a hand off them, and Perrin had it. If I'd come out, he'd have had to drop it to come out too. The casualty would have been back under it. And we'd have had to start again with a dead winch." She paused. "And because the casualty was my objective. It was on the card. It's what you score."

Doran Mace wrote something in his ledger. "Is that the order? The legs first? Or the score first?"

Anwen looked at him.

"The score first," she said. "Then the legs. I'd like to say it the other way round. I can't, honestly."

He looked up. For a moment she thought she had ruined it. Then he smiled, very slightly.

"Thank you," he said. "Most people say the other way round. It's very hard to train someone who's lying about why they do things." He wrote again. "You held your Sustain read on the casualty all run. You didn't drop it when the breaker went. You didn't drop it when the shutter came down. You knew exactly where the legs were under that housing the whole time. That's what I'll score you on." He closed the ledger. "And your teammate on the floor?"

Anwen was quiet for a moment.

"She chose too," she said. "She chose Perrin. Over the cell. Over the card. Over the run." She looked down at her list. "I'm not going to pretend I'd have done the same. I wouldn't. I'd have stayed on the cell and quenched and let the shutter come down and trusted Perrin to hold it. That was the right call by the book."

"Would he have held it?"

"He did hold it. When the breaker went."

"With a husk on his back?"

Anwen did not answer that.

Doran Mace nodded as if she had. "Graywater will post placements on the twentieth," he said. "Four, this year. I'll be honest with you, since you were honest with me. You're on my list. You're not at the top of it. Today moved you up."

"Thank you."

"Don't thank me. You did it." He began to pack his ledger into a satchel. Then he paused. "One more thing. You know the girl. Rowan. Well?"

"Since we were nine."

"Is she always like that?"

Anwen thought about it. About Kiva at nine, on the shop step, taking apart a broken kitchen meter with a butter knife to see where the spring went. Kiva at thirteen, on the ground in the Old Yard. Kiva at fourteen, white-faced at the laundry door, saying *Mrs. Pryce, please bring the burn salve, Mara says walk don't run.*

"She's always trying to fix the thing in front of her," said Anwen. "Even when she shouldn't. Even when it isn't hers."

Doran Mace looked at her for a moment longer. Then he nodded and stood up.

As he did, a woman came down the Cellhouse steps behind him. She was tall, in a long slate-blue coat, with a white streak through her black hair from the left temple. She walked straight to the table without looking at Anwen.

"Doran," she said. "The booth keeps the run record. Can you get me Team Seven? From three-thirty to the end."

"Tonight?"

"Tonight." She glanced at Anwen. Her eyes were very pale grey and very steady, and they rested on Anwen's face for exactly as long as it took to decide who she was. Then they went away again. "Not the placement scoring. The floor."

"I'll ask," said Doran Mace.

The woman nodded and walked away across the plaza.

Anwen sat at the empty table and looked after her. She knew who it was. Everybody in the Compact knew who it was. She had a poster of her on the back of her bedroom door when she was eleven.

She took out her list, and on the back of it, under the three answers she had not used, she wrote: *Rhea Sorn asked for our run. Not scoring. The floor.*

Then she sat there for a while longer with the pencil in her hand, feeling something she did not like.

It was not quite envy. She had done well today. She had done exactly what she meant to do, and the field officer had said so, and she was on his list. That was hers. Nobody had given it to her.

But nobody was going to ask for the run record to watch Anwen Pryce kneeling in the dark with her hands on a sandbag.

She wrote one more line. *I want it anyway. On my own.*

Then she put the list away and went home.

---

Perrin came up the back stairs at eight o'clock with a covered dish in each bandaged hand.

He could not knock. He kicked the door instead, gently, until Mara opened it.

"My mother made dumplings," he said. "For the hero. And also for Kiva."

Mara looked at his hands. They were wrapped in white cloth from the knuckles to the wrists, and the fingers that stuck out of the cloth were purple and swollen. She took both dishes from him without a word and stood aside.

Kiva was on the couch in the front room with her hair still wet. The bath had got most of the ash out. Mara had got the rest, sitting on the edge of the tub with a jug and a comb, working through it strand by strand without saying anything, the way she worked through a clogged meter. Kiva had sat with her eyes closed and let her. Neither of them had mentioned that the last time Mara had washed Kiva's hair, Kiva had been seven.

Perrin sat down on the floor in front of the couch, because his legs would not do anything else, and leaned his back against it.

"I can't use a fork," he said. "Somebody's going to have to feed me. I've decided it's you."

"My arm's in a sling."

"You've got another arm." He tipped his head back against the cushion and looked at the ceiling. "They posted the individual notes. On the gate. After you left."

"And?"

"*Cade, P. Still. Load hold under failure: exceptional. Sustained approx. 400 kg on hands, 9 sec, after winch loss. Controlled release.*" He said it in a flat, careful voice, as if he had read it many times. He probably had. "Exceptional. They wrote exceptional. The bursary board meets Monday. My Hall master came and found me and shook my hand. Very carefully. Because of the hands."

"Perrin, that's—"

"I know what it is." He was still looking at the ceiling. "Everyone saw. That's what I keep thinking. Three thousand people. My father was in the gallery. He's never come to anything. He came to this. He saw me hold it." He was quiet for a moment. "Nobody's ever seen me do anything, Kiva."

Kiva did not say anything. She put her good hand on his shoulder.

"And I wouldn't have held it," said Perrin, "if you hadn't been standing in front of me. With that thing on you. I'd have been holding the shutter with a husk chewing on my ear. I wouldn't have lasted nine seconds. I wouldn't have lasted two." He turned his head and looked at her. "So thank you. Properly. I mean it."

"You're welcome."

"And I'm still jealous." He said it in the same voice. "I want that said too. I'm still jealous. Of all of it. And I'm grateful. Both. At once. It's very uncomfortable and I'd like it to stop."

Kiva laughed. It hurt her chest. She did it anyway.

"And one more thing," said Perrin. "Next time you go over a rail to rescue me, say so first. I'd like to know I'm being rescued. I'd like to enjoy it."

"I'll try."

"*Try.*" He snorted. "Now feed me a dumpling. The big one. I held four hundred kilos. I've earned the big one."

---

The fever came after midnight.

It started in the heat-lines. Kiva woke in the dark in her narrow bed with her chest on fire, and for a moment she did not know where she was. The lines were burning. Not the way they burned when the doors fought. Deeper. Hotter. Like the whole path of them, from her breastbone to her elbow, had been filled with hot sand.

She was sweating. The sheet was wet under her. Her head ached. Her mouth was dry.

*Backwash fever,* she thought. The medic had said it might happen. *If the lines run hot tonight, cold cloths, water, and the Wren House in the morning.* She thought she should get up and get water. She thought she should call Mara.

She did not get up. She lay and burned.

The disk lay on her chest in the middle of it all, cold as a coin in snow.

And the room went away.

---

She was high up. Very high. And they were coming.

From every road. From every direction. She could not see their faces. Only their light, and the lights were all different, and they were walking toward her and toward each other, and they were very old and very bright and she could not look at any of them for long.

The lights touched.

She looked up. There was no sky.

And what was above came down. Full. Pressing. Into the air and the ground and her skin and her chest, into the three hot lines, into the cold disk, into everything.

Below her, every lamp answered.

---

She woke up standing.

She was in the narrow hallway outside her bedroom door. She did not remember getting there. Her bare feet were on the cold boards. Her left arm was out of the sling, stretched out in front of her, and her left hand was flat against the wall.

Against the flat's cell panel.

It was a small steel box set into the hallway wall, with a meter on its face and a red handle on its side. Every flat in the building had one. It held the flat's share of the building's reserve cell, so that if the core in the cellar ever shut down, the lights and the cold-cupboard would run for a few days more. Kiva had walked past it ten thousand times. She had never touched it. Mara's rule.

Her palm was flat on the steel. Her numb fingers were spread across the meter's glass.

Under her hand, the cell was screaming.

The needle on the meter was pinned past red. The panel was hot. It was ringing. She could feel it through her palm, the same way she had felt the drum in the Cellhouse, and it was the same thing. Three shapes. Fighting. Twisting round inside the cell. And something else, something bigger, pressing down on top of all of them like a hand.

Her heat-lines were blazing. All three. She had not opened any door. She had been asleep. They had opened on their own.

She tore her hand off the panel.

The ringing did not stop.

From somewhere below, deep in the building, there was a heavy *clunk* that she felt through the soles of her feet. Then every light in the flat went out. The cold-cupboard in the kitchen stopped humming. Through the floor, she heard the big night machines in the Pryce laundry downstairs grind to a halt, one after another.

The building's core had tripped.

In the dark, the panel under her hand was still ringing.

"*Kiva.*"

Mara was in the hallway behind her. Kiva did not know when she had got there. She came past Kiva in her nightshirt with one hand out, not to Kiva, to the panel. She broke the wire seal on the red handle with her thumb, the way she had done ten thousand times on other people's cells, and pulled it.

There was a clank and a long hiss. The ringing stopped.

It was very dark. It was very quiet. Kiva could hear herself breathing. She could hear Mara breathing. Through the floor, she could hear voices on the stairs, doors opening, somebody calling up *What's happened? Is it the core?*

Mara put her hand on Kiva's face.

"You're burning up," she said.

"I didn't open anything." Kiva's voice came out thin and strange. "I was asleep. I didn't—I woke up and my hand was on it."

"I know."

"I didn't *open* anything."

"I know. I know you didn't." Mara's hand moved to the back of Kiva's neck and held it. "Sit down. Sit down, here, on the floor. Your nose is bleeding."

Kiva sat. She had not known her nose was bleeding. She put her good hand to it and it came away wet and dark. Her heat-lines were still burning, but less. Her left hand, the one that had been on the panel, was throbbing. The first two fingers buzzed now, like a limb waking up, prickling and hot.

Mara went to the kitchen and came back with a candle and a wet cloth. She knelt in the hallway and held the cloth to Kiva's nose and the candle up to her face and looked at her.

"What did you see?" she said.

It was the question she had stopped asking four years ago. She asked it now very quietly, as if she already knew the answer would be hard to carry.

And Kiva told her.

Not pieces. Not *people* and *the sky*. All of it, as much as she could hold onto before it went. She told her about the height, and the people coming from every road with their different lights. About the lights touching. About looking up and finding no sky at all. About what came down, and how it was not heavy but full, and how it was enormous and paying attention and could not have been held back by anybody. About the city answering. Every lamp.

"Every lamp," said Mara.

"Every lamp. And every cell. And the cores. And the doors. Everybody's doors." Kiva held the cloth against her face. "Some went out. Some went white. It's the same every time. It's been the same since the Old Yard."

"Every time?"

"It doesn't come often. A few times. When I've been sick." She looked down. "I never told you because I didn't know what it was. I still don't know. I thought if I said it out loud, it would sound—"

"Like a fever."

"Like a fever. Or like something worse."

Mara sat back on her heels in the hallway. The candle shook a little in her hand. She was not looking at Kiva. She was looking at the panel on the wall with its broken seal, and her face had gone very still, the way it went at the bench when a meter did something she had not expected and she was working out why.

"The lamps went out," she said. "In the Old Yard. When you went down."

"Somebody said it was the rig."

"It wasn't the rig." Mara stood up slowly. "Stay there."

She went down the hall to the front room and opened the window. Kiva heard her lean out. Below, Coper Street was full of voices. The laundry was dark. So was the bakery. So, Kiva realized, listening, was every building on their side of the street.

No. Not every building.

"It's us," said Mara from the window. "Our building, and the laundry, and the bakery. We share the core. Across the street's still lit. The street lamps are lit." She was quiet. Then she pulled the window shut. "It's just us."

---

The building's core reset itself at a quarter to three. The lights came back on one flat at a time. The laundry machines started again downstairs. The neighbors went back to bed.

The flat's own cell stayed dead, with its quench box full of wet salt and ruined ash. Mara wrapped the panel's broken seal in a twist of paper and put it in her apron pocket.

Then she sat on the edge of Kiva's bed with a bowl of cold water and a cloth, and she did not sleep.

Kiva drifted. The fever came and went. Every time she opened her eyes, Mara was there, wringing out the cloth, laying it on her forehead, on the hot welts along her arm. Once she woke and Mara was holding her left hand, pressing the tips of the first two fingers one at a time with her thumb.

"Feel that?"

"A bit. It's coming back."

"Good." Mara did not let go.

At some point near dawn, the fever broke. Kiva knew it because she was suddenly cold, and wet through, and very tired, and her head was clear. The heat-lines still ached. But it was an ordinary ache now. A burn, healing.

She looked at Mara. Mara looked grey. She looked ten years older than she had at supper.

"The core reports trips," said Kiva. "Doesn't it. On the municipal wire."

Mara did not answer at once.

"Every licensed core in the city reports a trip," she said at last. "Automatically. To the Works. With the flat that caused it, if the core can tell. This one could." She wrung out the cloth. "It'll have gone at a quarter to three. It'll be on a clerk's desk by eight."

"An incident filing."

"An incident filing. Household."

Kiva lay still. She thought about the letter Ysra Crest had read from in the little office. *Your aunt's shop filed an incident report three years ago.* She thought about the slab discharging in the lane last spring. That had been at the school. Not the household.

"How many is that?" she said. "For you. For your license."

Mara's hands went still on the cloth.

"Kiva."

"How many?"

Mara put the cloth in the bowl. She sat for a moment with her hands in her lap, the scarred one over the other. Then she got up and went out of the room. Kiva heard her go down the stairs into the dark shop. She heard a drawer open. Then Mara came back up, and sat on the edge of the bed again, and held out a letter.

It was on heavy paper with a red stripe across the top. Kiva read it in the grey dawn light from the window.

*Grade Two Tuning License, M. Rowan. Household condition noted, 2677. One further incident filing within the license household will trigger automatic review.*

She read it twice. Then she looked up.

"This came in the winter," she said.

"It came in the winter."

"You didn't tell me."

"No."

"You signed the form for the Cellhouse. With this in the drawer."

"The Cellhouse isn't the household. A trial incident goes on the Compact's book, not mine." Mara's voice was very steady and very tired. "I checked. Before I signed."

"But this is the household."

"This is the household."

Kiva held the letter. Her hand was shaking. She did not know if it was the fever or something else.

"What happens? In review?"

"They suspend the license while they look at my books. A season, usually. Sometimes two." Mara looked at the window. "No license, no municipal contract. The van stops coming. The cell books stop coming. The reconciling stops." She shrugged, a small, tired movement. "The shop's still mine. I can still fix kitchen meters for the neighbors. It just won't pay the rent."

"Because of me."

"Because of a cell." Mara turned and looked at her. "I want you to hear me. Because of a cell, and a fever, and something we don't understand. Not because you did anything wrong tonight. You were asleep."

"You should have told me," said Kiva. "In the winter. When it came."

Mara was quiet for a long time.

"Yes," she said. "I should have. I didn't want you carrying it. You were carrying enough." She looked down at her hands. "That was wrong of me. It was the same thing I told you not to do. Holding something that's going to come out anyway, and not saying it out loud." She let out a breath. "I'm saying it now. I'm sorry."

Kiva folded the letter and held it.

Outside, the sky was getting light over the Eastbank roofs. Somewhere down the street, a lift-truck started up. The bakery's ovens would be on by now. The core in the cellar was humming again, the way it always did, the sound Kiva had stopped hearing years ago and could hear now very clearly.

"I've got to go to the Compact at nine," she said. "Ysra Crest. With my book."

"I know."

"What do I tell her?"

Mara stood up. She took the bowl and the cloth and stood by the door with them for a moment, her back to Kiva.

"The truth," she said. "All of it. Same as you'd write it down." She turned. "And I'm going to the district records office at eight to file this before the Works files it for me. I'd rather they read it in my hand than a clerk's."

"Will that help?"

"Probably not." Mara almost smiled. "But it's mine to write."

She went out. Kiva lay back on the damp pillow.

The disk lay on her chest. She put her good hand over it.

It was cold.

It had been cold all night. Cold through the fever. Cold through the dream. Cold when the panel screamed and the core tripped and her nose bled into Mara's cloth. Four years, and it had never once been anything else.

She did not know if it had done this. She did not know if it had carried the dream to her, or if the dream had come on its own and the disk had only lain there in the middle of it, the way a stone lies in a river. She did not know whether the bright people had come to hold the sky shut or tear it open. She did not know why every lamp answered.

She knew her hand had been on the panel. She knew the panel had held what was in her. She knew it had cost Mara her license, or would.

She took the field book off the chair beside her bed, and the pencil on its string, and turned to a clean page.

*Exercise:* None. Asleep.

*What went wrong:* Fever from the backwash. Dream again. Woke standing in the hall with my hand on the flat cell. Lines open by themselves. Cell held it. Core tripped. Whole building dark.

*Who was hurt:* Me (nose). The laundry lost a night's wash. Mara's license.

*What it cost:* The flat cell (quenched). A household incident filing.

*How long to come back:* Don't know.

*Second go:* There isn't one.

She stopped at the last column for a long time.

*Why:* Don't know. Writing it down so somebody can.

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-08.md

# Chapter 8 — Kiva: The Lowest Defensible Place

Ysra Crest's office was at the top of the Measure Hall, at the end of a long corridor lined with framed rosters going back two hundred years. Kiva walked past them at five to nine with her book in her good hand and her arm in its sling, and did not look at any of them.

Her fingers were coming back. On the tram she had pressed the tips of the first two, one at a time, with her thumb, the way Mara had all night. She could feel it now. Not the way she felt the third finger, sharp and close. Duller. As if the first two were wearing thin gloves. But she could feel it.

She had written that in the book too.

The door was open. Ysra was sitting behind her desk with a cup of tea and a stack of paper. She was not alone. Orla Dane sat in a chair by the window with her bad leg stretched out and her stick across her lap. She looked as if she had not slept either.

"Sit, Miss Rowan," said Ysra. "Give me the book."

Kiva gave it to her. She sat.

Ysra read it.

She did not skim. She started on the first page, the Friday night in the Old Yard, and read every row, every column. She read the Terrace Ring. She read *What went wrong: Perrin. Why: Perrin.* and did not smile. She read the trial. She read the card copy Kiva had written out underneath it. She read the last page, the one from three o'clock that morning, and stopped there for a long time.

Then she closed the book and set it on the desk and folded her hands on top of it.

"Tell me about this morning," she said.

Kiva told her. The fever. The dream, though she did not say what was in it, only that it came. Waking in the hallway with her hand on the panel. The core tripping. Mara's quench. The letter with the red stripe.

"The household filing will be on a desk at the Works by now," she said. "My aunt's gone to file it herself. Her license goes to review."

Ysra was quiet.

"That isn't mine," she said at last. "That's the Works. I can't touch it." She looked at Kiva. "But I'm glad you told me. I'd have heard by Monday, and I'd have wondered why you didn't."

"I didn't want you to wonder."

"No." Ysra picked up her tea and drank some. "Now. The run. Why did you tune?"

"Because the cell was going to trip with Perrin and Anwen under the shutter. I could feel the fault. I knew how to fix it. I thought I could hold one door on it."

"You'd said you wouldn't."

"I'd said I wouldn't. Then I changed it. Out loud."

"And when the husk came up the stair?"

"I was frightened," said Kiva. "And the Ember came in behind the hand door before I could stop it. And then the still door. And all three went into the cell."

"Did you know that would happen?"

Kiva thought about it.

"I knew it could," she said. "I told Kellan it could. In the corridor. I thought I could keep them out." She looked at the book under Ysra's hands. "I could, until I was frightened. I don't know how to keep them out when I'm frightened. I haven't learned that yet."

Ysra nodded slowly.

"And the rail. Why didn't you declare?"

"I didn't think of it," said Kiva. "There was time. I've thought about it all night. There was time to say two words. I didn't say them."

"Would you say them now?"

"Yes."

"How do you know?"

"I don't," said Kiva. "I know I'll try. I know I wrote it down. That's all I know."

---

Ysra put her tea down. She leaned back in her chair and looked out of the window for a moment, at the roofs of the Compact Quarter and the flight lanes over them, and then back at Kiva.

"I had my hand on the master stop," she said. "From the moment the needle crossed eight. Did you see?"

"After. I saw your hand on it after."

"I've run the Cellhouse for nineteen years. I've pulled that lever eleven times. Every time, it was because I thought a team had lost control of something they couldn't get back." She tapped the book. "I didn't pull it on Friday. Do you want to know why?"

Kiva nodded.

"Because I wanted to see whether your team could stop what you'd started." Ysra's voice was dry and level. "If I'd pulled it, the cell would have quenched and the run would have ended, and I'd have learned exactly what I already knew. That you can't hold three doors. That they fight. That a cell keeps what you leave in it. I've known all that for four years. I've had it on a board in front of me every spring." She paused. "What I didn't know was what happened next. Whether you'd see it. Whether you'd call it. Whether anyone would listen."

"Kellan listened."

"Renn listened. Because, he told the booth afterward, your numbers had been right all run." Ysra almost smiled. "That's the highest praise I've ever heard that young man give anyone."

Kiva did not know what to say.

"The run failed," said Ysra. "I want that clear. It failed on the board and it failed in fact. You broke your own declared role. You put the cell into a state no one in the Compact has seen before. You took an undeclared violation. You hurt yourself, and you could have hurt Cade and Pryce badly. If you'd been on a real substation, people would have lost power at the worst possible moment. Those things are true."

"I know."

"And this is also true." Ysra laid her palm flat on the book. "For four years, I've had a board that can't read you. Columns that won't hold still. A Rank that reads Ember, then Flame, then nothing. Every spring I've looked at it and deferred, because I had no way of knowing what you could and couldn't do. Not safely. Not in a way I could defend to three thousand people if something went wrong."

She lifted her hand.

"Now I have this. A book that can read you. Written in your own hand. Every limit. Every failure. Every time you said it out loud and every time you didn't." Ysra looked at her steadily. "That's not a classification. The board still can't name you. I still have no column for this. But it's something I can rule on."

Kiva sat very still.

"I'm prepared to post you," said Ysra. "Provisional. Third Division, Crooked Line School, the third team. It's the lowest place on the Circuit. It's the lowest place I can defend in public. It carries tuition. No stipend. No live-cell events until your sponsor certifies you in writing. Before every sanctioned event, you declare to the referee which single door you'll use. Any change, you declare before you make it. Your book goes to your sponsor every week. You're reviewed after your first sanctioned meet. If there's one incident in a sanctioned event, you're off the roster for the season."

"My sponsor," said Kiva.

"Your sponsor," said Ysra. She turned her head and looked at Orla. "A provisional placement for an unclassified candidate whose household license is under review requires an accountable sponsor. Someone whose name goes on the roster beside the candidate's. Someone who carries it if it goes wrong." She paused. "That's the condition. I can't waive it. I wouldn't if I could."

---

Orla had not said anything. She was sitting by the window with her hands on her stick, and she was looking at Kiva, not at Ysra.

"Four years," said Ysra quietly.

"Twenty-one," said Orla.

Ysra did not answer that. She only waited.

Orla turned to Kiva.

"I told your aunt I'd put my name next to a student's once," she said. "You were on the stairs. You heard."

"I heard."

"His name was Jessamy Hart. Twin-kindled. Ember and hand. I put him in an ashcraft trial because I thought I understood him and I wanted the board to see what I saw." Orla said it plainly, without stopping, the way you pull a splinter. "His doors fought on a live cell. He lost a hand. Two other students were burned pulling him out. The board sealed his file. He left Meridian at nineteen and I never wrote to him." She looked down at her stick. "That's why. Not a good reason. The true one."

Kiva did not know what to say. So she said the thing that was in front of her.

"I'm not him."

"No," said Orla. "You're not. He never wrote anything down. He never said *one* when the line warmed. He never stood in a yard at ten o'clock at night doing the same drill eleven times because it was eleven seconds and not ten. And he never told the board, to its face, exactly what he couldn't do." She looked up. "I didn't know how to teach him that. I didn't know it needed teaching. I learned it from losing him."

She reached into the canvas bag at her feet and took out a sheet of paper folded in half. It was soft at the folds, as if it had been carried a long time. She unfolded it on her knee. The first page was filled in, in a hand Kiva knew. The second was blank.

"I filled this in four years ago," said Orla. "The week after the Old Yard. I've carried it every day since. I never did the second page."

She held out her hand to Ysra for a pen. Ysra gave her one without a word.

"You don't have to," said Kiva.

"I know," said Orla. "That's why it counts."

She filled in the second page. It took her a long time. Her hand was not entirely steady, and she stopped twice to read back what she had written. When she came to the line at the bottom, she signed it, *O. Dane*, and under it, *Senior Lecturer, Crooked Line School. Former Field Officer, Home Line.* Then she handed it across the desk.

Ysra read it. She signed it below Orla's name. She took a stamp from a drawer, pressed it into a pad of red ink, and stamped the page, very firmly, once.

"If she has an incident in a sanctioned event," said Ysra, "your sponsoring standing goes. And the board will look at your teaching contract. You know that."

"I know that."

"Then it's done." Ysra slid the page into a folder. Then she looked at Kiva, and for the first time, her long plain face did something that might have been warm. "Provisional, Third Division. It'll post on Monday with the others. Congratulations, Miss Rowan. You've earned the lowest defensible place in the Collegiate Circuit. I hope you hate it."

"Why?"

"Because people who hate the bottom work hardest to leave it," said Ysra. She picked up her tea. "One more thing. Graywater asked the booth for your run record last night. The floor, not the scoring. I gave it to them. It's public." She drank. "Don't let it go to your head. They ask for a great many records."

---

Mara was at the district records office on Hanley Street, at a long table in a back room, with three enormous bound books open in front of her.

Kiva found her there at half past ten. The clerk at the front desk, an elderly man with ink on his cuffs, had taken one look at the sling and the salve and pointed down the corridor without asking her name.

The back room was cool and dim and smelled of old paper. It was lined floor to ceiling with shelves of identical books, each bound in grey cloth with a year stamped on the spine. *Transient Register.* Every flicker, every trip, every unexplained surge on every municipal meter in Meridian, written down by hand. Six hundred years of it, nearly. Some of the oldest books were falling apart and tied with tape.

Mara did not look up when Kiva came in. She had her reading glasses on and a pencil behind her ear and one finger on a line in the nearest book.

"I filed it," she said. "Ours. Last night. It's in today's book." She turned it so Kiva could see. "Coper Street core. Trip, two forty-four. Upstream fault, flat three panel. Household filing, M. Rowan, by her own hand." She took off her glasses. "The clerk says the Works will send the review notice by the end of the week."

"I'm sorry," said Kiva.

"I know you are. Sit down. I want to show you something."

Kiva sat. Mara pulled the second book across the table. Its spine said *2676*.

"After I filed," said Mara, "I sat here for a while. I was thinking about the lamps in the Old Yard. About what you said in the hall. *Every lamp.*" She opened the book to a page marked with a slip of paper. "So I looked up the day you were Kindled."

She put her finger on a line.

It was written in a cramped, careful hand, in faded ink.

*T-0. 14:52. Eastbank grid, Lantern grid, Weighbridge grid. All meters, same second. Duration under one second. No trip. No load change. Cause not found. Not grid.*

Kiva read it twice.

"T-zero," said Mara. "That's the register's code for a transient with no cause. A flicker on a meter that nobody can explain. You get a few a year. A bad cell somewhere, a rift on the Sag Line, a fault on a wire. But always one meter. One street. One substation." She tapped the line. "This is three grids. Every meter. The same second. Fourteen fifty-two." She looked at Kiva. "That's when you went down in the Old Yard. I know, because I looked at the clock over the assessors' table when they put you in the frame. It said a quarter to three."

Kiva stared at the line.

"So it was me," she said. "My Kindling. Three doors opening. That's what it was."

"Maybe," said Mara. "That's one story." She pulled the third book across. It was older, much older, its grey cloth faded almost to white. The spine said *2604*. "The clerk here has been in this room for forty years. I asked him if he'd ever seen a T-0 across more than one grid. He went very quiet. Then he went into the back stacks and came out with this."

She opened it to another marked page.

*T-0. 03:10. Compact Quarter, all meters, same second. No fault. Not grid.*

And in the margin, in a different hand, small and slanting:

*Again. Same as the '46 entry in the old book. Every lamp at once.*

"The forty-six entry," said Mara, "is in a book from 2446. The clerk says it's too fragile to bring out. He's going to copy it for me by hand. He says it says the same thing. Every lamp, every district, one instant, at night. No fault found."

Kiva sat with her hand on the old book.

"Three times," said Mara quietly. "In two hundred and thirty years. Every lamp in this city has flickered at once, and nobody could say why. One of those times was the day you were Kindled." She took off her glasses and rubbed her eyes. "I don't know what that means. I don't think anyone does. It could be your Kindling. It could be the dream. It could be something else that happened at the same time, that has nothing to do with you at all. A rift. The filter. The six-hundredth year. Three stories." She put her glasses on the table. "I've got three lines in three books. I know which I trust more."

"What do you trust?"

"That it happened," said Mara. "That somebody wrote it down. That it isn't a fever."

Kiva looked at the slanting note in the margin. *Every lamp at once.* She thought about the bright people coming from every road. She thought about the sky with nothing in it, and the whole city ringing like a bell.

She did not know if a clerk in 2604 had dreamed it too. She did not know who had been dreaming in 2446. She did not know if anyone had been dreaming at all, or if the lamps had simply flickered three times in two hundred years for a reason nobody had ever found.

She took out her book and the pencil on its string, and copied all three lines onto a clean page, exactly as they were written, with the dates and the times and the note in the margin.

In the last column, she wrote: *Why: unknown.*

---

They walked home along the river because Kiva could not face the tram.

It was Saturday and the embankment was busy. Families walking. Kids on lift-boards, the small, cheap kind that only rose a hand's height off the paving and ran for ten minutes on a thumb-sized cell. A long Halden barge going downstream with its induction rings humming. On the far bank, the white arenas of the Compact Quarter caught the noon sun.

Mara walked slowly, to match Kiva's knee. She had the roster consent form in her apron pocket. Kiva had given it to her on the steps of the records office. Under eighteen, household under review, provisional placement. Guardian's countersignature required.

They walked for a long time before Mara spoke.

"I'm going to sign it," she said.

Kiva looked at her.

"I want to tell you why. All of it." Mara kept walking, looking ahead. "If you're on a Compact roster with an accountable sponsor, the Compact carries your ash liability in sanctioned work. Not the household. That's in the rules. When my license goes to review, the first thing the Works will ask is whether the household condition still stands." She paused. "If you're on a roster, I can tell them it doesn't. Not the way it did. It might save my license. It might save the shop."

"That's good."

"It is good. It's also why I don't trust myself." Mara stopped at the rail and looked down at the brown water. "I've said no to you for four years because I was afraid for you. And now I'm saying yes, and part of the reason is that it's good for me. I don't like that. I want it said out loud, so you know. So you can decide what my signature's worth."

Kiva stood beside her at the rail.

"What's the rest of the reason?" she said.

Mara was quiet.

"The book," she said at last. "I read it again this morning. Before I went to file. Every page." She turned her scarred hand over on the rail and looked at it. "You wrote *Should have said it.* About the rail. You wrote it on the team card for Ysra Crest to read. You didn't make it smaller than it was." She let out a breath. "And you told me what you saw. Last night. All of it. For the first time."

"I should have told you years ago."

"And I should have told you about the license. We're both bad at it." Mara almost smiled. "We'll get better."

She took the form out of her pocket and unfolded it on the rail, against the wind. She took the pencil from behind her ear.

"Three things," she said. "My conditions. Not the Compact's. Mine."

"All right."

"You never touch a cell in the flat. Not the panel. Not the cold-cupboard. Not a lamp-cell. Not ever, until I say."

"All right."

"When the dream comes, you tell me. That night. Even if it's three in the morning. Even if there's nothing to say but that it came."

"All right."

"And the book stays on the kitchen table. Every night. And I read it." Mara looked at her. "Not to check up on you. To know. The way I'd read a cell book."

"All right," said Kiva. "Yes."

Mara signed the form. *M. ROWAN*, in her square capitals, and the date. She folded it and handed it to Kiva.

"I'm not saying it's safe," she said. "I'm saying I can see the meter now. And the shutdown path. And the book. That's all I ever asked for." She put the pencil back behind her ear. "The rest we find out."

---

Rhea Sorn watched the run four times before she let herself look at the thing she had seen the first time.

She sat alone in the plate room at Graywater's company hall on the Brickfields road, on a hard chair in front of the projection table, with the lamps turned down. It was Saturday evening. Most of the company was out on a rotation at the Sag Line or home with their families. The hall was quiet except for the hum of the core under the floor and, somewhere above her, a lift craft settling onto the roof pad.

On the table, the Cellhouse run played out in pale light. The booth's run plates were good ones. Every move on the floor, from above, slightly grey, the way the Cellhouse always looked from the gallery.

She ran it from three thirty.

She watched the girl on the mezzanine with her hand on the drum. She watched the three husks come out of the chute and two go for the stair and one cut across the floor in a straight line for the pump room door. She watched the girl see it. Rhea could not see her face from above, but she could see her see it. Her whole body changed.

She watched her go over the rail.

The landing was bad. Rhea winced at it the first time and did not wince the second. She had seen a hundred bad landings. This one was a girl with a strained knee coming off struts onto one leg and still, somehow, getting where she was going. Two paces in front of the gap. Between the husk and the boy.

And then the stance.

Rhea knew stance. She was not still-door. She was Ember, Edge only, Torch rank, which was as far as her door went and as far as it would ever go. She had stood on rifts for twenty-three years with nothing but a blade and her feet. But she had stood beside still-door fighters on every one of them. She knew what it looked like when one planted.

The girl planted. With the struts still half on her calves. With the Ember still in the doorway. Two doors, open at once, and the husk hit her square, and she did not move.

And her left hand came up. Rhea had seen that on the first run-through and not understood it. She understood it now. It was the hand a hand-door fighter puts up to throw a ward. The girl had put it up. And no ward came. She had kept that door shut. On purpose. While a husk dragged its limb across her bare arm and the breaker cracked overhead and the shutter came down on the boy behind her.

Then she turned her head and called the quench to the Ember-door boy on the stair. Rhea could not hear it on the plate. She did not need to. She saw the boy hear it. She saw him decide. She saw him go up the rail.

Rhea had been doing this for twenty-three years. She had trained four probationary classes. She had sat on Graywater's draft board for six seasons. She knew what kills looked like on a plate and what standings looked like on a board, and she knew how little either of them told you about whether a person would be any use on a real rift at three in the morning with the water rising.

That girl was not a fighter. Not yet. Maybe not ever.

She was a responder.

She had read the cell and called the clock to the tenth. She had seen the husk's line before anyone else on the floor. She had put herself where the danger was going and not where the score was. She had kept a door shut that wanted to open, under impact, with her own lines burning. And when she could not reach the thing that would fix it, she had told someone who could, and been right.

None of that would show on any standing. Graywater's own board would not know how to count it.

Rhea ran it again, slowly, from three thirty-nine.

And there it was. The thing she had seen the first time and made herself not look at.

As the husk hit the girl, something swung out of her collar on a cord. It hung against the front of her shirt for the rest of the run, grey with ash. Small. Round. Dark.

Rhea stopped the plate. She leaned in until her face was a hand's width from the table.

Nine grooves. Fine rings, each a little narrower than the last.

She sat back.

She had seen it before. Once. She had been nineteen, a probationary with Graywater, seconded to a Home Line crew on the Ferren culverts in the spring floods. She had gone into a culvert after a child, and the water had come up behind her, and she had been sure, for a long and very cold minute, that she was going to drown in the dark with the child in her arms. Then a hand had come down through the grating above her and closed on her collar and hauled. A tall woman with cropped grey hair and a long scar on her jaw. Ilse Corran.

Afterward, on the bank, Ilse had sat beside her wrapped in a blanket while the medics worked, and something had hung out of her collar on a cord, grey with mud. Small. Round. Dark. Nine grooves.

Rhea had asked her what it was. Ilse had tucked it back inside her shirt and said, *Something I carry.*

Ilse had been dead for seven years.

Rhea sat in the dark plate room for a long time with the frozen image on the table in front of her. A girl with ash on her face and a disk on her chest, holding a husk off a boy.

She did not know what the girl was. A threat, maybe. A cell that kept the wrong things. A tool that some industry house would try to buy before the season was out. A recruit, in five years, if she lived and learned to close her doors. Or something else. Something that Ilse Corran had seen in a six-year-old and never explained to anyone.

She took a draft card from the stack on the table and wrote on it in her blunt hand.

*Rowan, K. Unclassified. Crooked Line, provisional. NOT for draft this season. Watch first sanctioned meet. Called a quench from under a husk with two doors open and was right. Kept the third shut under impact. Responder. — R.S.*

She put the card in the tray for the draft board.

Then she took a second, smaller card, and wrote on it only two words. She looked at them for a while. Then she put that card in her own coat pocket, not in the tray, and turned off the projection table, and sat for a while longer in the dark.

*Corran's disk.*

---

The rosters posted on Monday at noon.

They went up on the Measure Hall gate under glass, the way they always did, and by a quarter past there were five hundred people in front of them. Kiva did not push to the front. She stood at the back with her arm in its sling and let Perrin push for her, because Perrin was very large and very happy and nobody argued with him.

He came back with his bandaged hands in the air.

"Stonehand bursary," he said. "Second Division. Full tuition and a stipend. My father cried. On the tram. In front of people." He grinned so widely it looked painful. "Second Division, Kiva. I've never been in the second anything."

"Perrin, that's—"

"I know. I know." He bumped her good shoulder with his, gently for once. "You're in the Third. Crooked Line. Provisional. Your name's at the very bottom. In small letters. With a little star and *Sponsor: O. Dane* next to it, like a footnote." He looked at her. "It's the best thing I've ever seen on that board."

Anwen was beside them with her list. She had read the boards already. She had read every one.

"Graywater posts field placements on the twentieth," she said. "I'm on Doran Mace's list. Not at the top. Higher than I was." She folded the list. "I've got eight days to get higher. I'm going to the Wren House lanes this afternoon. I won't be at Orla's tonight."

"All right."

"I'm not saying that because I'm angry with you."

"I know."

"I'm saying it because it's mine," said Anwen. And then, after a moment, more quietly, "Come on Thursday, though. I want to see you do the drill in ten."

Kellan Renn came out of the crowd at the front of the gate. He had a fresh scab on his right knee, Kiva saw, and the red mark on his forearm from the Terrace Ring had gone yellow-green. He stopped in front of her.

"First Division," he said. "Crooked Line, first team. Kellan Renn, Fire. No asterisk."

"Congratulations."

"Thank you." He looked at her sling. "Third Division."

"Provisional."

"The lowest defensible place." He said it as if he were reading it off a meter. Then he said, "Good."

"Good?"

"Good. It's a number. It's on the board. I know where you are." He put his hands in his pockets. "I'll be comparing."

"I know."

He nodded once. He started to turn away. Then he stopped and looked back.

"Your fingers," he said. "The two on the left. The medic said they'd come back."

"They're coming back."

"All the way?"

Kiva pressed the tips of the first two fingers with her thumb, one at a time. She could feel it. Duller than the third. A little further away. As if she had felt them once through thin gloves and the gloves were still on.

"Mostly," she said.

He looked at her for a moment longer. He did not say anything. Then he went away down the Measure Hall steps into the crowd, and she watched the back of his head until she lost it.

---

That evening, Kiva went to the Old Yard.

Orla was already there, on her crate by the first shed, with the hand lamp hung on its hook and her bad leg stretched out in front of her. She did not get up.

"No drills tonight," she said. "Not with that arm. You can sit."

"I know."

"Then why are you here?"

Kiva did not answer at once. She walked down the lane between the black posts. The ground was rutted and not quite level. She knew every rut. At the far end, the last post leaned out of the line.

She stopped beside it.

She thought about the Cellhouse. The cell under her hand, and the joy, and the fear, and the three doors going in. The ringing that would not stop. She thought about Perrin's purple hands, and Kellan's foot on the rail, and Anwen not coming out. She thought about the transient register, and three lines in three old books. *Every lamp at once.* She thought about the bright people on every road, and the sky that was not there.

She did not understand any of it. She understood a little more than she had a week ago. That was all.

She put her good hand on the post.

The wood was rough and cold. The iron bands were cold. Under her shirt, the heat-lines ached from her breastbone to her elbow, a long, low, healing ache. The disk lay cold in the middle of them.

"It's still crooked," she said.

"It is," said Orla, from the far end.

"Somebody had a reason once."

"Somebody did."

Kiva took her hand off the post. She took out her book and opened it against her knee, and wrote the date at the top of a clean page, by the yellow light of Orla's lamp.

*Exercise:* Third Division, provisional. Sponsor O. Dane.

She looked at the other columns. *What went wrong. Who was hurt. What it cost.* She did not know yet. Nothing had gone wrong yet. Everything was still in front of her.

She left them blank.

Then she closed the book and walked back up the lane to where Orla was sitting, and stood in front of her with her arm in its sling and her two dull fingers and her three burning lines, and said the only thing she could think of that was true.

"When the arm's better," she said. "Again."

---

## FORMULA ALIGNMENT — EDITORIAL, NOT BLIND

# O'Connor 1.3.0 — numerical author formula

# Applying the owner's numerical craft formula

The owner explicitly selected
`/Users/drive/penname/research/ironprince-craft-formula.md` to govern this book.
Use its craft instructions more or less exactly, including the numbers. These
are active design targets for the planning model and Opus/Fable author, not merely
editorial observations. The compiler embeds sections 2–6 and 8–9 verbatim below
these instructions and records the source hash so runs can be compared.

Let the sentence-length distribution, short/long sentence shares, paragraph
rhythm and Flesch reading targets influence the actual prose. Let the POV shares,
front-loaded progression vocabulary and distributed character-development rates
influence how the book is built. Preserve section 8's secondary/use-to-taste
status; do not turn its explicitly excluded filler/repetition tics into quotas.

Write original scenes in open-range movements of six or more chapters. The formula constrains the intended
shape and cadence; the author still chooses conversations, tactics, discoveries
and how events unfold. Do not reduce this brief to “clear writing with progression.”
The owner names the cast. No source novel quotations, character substitutions or
scene-by-scene replication belong in the manuscript.

Aim at the supplied distributions across a movement and the cumulative book.
Book-third teaching proportions and book-level POV proportions need not be met
independently by each chapter. Do not stop after each sentence or chapter to run a
gate. Write the full movement, then compare target versus observed results in the
existing editorial pass and make a focused same-author repair for material drift.
Do not silently broaden tolerances or drop inconvenient targets.

Keep the source's own caveats: pause-derived paragraph/scene measures are
directional proxies, and development counts are heuristic. Their adoption as
design targets does not establish that they accurately measure the printed book.
The full source-book audit remains unfinished. Keep source values such as ~1.7x
and ~950 words unchanged; the known arithmetic/denominator questions belong in
the audit, not a silent rewrite of the owner's chosen prompt. If competing targets
cannot be reconciled, report the conflict rather than fabricating a passing score.

This prompt guides output; it does not train model weights or guarantee exact
statistical compliance. Compliance must be measured after prose exists.

Source: /Users/drive/penname/research/ironprince-craft-formula.md
Source SHA-256: 9f97e2f225c0bcf61d2e922719153fcf00e3b029f7b1552f213888d33130c0b8

The following craft sections (2–6, 8–9) are included verbatim. Sections 1 and 7's legal discussion is not drafting guidance. Use original names, world, systems, events and phrasing; the owner names the cast.

## 2. Sentence rhythm

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Mean sentence length | 14.6 words |
| Median sentence length | 11 words |
| Spread (population stdev) | ~26 words — wide, meaning short declaratives sit right next to long compound-complex sentences, not clustered near the mean |
| Share of very short sentences (≤5 words) | 27.7% |
| Share of very long sentences (≥40 words) | 3.3% |

**Read:** roughly 1 sentence in 4 is a hard, short beat (often a single clause landing a
punch after a longer setup) — this is the "short sentence for impact" technique, not
uniform mid-length prose. Target this ratio, not just the mean; a manuscript that hits mean
14.6 but has no short-sentence spikes will not read the same.

## 3. Paragraph and scene rhythm (ASR-inferred proxy)

Whisper transcripts carry no typographic paragraph marks — this section is inferred from
pause gaps between narrated speech segments, not a count of the print manuscript's actual
paragraph breaks. Treat as directional, not exact.

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Inferred paragraph length (pause ≥0.6s) | median 18 words, mean 26.8 words — short, frequent beats |
| Inferred scene/section length (pause ≥1.6s) | mean ~950 words between scene-level breaks, ~8.7 scene breaks per 10k words |

**Read:** short paragraphs are the default texture, with scenes running roughly 900–1000
words before a beat change (POV settle, location change, or time-skip). Avoid long
unbroken expository blocks; break early and often.

## 4. Progression-mechanic ("change frequency") callout rate

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Progression/stat-vocabulary density | ~58 hits per 10k words (rank, growth-analog, spec, level-up, stat-block language) |
| Placement | front-loaded — the opening third of the book carries roughly 1.7x the progression-callout density of the middle and closing thirds (900 vs. 471 vs. 514 hits, by third) |

**Read:** the system gets *established* early and hard, then recedes into the background
as character and plot take over — it does not stay evenly dense through the whole book.
Build your own progression system's vocabulary (do not reuse his axis names — see §7), but
match this front-loaded shape: teach the mechanic aggressively in the first third, then let
it surface only at genuine turning points afterward.

## 5. Character-development beat cadence

Methodology: named-character mention within ~120 characters of a reflection/interiority
marker ("realized," "decided," "for the first time," "swore," "no longer," etc.) — a
heuristic count, not a scene-by-scene read.

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Protagonist development-moment rate | 4.5 per 10k words |
| Protagonist distribution across thirds | roughly even (41 / 56 / 50 — a slight middle-third bump, not backloaded) |
| Supporting-cast development-moment rate | an order of magnitude lower per character (0.1–0.7 per 10k words each), spread across 8–10 named characters rather than concentrated in one deuteragonist |

**Read:** growth beats for the lead land steadily throughout, not saved up for a finale —
and the supporting cast gets *many* small beats each rather than one character getting a
big secondary arc. If your story gives one best-friend character 80% of the secondary
development budget, that's a different shape than this book's.

## 6. POV structure

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Protagonist POV share | ~87% of chapter-word-volume (combining his given-name and surname references) |
| Secondary-character POV/focus share | ~13%, spread across 4–5 named characters at 2.5–3.7% each — brief cutaways, not sustained alternating-POV chapters |

**Read:** this is single-POV-dominant with occasional short cutaway scenes to a handful of
other named characters, not a true multi-POV structure. If you want to signal "feels like
Iron Prince," don't build alternating-chapter dual POV — keep the lead's POV overwhelming
and let other characters get glimpses, not chapters.

## 8. Supporting craft signals (secondary, use to taste)

- **Filler-word discipline:** "that" runs hot (92.1 per 10k words) — a known, common genre
  tic, not a target to imitate; if anything, tighten below this. Other fillers ("just,"
  "almost," "felt," "seemed") sit in the 5–25-per-10k band — moderate, not aggressively
  self-edited, not sloppy either.
- **Adverb density:** ~161 -ly adverbs per 10k words — fairly high; this is a book that
  lets adverbs carry tone rather than insisting on pure show-don't-tell. Not a craft flaw
  to correct, a genre-register signal to optionally match.
- **Dialogue-tag (reporting-verb) density:** ~41 per 10k words — moderate dialogue presence,
  consistent with an action/interiority-forward book rather than a dialogue-driven one.
- **Combat-vocabulary density:** ~22 per 10k words (strike/block/dodge/blade/impact/etc.) —
  present throughout, not just in set-piece fight chapters.
- **Readability:** Flesch Reading Ease 72.3, Flesch-Kincaid grade 6.8 — accessible YA/adult
  crossover register, short-to-medium sentence construction, low jargon-density outside the
  progression-vocabulary spikes.
- **Repetition discipline:** common connective 3-grams ("out of the," "one of the," "for a
  moment") sit at 2–4 per 10k words — normal English-prose background rate, not a
  distinctive tic to replicate. Don't chase these; they're noise, not signature.

---

## 9. One-paragraph brief for the drafting agent

> Write single-POV-dominant (~85–90% lead-character screen time), short-paragraph,
> pause-heavy prose (median paragraph ~18 words, scenes breaking roughly every 900–1000
> words). Mix short punch sentences (~28% of all sentences ≤5 words) against longer
> compound-complex ones (mean 14.6 words, wide spread) rather than settling into uniform
> mid-length sentences. Establish your progression system hard and often in the first
> third of the book, then let it recede to occasional high-stakes surfacing afterward. Land
> a protagonist growth/realization beat roughly every 2,200 words, spread evenly across the
> book rather than saved for the climax, and give 8–10 supporting characters small
> development beats each rather than concentrating growth in one deuteragonist. Moderate
> dialogue-tag and adverb use; accessible ~7th-grade reading level; combat vocabulary
> present in the background throughout, not confined to fight scenes. Every name, every
> system-label, every scene: yours, invented, original.
